|                  |
|      YAKUMO      |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 10  |
 ------------------
AREA 956 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 777, CULTIVATED 61 ; COUNTY = FUTAMI ; 
POP 23,781 ( 1990 ) ( YAKUMO 18,872, KUMAISHI 4,909 ), 18,896 
( 2010 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 7.9 C ( AV 1981-2010 ), PRECIPI 1,279 MM, 
SNOW 613 CM ( AV 1982-2010 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = ABALONE, COW, & RIVER. 
  
LOCATION  

Yakumo ( ya-ku-mo ) is a town in Oshima general sub-prefecture, south 
Hokkaidou ( ``dou-nan'' ).  The area crosses the narrow part of the 
Oshima Peninsula.  It faces Uchiura Bay ( Funka-wan ) and the Sea of 
Japan.  The name ``Funka-wan'' is a translation of ``Volcano Bay'' into 
Japanese. 

( UTF code ; Yakumo = \u516B\u96F2. )

The area was newly formed by the merger of Yakumo Town and Kumaishi 
Town in 2005.  The former Yakumo Town was located in the Pacific 
Ocean side.  The former Kumaishi Town was located in the Sea of Japan
side. 

( For Volcano Bay, see Oshima general sub-prefecture. )

NAME 

The name ``Yakumo'' is Japanese.  It means ``overlapping clouds.''  
Yoshikatsu Tokugawa, who was the founder of Tokugawa Farm, named it.  
He quoted the name from an old poem.  ( Tokugawa Farm was the 
birthplace of Hokkaidou's famous souvenirs ; butter candy and carved 
wooden bear ( a bear eating a salmon ). )  #1
 
The name ``Kumaishi'' comes from ``kuma-us-i'' in Ainu language.  It 
means ``fish drying site, many, place.''  The Kanji characters for 
Kumaishi mean ``bear, stone.''

( UTF code ; Kumaishi = \u718A\u77F3. ) 

With the merger of two towns in 2005, Futami county was established.  
The name ``Futa-mi'' is Japanese, which means ``two seas.''  It 
signifies the Pacific Ocean and the Sea of Japan. 

( UTF code ; Futami = \u4E8C\u6D77. )
 
HISTORY 

In the early Edo era, the Matsumae Domain built a guardhouse ( ``ban-
sho'' ) in Kumaishi on the Sea of Japan side.  It was the northern end 
of Japanese territory.  On the Pacific Ocean side, there were trading 
places ( ``basho'' ).  They were located in Nodaoi and Yuurappu.

( UTF code ; Nodaoi = \u91CE\u7530\u8FFD.  Yuurappu = 
  \u904A\u697D\u90E8. ) 
   
In the mid Edo era, the Matsumae Domain built a guardhouse ( ``ban-
sho'' in Yamukushinai ( later called as Yamakoshinai ).  It became the 
northern end of Japanese territory on the Pacific Ocean side.

The trading place Yuurappu was renamed Yamakoshinai.  The main village 
was moved from Nodaoi to Otoshibe due to an increase in migrants 
engaged in fishing.  

( UTF code ; Otoshibe = \u843D\u90E8.  Yamakoshinai = 
  \u5C71\u8D8A\u5185. ) 

Currently, the Hakodate Main Line within Yakumo Town includes stations 
of Otoshibe, Nodaoi, Yamakoshi ( the former Yamakoshinai ), and Yakumo 
( from south to noth ).  In an Ainu-kotan ( Ainu village ) of Otoshibe, 
Ainu leader Ekashiba ( Takojirou Benkai ) was born.  #2

( UTF code ; Yamakoshi = \u5C71\u8D8A. ) 

The following is the history of Yakumo Town after the Meiji period ;

OLD YAKUMO TOWN ; In 1872 ( Meiji 5 ), Yamakoshinai branch of the 
Hokkaidou Development Commission ( Kaitaku-shi ) was established.  In 
1878 ( Meiji 11 ), Tokugawa Farm opened.  In 1879 ( Meiji 12 ), 
administrative offices ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) were established in 
Yamakoshinai and Otoshibe villages.
 
In 1890 ( Meiji 23 ), the administrative office of Yamakoshinai village 
was moved to Yakumo village.  In 1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Yakumo Village 
( 2nd-class municipality ) was established.  In 1903 ( Meiji 22 ), 
Yamakoshinai Station and Yakumo Station on the Hokkaidou Railway ( now 
Hakodate Main Line ) opened.  

In 1907 ( Meiji 40 ), Yakumo Village ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1915 ( Taishou 4 ), Otoshibe Village ( 2nd-class 
municipality ) was established.  In 1919 ( Taishou 8 ), Yakumo Town was 
established.  In 1957 ( Shouwa 32 ), Yakumo Town merged Otoshibe 
Village.

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Matsumae 
  Town. )

OLD KUMAISHI TOWN ; In 1973 ( Meiji 6 ), administrative offices 
( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) were established in three villages ; Ainumanai, 
Kumaishi, and Tomarikawa.  These were a continuation of village chief
( ``kochou'') system from the Edo era.

( UTF code ; Ainumanai = \u76F8\u6CBC\u5185.  Tomarikawa = 
  \u6CCA\u5DDD. )

In 1902 ( Meiji 35 ), three villages were merged and Kumaishi 
Village ( 2nd-class municipalities ) was established.  In 1962 ( Shouwa 
37 ), Kumaishi Town was established.  Herring fishing flourished in 
Kumaishi from the Edo era to the Meiji period.  The peak of Kumaishi's 
population was 10,049 in 1962 ( Shouwa 37 ).  

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC

The town hall of Yakumo is located near the mouth of the Yuurappu 
River.  It was the former Yakumo Town's town hall.  National Route 277 
( Unseki-kokudou ) branches off from National Route 5 ( Oonuma-
kokudou ) near the town hall.  The Hakodate Main Line runs along 
Uchiura Bay.  #3

INDUSTRY

Distinctive industries in Yakumo are animal husbandry, agriculture, 
fishery, and meat processing.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = pasture 
4,360 hectares in 2015, fodder 989, paddy rice 338, potatoes 90 
( including seed potatoes ), adzuki beans 67, soybeans 51, green onions 
20 ( mainly Nanpaku-negui ), flowers 3, spinach 1, tomatoes 1, 

( continue ) --> other vegetables 95+X ( pumpkins, sweet corn ), and 
other grains 11 ; dairy cows 9,111 head in 2015, beef cattle 4,893, and 
hens 1,800.  STATISTICS OF FISHERIES = right-eye flounder 642 tons in 
2017, salmon 403, squid ( flying squid 248, other squid 1 ), ``konbu'' 
kelp 178, walleye pollock 156, shellfish 144, shrimp/prawns 81, 

( continue ) --> octopus 66, yellowtail 52, crabs ( snow crabs 22, 
other crabs 16 ), herring 21, olive flounder 20, Pacific cod 19, sea 
urchins 18, mackerel 16, akta mackerel 15, sharks 9, sea trout 7, 
``hatahata'' 3, other fish 236, and other marine animals 118.  #4  #5

As for meat processing, Japan Food Packer's Dounan Factory ( Nippon Ham 
Group ) processes pork and beef.  There was a Kentucky Fried Chicken's 
experiment farm.  It's now a tourist farm.  #6  #7

As for sightseeing, Yakumo Hot Spring and Ken-ichi Hot Spring are 
located along National Route 277.  Guinkon-yu Hot Spring is located 
along the Otoshibe River.  The name ``Guinkon'' means silver-wedding-
anniversary in Japanese. 

In the former Kumaishi Town, Abalone Festival is held in late May.  I 
passed the festival during a bus excursion.  Many cars gathered and 
police officers guided them.

NOTE
 
#1

Yoshikatsu Tokugawa asked land granting because he intended to make 
jobs for former samurai in the Owari Domain ( now Aichi Prefecture ).  
His offer was accepted in 1879 ( Meiji 11 ) and Tokugawa Farm was 
established in Yuurappu district in Yamakoshinai village.  The area was 
496 hectares.

The name ``Yuurappu'' comes from ``yu-rap'' in Ainu language.  It 
means ``hot spring, down.''  In 1881 ( Meiji 14 ), Yuurappu district 
was separated from Yamakoshinai village and became a new village.  At 
the time, Yoshikatsu Tokugawa named it Yakumo.  The name reffered to 
the oldest poem in the country.  The lyrics are as follows ;

``Yakumo-tatsu  Izumo-yaegaki  Tsumagomi-ni  Yaegaki-tsukuru 
  Sono-yaegaki-wo.''

( Overlapping clouds stand, fences within fences in Izumo, to live 
  with my wife, make fences within fences, the fences within fences ).

It is said that the poem was read by Susanoo-no-mikoto, who was a god 
of mythology.  The lyrics are mysterious.  Actually, it's an old song 
for weddings.

Tokugawa Farm started Western-style agriculture including dairy 
farming.  In winter, farmers on the farm carved wooden bears.  The 
wooden bears became a famous souvenir from Hokkaidou.  The prototype of 
the wooden bears was a carved wooden bear that Yoshichika Tokugawa 
bought in Switzerland in 1922.

As of 1927 ( Shouwa 2 ), Tokugawa Farm owned 5,083 hectares of land 
including forests.  After World War II, the Allied Powers ( US Army ) 
ordered land reform throughout the country.  Tokugawa Farm was 
demolished.

( For large farm in the Meiji period, see Kyougoku Town in Shiribeshi 
  general sub-prefecture. )

As for the land reform after World War II, troubles continued.  For 
example, a family in a rural area owns 2-tan ( 20 ares ) of paddy 
fields, but they don't cultivate.  They lent the land.

( continue ) --> The family was informed just before the enactment of 
the Land Reform Act, and borrowed the land from a landlord.  After the 
enactment the law, the landlord had to transfer the land to the tenant 
farmer.  The landlord still says, ``I hate them ( the family ) very 
much.  I want to kill them.'' 
 
#2 

Ekashiba ( 1847-1924 ) was an Ainu leader, livestock dealer, and
veterinarian.  His Japanese name was Takojirou Benkai.  In 1900 ( Meiji 
33), he presented two brown bears for the wedding ceremony of the Crown 
Prince ( later Emperor Taishou ).

In 1902 ( Meiji 35), during a winter training on Mt. Hakkouda in Aomori
Prefecture, 199 soldiers died.  Ekashiba and other seven Ainu men 
conducted search operations for missing soldiers.  In 1924, after 
drinking with his business partners, he fell from a log bridge and 
died.

( For Mt. Hakkkouda disaster, sea Kucchan Town in Shiribeshi general 
  sub-prefecture. )
 
#3

National Route 277 runs near the town hall of Yakumo.  It crosses 
Unseki Pass and reaches Kumaishi.  Unseki Pass divides Pacific Ocean 
side and the Sea of Japan side. 

The name ``Un-seki'' means ``cloud, stone'' in Japanese.  The word 
``un'' ( cloud ) means ``kumo'' in Ya-kumo and the word ``seki'' 
( stone ) means ``ishi'' in Kuma-ishi.
 
The town hall of the former Kumaishi Town was located along the Sea of 
Japan.  National Route 229 ( Hiyama-kokudou ) passes along the 
coastline of the Sea of Japan.  The town hall and the national highway 
belonged to Hiyama sub-prefecture. 

From the town hall of the former Kumaishi Town hall to the current 
Yakumo Town hall, it takes about an hour by car.  The current Yakumo 
Town hall is located in Oshima general sub-prefecture.  There were many 
reasons for the merger beyond the sub-prefecture border.  

#4  

During the Taishou period ( 1912-1926 ), farmers in Yakumo cultivated 
mainly potatoes.  The potatoes were processed into potato starch and 
exported abroad.  The export declined due to the Great Depression.  
After the 1930s, dairy farming increased. 

Nowadays, Yakumo is the main dairy farming region in south Hokkaidou.  
The main breed of dairy cow is Holstein.  It is the largest breed among 
cows and cattle.  ( Cows are for milk and cattle are for beef. )
 
Holstein is a black and white cow.  Holstein was bread in Netherlands. 
It was named after a place in Germany.  In Japanese, it was called 
``roppaku-gyuu,'' which means ``six-white, cow,'' because there are six 
white spots around its nose. 

( For dairy cow, see Eniwa City in Ishikari sub-prefecture. )

#5

As for fishery in Yakumo, the main catches in the Pacific side are 
farmed scallops, salmon, right-eye flounder, and farmed ``konbu'' kelp. 
The main catches in the Sea of Japan side are walleye pollock, squid, 
and farmed abalone.  For the abalone culture, deep sea water is used.  
Deep sea water is rich in minerals and low in bacteria. 
  
Abalone is expensive.  The shell is hard.  Inside the shell is mother-
of-peal ( ``shinju-sou'' ).  The shell has holes.  They are for 
breathing.  ( They are not eyes.  Scallops have eyes. )

#6

In Hokkaidou, fried chicken is called ``zangui.''  The word would have 
come from Chinese.  In Japanese, fried chicken is generally called 
``kara-ague.''  It means ``Tang ( old China ), deep fried.''

As for chicken, I think chicken tastes better in Hokkaidou.  Chickens 
can grow healthy under a cool climate, without sweating.

#7

A meat processing factory is located in Yakumo Town.  Swine farms are 
located in mountainous areas in Mori Town and Imakane Towns.  In the 
past, Brazilians worked at the meat processing factory.  There was a 
Brazilian restaurant in the center of Yakumo Town. 

( For swine industry, see Toubetsu Town in Ishikari sub-prefecture. )

Masao Kanamori who instructed a brass band club at a local high school 
said ; ``I sometimes went to the Brazilian restaurant.  There were 
bossa nova concerts.  I was hooked on bossa nova.''

Students played musical instruments at Faculty of Comparative Culture, 
University of Tsukuba.  They also recommended bossa nova. 
( This is an example of bossa nova from Rio de Janeiro ; ``Aguas De 
Marco'' ( a'guas de ma'ruso ) --> http://youtu.be/w7RoHKfOrD0 )
 
( In English ; ``Aguas De Marco'' is ``Water Of March.''  ``Rio de 
  Janeiro'' is ``River of January.'' )

( UTF code ; Aguas de marco = \u00C1guas de mar\u00E7o. )

( Single of the chapter ; ``Frevo'' from Brasil.  --> 
  http://youtu.be/Vjy5D4RZ_yw )

|                  |
|    OSHAMANBE     |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 11  |
 ------------------
AREA 311 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 232, CULTIVATED 18 ; COUNTY = YAMAKOSHI ; 
POP 9,127 ( 1990 ), 6,386 ( 2010 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 7.4 C ( AV 1981-
2010 ), PRECIPI 1,289 MM, SNOW 613 CM ; COUNTRY SIGN = CRAB & IRIS. 
 
LOCATION

Oshamanbe ( o-sha-ma-n-be ) is a town in Oshima general sub-prefecture, 
south Hokkaidou ( ``dou-nan'' ).  ( The official spelling is 
``Oshamambe.'' )  Oshamanbe is located in the root of the Oshima 
Peninsula.  It faces Uchiura Bay.  The rivers of Oshamanbe, Monbetsu, 
and Kun-nui flow.  #1  #2  #3

( UTF code ; Oshamanbe = \u9577\u4E07\u90E8. )

NAME 

The name ``Oshamanbe'' is derived from ``o-samam-pet'' in Ainu 
language, which means ``river mouth, bend, river.''  Or, it's derived 
from ``o-samampe'' in Ainu language, which means ``river mouth, 
flatfish.'' 

HISTORY 

During the Edo era, in 1669, the Ainu forces advanced from Hidaka to 
Matsumae.  It was the revolt of Shakushain ( Shakushain-no-tatakai ).  
The leader of the Ainu forces was Shakushain, a chief of Ainu people.  
The Ainu forces were defeated by the gun corps of the Matsumae Domain 
along the Kun-nui River.  #4 
 
In 1773, the Matsumae Domain built a guardhouse ( ``ban-sho'' ) and 
Inari Shrine.  ( Updated in 2022.  On the graund of this Inari Shrine, 
water gushed out.  The water pillar reached a height of 30 meters. 
It continued from August to September 2022.  It was an old well for 
drilling natural gas.  The shrine is a mysterious place. )

In 1864, a trading place ( ``basho'' ) in Yamakoshinai was abolished, 
and Yamakoshinai village and Oshamanbe village were established. 
Yamakoshinai was located in what is now Yakumo Town.
 
After the Meiji period, in 1872 ( Meiji 5 ), Oshamanbe branch of 
Hakkaidou Development Commission was established.  In 1873 ( Meiji 6 ), 
a village chief ( ``kochou'' ) was appointed.  It was a continuation of 
village chief system from the Edo era.  In 1903 ( Meiji 36 ), Oshamanbe 
Station on the Hokkaidou Railway opened ( now Hakodate Main Line ).  

In 1906 ( Meiji 39 ), Oshamanbe Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1923 ( Taishou 12 ), Oshamanbe Village ( 1st-class 
municipality ) was established.  The Nagawa Line opened ( now Muroran 
Main Line ).  Oshamanbe Station became a junction.  In 1943 ( Shouwa 
18 ), Oshamanbe Town was established.

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Matsumae 
  Town. )

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC

The town hall of Oshamanbe is located near the mouth of the Oshamanbe 
River.  National Route 37 ( Shizukari-kokudou ) branches off from 
National Route 5.  ( National Route 5 in Oshamanbe is called Oonuma-
kokudou in the south and Youtei-kokudou in the north ).  The Muroran 
Main Line branches off from the Hakodate Main Line.

The eastern part is Shizukari Pass and Rebungue Pass.  They are steep 
sea cliffs.  So, the first road and railroad that connected Hakodate 
and Sapporo headed inland from Oshamanbe.  Currently, they are National 
Route 5 and the Hakodate Main Line.  #5  #6
 
Cf. The Sapporo Main Road ( Sapporo-hondou ) that connected Hakodate 
and Sapporo in the Meiji period had a ferry line beteewn Mori and 
Muroran.  Currently, it is National Route 5 and National Route 36. 

( For the Sapporo Main Road, see Mori Town in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. )  

INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industries in Oshamanbe are fishery and dairy farming.  
STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = pasture 1,412 hectares in 2015 and fodder 
239 ; dairy cows 2,224 head in 2015.  STATISTICS OF FISHERIES = salmon 
386 tons in 2017, shellfish 107 ( mainly farmed scallop ), 

( continue ) --> right-eye flounder 93, yellowtail 27, sea urchins 20, 
walleye pollock 17, crabs 10 ( including horsehair crabs ), olive 
flounder 13, flying squid 8, tuna 2, mackerel 1, octopus 1, seaweed 1, 
herring - ( zero ), other fish 38, and other marine animals 17.

Fishing ports are located in Shizukari, Oshamanbe, Oonaka, and Kun-nui. 
Crabs are the specialty of the town.  The lunch box of Oshamanbe 
Station is Kani-meshi ( crab rice ).  #7  

NOTE 

#1

Oshima Peninsula is located in the south of Hokkaidou.  The area lies 
south of a line connecting Oshamanbe, Kuromatsunai Basin, and Suttsu 
Bay.

As for Oshamanbe River, Natural gas is extracted in the lower reaches.
Futamata Radium Hot Spring is located in the upper reaches.  It's a 
secluded hot spring.

As for the Kunnui River, the name comes from ``kunne-nay'' in Ainu 
language.  It means ``black, river.''  The river was black because it 
contained iron sand.  In the past, iron sand was collected in 
Oshamanbe and Yakumo and transported to Muroran for use in steel mills.
 
( For iron mining, see Kyougoku Town in Shiribeshi general sub- 
  prefecture. )

#2

In Ainu language, rivers are called ``nay'' or ``pet.''  The word 
``nay'' means a stream, whose course is changeable, for example, 
Wakkanai, Utashinai, and Kikonai.

The word ``pet'' means a river, whose course is stable.  The word 
``pet'' is called ``betsu'' in place names, for example, Ebetsu, 
Makubetsu, and Shibetsu.  Rivers were place names. 

There are also rivers with Japanese names in Hokkaidou. The most 
typical names are ``Futamata-gawa'' and ``Futamata-zawa.''  There are 
54 Futamata-gawa rivers and 42 Futamata-zawa rivers in Geospatial 
Information Authority Map ( Chiri-in-chizu ) in March 2025.

The name ``Futamata-gawa'' means ``bifurcation ( baifu-ca'tion ) 
river.''  The name ``Futamata-zawa'' means ``bifurcation stream.'' 
Names were simple in the past.

#3

These are rivers with funny names in Oshamanbe ; ( 1 ) The Kyuushi-
battari River, which sounds like ``sudden death river'' in Japanese.

( 2 ) The Warui River, which means ``bad river'' in Japanese.  It was 
bad because salmons did not appear in autumn and timbers could not be 
transported.  ( by Housei Nagata 1891.  ``Explanation Of Ezo Language 
In Hokkaidou ( Hokkaidou-ezo-go-chimei-kai ).'' )

Cf.  As for the name ``bad river,'' Enbetsu Town in Rumoi sub-
prefecture has the same meaning.  It comes from ``wen-pet'' in Ainu 
language.  It means ``bad, river.''  The river was bad because there 
were few salmon.  Ainu people ate dried salmon during the long winter.

These are rivers with funny names in Hokkaidou ;
( 1 ) The Yarikire-nai River in Yuni Town, Sorachi general sub-
prefecture, which sounds ``I cannot bear it! -river'' in Japanese.  The 
name comes from ``yanke-nai'' in Ainu language, which means ``no fish, 
stream.''
 
( 2 ) The Obake River in Urakawa Town, Hidaka sub-prefecture.  It means 
``ghost river'' in Japanese.  Ghosts appeared along the river in 
winter.  ( Usually, ghosts appear in summer in Japan.  See Urakawa 
Town. ) 

( 3 ) The Osaru River in Date City, Iburi general sub-prefecture.  It 
sounds like ``a little monkey river'' in Japanese.  The name comes from 
``o-sar-pet'' in Ainu language.  It means ``river mouth, reed, river.'' 
Ainu people made their houses using reeds.  Upstream of the Osaru River 
even lies Niagara Falls.  

( In Japanese ; ``Reed'' ( plant ) is ``ashi.'' ) 
 
( For Niagara Falls, see Date City, Iburi general sub-prefecture. ) 
 
( 4 ) The Chiri-chiri River in Shiriuchi Town, Oshima general sub-
prefecture.  It sounds ``geography, geography river'' or ``dust, dust 
river'' in Japanese.  The name comes from ``chir-chir-nay'' in Ainu 
language.  It means ``bird, bird, stream.''

( 5 ) The Doudoromappu River in Tsubetsu Town, Okhotsk general sub-
prefecture.  It sounds like ``a muddy map river'' in Japanese.  The 
name comes from ``tu-utur-oma-p'' in Ainu language.  It means ``two, 
together, enter, river.''

( 6 ) The Ponkikin River in Tsubetsu Town, Okhotsk general sub-
prefecture.  In Google search, ``do you mean Ponkikki?,'' which was an 
old TV show for children.  The name comes from ``pon-kikin'' in Ainu 
language.  It means ``small, hackberry.''

( In Japanese ; ``Hackberry'' is ``ezo-no-uwamizu-zakura.''  
  ``Chinese hackberry'' is ``enoki.''  )

( 7 ) The Kemichap River in Tsubetsu Town, Okhotsk general sub-
prefecture.  It sounds like ``ketchap river.''  The name comes from 
``kene-cha-p'' in Ainu language.  It means ``alder, to cut, river.''  
Nature is affluent in Ainu culture.  

( In Japanese ; ``Alder'' is ``han-no-ki.'' )

( 8 ) The Bochi-no-sawa River in Oketo Town, Okhotsk general sub-
prefecture.  It's a Japanese name, meaning ``the stream of cemetery.'' 
Will-o'-the-wisps may appear.  When I found the name, I remembered Boot 
Hill cemetery in the pioneering years of the USA.

( 9 ) Gakkari-zawa in Otaru City, Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture.  
It's a Japanese name,  meaning ``disappointment stream.''  It was an 
old place name around Asarigawa Hot Spring.  There were hopes and 
disappointments in the pioneering years.

#4

Shakushain was a chief of Ainu people.  Before the revolt of 
Shakushain, there were conflicts between Ainu chiefs.  A rumor spread 
among Ainu people like this, ``An Ainu messenger who attempted to 
borrow weapons from the Matsumae Domain was poisoned to death.''

Then, Ainu chiefs united and revolted in many places.  The Ainu forces 
attacked trading ships, placer gold miners, and falcon catchers.  But, 
the Ainu forces were defeated by the gun corps of the Matsumae Domain.

Reading this history, I thought, ``( 1 ) People unite when they have a 
common enemy.  ( 2 ) They could not beat Western culture.  They could 
not beat the guns.'' 

The background of the revolt was the trade imbalance between Ainu 
people and Japanese people.  Japanese people obtained goods at unfairly 
low prices.  Also, the eruption of Mt. Tarumae in 1667 may have caused 
food shortages.
  
#5

As for the population of Oshamanbe, the percentage of residents aged 18 
to 19 is relatively high.  This is because Toukyou University of 
Science has Oshamanbe Campus.  Students enroll in April and leave in 
February.

The Oshamanbe campus opened in 1987.  It was during the bubble economy. 
Baby boomers supported the economy and their children, the 2nd baby 
boomers, went on to universities.
 
Hokkaidou has a very low population density.  Looking at a map of 
population density in the country, Hokkaidou looks like a black 
continent ( HTTP://MOOO.COM/NOTE/INTF1.JPG ).

Today, most people live in cities.  A professor who moved from a suburb 
of Toukyou to Hokkaidou said that he could sell his house for more than 
he paid for it.  City residents consume rather than produce.  

( For depopulation, see Otaru City in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

As for Hokkaidou's population, Hokkaidou University is like a water 
tower.  It's a large university.  Students come from all over the 
country and overseas.

Hokkaidou university has 18,000 students.  It's more than the 
population of most towns and villages in Hokkaidou.  Students live in 
Hokkaidou for four years and more.  Quite a number of them find jobs in 
Hokkaidou.  ( Toukyou Metropolitan University, where I'm working now, 
has 8,500 students. )

In the case of University of Tsukuba, where I studied, there were 
international students from Taiwan in the the 1980s.  My teachers 
taught them.  In the 1990s, our faculty had an excursion in Taiwan.  
The former international students became professors.  They were kind to 
us.

From the 1990s through the 2000s, I didn't meet any international 
students from Taiwan, but I met many South Korean students.  In the 
2010s, I didn't meet any South Korean students.  

Maybe those countries became rich and young people go to the USA and 
Europe. Maybe there's nothing to learn from this small country.  But 
many Taiwanese and South Koreans visit Hokkaidou for sightseeing.  

In 2024, I talked with a professor from Beijing.  He said, ``Today, 
Chinese students also want to go the USA and Europe, but Japan is still 
popular, because tuition fees are low.''

( For international student, see the note of Otaru City in Shiribeshi 
  general sub-prefecture. )

#6

The eastern end of Oshamanbe Town is Rebungue Pass.  Rebungue Pass is a 
steep sea cliff located on the border with Toyoura Town.  In the Edo 
era, Rebungue Pass was counted as one of the three most difficult 
passes in Ezo.  ( Ezo is the old name for Hokkaidou. )  

Two other difficult passes were Saru Pass in Erimo Town ( Hidaka sub-
prefecture ) and Raiden Pass in Iwanai Town ( Shiribeshi general sub-
prefecture ). 

( For Rebungue Pass, see Toyoura Town in Iburi general sub-
  prefecture. )

#7

The shape of the Port of Kun-nui is like a wine glass.  This shape 
makes it easy for fishermen to moor their fishing boats.  Kun-nui is 
located at the innermost part of Uchiura Bay.  The tidal range is 
large.  A long sandy beach appears at low tide.  In the past, fishermen 
had difficulty leaving the port at low tide.

( Single of the chapter ; ``Orinoco Flow'' by Enya.  --> 
  http://youtu.be/654tlKKI_Ys )
 __________________
|                  |
|    KAMINOKUNI    |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 12  |
 ------------------
AREA 548 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 505, CULTIVATED 6 ; COUNTY = HIYAMA ; 
POP 7,747 ( 1990 ), 5,428 ( 2010 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = WARRIOR'S HELMET. 
 
LOCATION 
 
Kaminokuni ( ka-mi-no-ku-ni ) is a town in Hiyama sub-prefecture, south 
Hokkaidou ( ``dou-nan'' ).  The area faces the Sea of Japan.  Forests 
occupy 92 percent of the area.  The Amanogawa River flows from Mt. 
Tongari.  The Ishizaki River flows from Mt. Dai-sen-guen. 

( UTF code ; Kaminokuni = \u4E0A\u30CE\u56FD. )

NAME 

The name ``Kami-no-kuni'' is Japanese.  It means ``upper, of, 
country.''  In the 15th century, the Andou clan in Tsugaru ( now Aomori 
Prefecture ) entered Ezo ( now Hokkaidou Prefecture ).  They called the 
western part ``Kami-no-kuni'' and the eastern part ``Shimo-no-kuni.''  
The name ``Shimo-no-kuni'' means ``country, of, lower.'' 

Kami-no-kuni was near to the Andou clan's homeland.  Shimo-no-kuni was 
far from it.  Shimo-no-kuni was located in what are now Hakodate and 
Hokuto cities.  In their homeland, the Andou clan also governed Kami-
no-kuni and Shimo-no-kuni.  ( Their homeland was what are now Aomori 
and Akita prefectures. ) 

HISTORY 

In the early 13th century, the Andou clan traded with Ainu people.  
They also managed exiles from the Kamakura Shogunate.  In the mid 15th 
century, the Nanbu clan advanced to Tsugaru, so Masasue Andou and his 
vassals escaped to Ezo.  #1 

Masasue Andou built 12 forts in Ezo.  In the area what is now 
Kaminokuni Town, there were two forts ; Hanazawa-date and Hiishi-date. 
Around the forts were Japanese settlements.  They were one of the 
oldest Japanese settlements in Ezo.  #2  #3
 
Koshamain's War occurred in 1457.  Koshamain was an Ainu chief.  
Nobuhiro Takeda, who was a vassal of the Andou clan, ended the war.  In 
the 16th century, the main fort in Ezo moved from Kaminokuni to 
Matsumae. 

During the Edo era ( 1603-1868 ), Kaminokuni was governed by the 
Matsumae Domain.  Kaminokuni was a village of Buddhist temples, Shintou 
shrines, and fishing. 

( For the Andou clan, see Hakodate City and Nanae Town in Oshima 
  general sub-prefecture. )  

( For the oldest Japanese village, see Fukushima Town in Oshima general 
  sub-prefecture. )

After the Meiji period, in 1872 ( Meiji 5 ), administrative offices 
( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) were established in Kaminokuni and Shiofuki 
villages.  Those were a continuation of village chief ( ``kochou'' ) 
system from the Edo era.

In 1879 ( Meiji 12 ), three administrative offices for seven villages 
were established ; One was for the villages of ( 1 ) Chiisago and ( 2 ) 
Ishizaki ; One was for the villages of ( 3 ) Kaminokuni, ( 4 ) Kita, 
and ( 5 ) Oodome ; One was for the villages of ( 6 ) Kinoko and ( 7 ) 
Shiofuki.

( UTF code ; Chiisago = \u5C0F\u7802\u5B50.  Ishizaki = \u77F3\u5D0E. 
  Kinoko = \u6728\u30CE\u5B50.  Kita = \u5317.  Oodome = \u5927\u7559. 
  Shiofuki = \u6C50\u5439. )

In 1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Kaminokuni Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established by the merger of seven villages.  In 1967 ( Shouwa 42 ), 
Kaminokuni Town was established.

( For 2nd-class municipality, see the note of Matsumae Town. )

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC 

The town hall of Kaminokuni is located near the mouth of the Amanogawa 
River.  National Route 228 ( Matsumae-kokudou ) runs along the sea.  #4
#5

Previously, the Esashi Line ran along the valley of the Amanogawa 
River.  The railroad connected Esashi Station and Kikonai Station until 
2014.  The former station sites remain ( as of when I wrote this draft 
in 2019 ).  There was also Amanogawa Station for sightseeing.  The name 
``Amanogawa'' is Japanese and means ``the Milky Way.''

INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industries in Kaminokuni are fishing, paddy rice 
cultivation, and forestry.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 239 
hectares in 2015, beans ( soybeans 74, adzuki beans 8, other beans 1 ), 
wheat 71, buckwheat 37, orchards 5, pasture 5, 

( continue ) --> fodder 4, and other vegetables 30+X ( snow peas, 
asparagus, ``nira'' ( Chinese chive or gynmigit ) ) ; dairy cows X 
( concealed ) in 2015.  STATISTICS OF FISHERIES = atka mackerel 130 
tons in 2017, olive flounder 44, sea urchins 34, salmon 27, squid 
( flying squid 12, other squid 15 ), octopus 25, shrimp/prawns 8, 

( continue ) --> Pacific cod 7, shellfish ( abalone 1, other shellfish 
6 ), tuna 6, yellowtail 6, right-eye flounder 5, mackerel 4, sharks 4, 
sardines 3, sea trout 1, herring 0, other fish 23, and other marine 
animals 5.  #6  #7 

As for sightseeing, Joukokuji Temple is the oldest building in 
Hokkaidou.  The current building was built in 1758.  Fort Hanazawa was 
located on a hill near the center of the town.  Nowadays, windmills 
stand on the hill.  #8  #9

NOTE

#1

As for exiles in the early 13th century, thieves, pirates, and 
political prisoners were caught in the western country.  They were 
taken to the eastern country ( now Kantou region ).  After their trial, 
they were exiled to remote areas and islands including Ezo ( now 
Hokkaidou region ). 

As for the explorers of Ezo, Tadataka Inou and Takeshirou Matsuura are 
famous.  Tadataka Inou surveyed Ezo in 1800 to plot precise coastline 
on the map.  Takeshirou Matsuura explored Ezo in 1845, 1846, 1849, 
1856, 1857, and 1858.  He surveyed place names and drew atlas and 
gazetteers of Ezo.  

More than five centuries before these explores, Japanese people lived 
in what is now south Hokkaidou.  But, most of the area was unknown. 
Hokkaidou is a large island.  The climate is too cold to live for 
Japanese people before the Edo era. 

#2

This is about Koshamain's War.  In 1456, an Ainu man asked a Japanese 
blacksmith to make a large knife.  The knife is called ``makiri'' in 
Japanese.

When the knife was made, the Ainu man quarreled over the quality and 
the price.  The blacksmith got angry and stabbed the Ainu man to death 
with the large knife.
 
In 1457, Ainu people united under Koshamain and attacked Japanese 
people.  ( Koshamain was an Ainu chief. )  The Ainu forces destroyed 10 
of 12 forts.  ( ``Fort'' was called ``date'' in Japanese.  The word 
exists in place names such as Hakodate and Shimodate. )

These were the 10 destroyed forts ; Shinori-date ( in what is now 
Hakodate City ), Usukeshi-date ( Hakodate City ), Nakano-date ( Kikonai 
Town ), Wakimoto-date ( Shiriuchi Town ), 

( continue ) --> Onnai-date ( Fukushima Town ), Oyobe-date ( Matsumae 
Town ), Oo-date ( Matsumae Town ), Nebota-date ( Matsumae Town ), 
Haraguchi-date ( Matsumae Town ), and Hiishi-date ( Kaminokuni Town ).

Japanese people escaped to Hanazawa-date ( in what is now Kaminokuni 
Town ) and Mobetsu-date ( Hokuto City ).  In 1458, Nobuhiro Takeda shot 
Koshamain and his son to death with a bow.  The Koshamain's war ended.

However, the conflict between Ainu people and Japanese people 
continued. Mobetsu-date was also destroyed by the Ainu forces in 1508.

#3

Masasue Andou built 12 forts in Ezo ( old Hokkaidou ) and returned to 
his homeland.  Then, he built Hiyama Castle in what is now Noshiro 
City, Akita Prefecture. 

Among the vassals of the Andou clan were the Kakizaki clan and the 
Takeda clan.  The Kakizaki and Takeda clans stayed in Ezo.  Nobuhiro 
Takeda, who ended the Koshamain's War, inherited the Kakizaki clan.  He 
changed his family name from Takeda to Kakizaki. 

Nobuhiro Kakizaki ( formerly Nobuhiro Takeda ) built a new fort, 
Katsuyama-date.  It was located next to Hanazawa-date, one of the 12 
forts built by the Andou clan. Both Katsuyama-date and Hanazawa-date 
were located in what is now Kaminokuni Town.  

Mitsuhiro Kakizaki, who was the son of Nobuhiro Kakizaki, moved the 
main fort from Katsuyama-date to Oo-date.  ( Oo-date was located in 
what is now Matsumae Town. )  His descendant, Yoshihiro Kakizaki, 
founded the Matsumae Domain in the Edo era. 

( For the Matsumae Domain, see Matsumae Town in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. )

#4

As for the Amanogawa River in Kaminokuni Town, the Kanji characters are 
the same as the Milky Way.  This is a story about the Milky Way 
( Amanogawa ) in Kaminokuni Town.

``In 1614, Geronimo de Angelis, who was a Jesuit missionary from Sicily 
  Island, was heading for Matsumae.  He landed in Kaminokuni due to a 
  storm.  Later, he drew a map of Ezo and marked his landing point 
  ``Tcuga ( tsuga ).'' 

The old name of Kaminokuni was Tsuga.  The pronunciation of the Kanji 
characters changed to Amanogawa.  The original name ``Tsuga'' 
comes from ``to-ka'' in Ainu language.  It means ``pond, shore.''

( UTF code ; Amanogawa = \u5929\u306E\u6CB3.  Tcuga = T\u00E7uga. 
  Tsuga = \u5929\u6CB3. ) 
  
#5

The built-up area of Kaminokuni Town faces the sea.  In July 1993, 
Okushiri earthquake occurred.  At the time, tidal waves reached the 
coast of Hiyama sub-prefecture.

But, the built-up area of Kaminokuni suffered very little damage.  
Because, Okushiri Island acted as a shield between the epicenter and 
Kaminokuni.  In Okushiri Island, more than 200 people died.
 
( For Okushiri earthquake, see Okushiri Town in Hiyama sub-
  prefecture. ) 
 
#6

As for paddy rice cultivation in Kaminokuni Town, paddy fields stretch 
out in front of the mountains.  The landscape is like the countryside 
of Honshuu Island.

According to 2015 Census of Agriculture and Forestry, the average paddy 
rice cultivation area per farm is as follows ; 
   10.2 hectares ; Sorachi general sub-prefecture, which is one of the 
      active rice producing area in the coutnry.
   7.9 hectares ; Hokkaidou. 
   3.9 hectares ; Kaminokuni Town.
   1.1 hectares ; Honshuu Island.

The year 1993 was a crop failure ( fa'y-lee-ur ) in rice harvest, 
because of cold weather.  At the time, a crop failure division was 
established in Kaminokuni town hall.  ( A crop failure is called 
``kyousaku'' in Japanese. ) 
 
( UTF code ; ``kyousaku'' = \u51F6\u4F5C. )

( For paddy rice cultivation, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi general sub-
  prefecture. ) 

( For average cultivated area per farm, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi 
  general sub-prefecture. ) 
 
At the time of the crop failure, I was a university student.  I ate 
Thai rice in the university canteen.  The Japanese government urgently 
imported Thai rice.  Many Japanese people complained about the 
difference in taste of rice.

Japanese rice production is like the just-in-time method.  Neither 
farmers nor the government produce surplus.  Japanese people like 
``new rice.''  ( ``New rice'' is called ``shin-mai'' in Japanese. ) 

( The just-in-time method was adopted by Toyota Motor.  It was during 
  the post-war period of material shortages.  Meanwhile, the mass-
  production method was adopted by Ford Motor in the USA.  The company 
  lead the world's first motorization at the beginning of the 20th 
  century. )

( For motorization, see the note of Otobe Town in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. ) 

The year 1993 was a cold summer.  A cold summer is called ``reika'' in 
Japanese.  That year, I was writing my graduation thesis at the 
laboratory of geography, University of Tsukuba.  At the beginning of 
the summer, a large cooler was installed in the laboratory, however, we 
didn't use it.

When I was a child, if the temperature rose by 30 degrees Celsius, it 
was a hot day.  Nowadays, the temperature in Toukyou becomes above 36.5 
degrees Celsius every day in summer.  ( Higher than body temperature. )

The year 2025 was a rice shortage.  It was not caused by a cold summer. 
( The summer was hot in Toukyou. )  The cause lay in the market.  It 
was an imbalance between supply and demand.

International students from Asian countries were surprised by the 
rising price of rice in supermarkets.  My wife bought Government 
Reserved Rice ( Bichiku-mai ).  Then, she bought California rice 
( Calrose ).  She said, ``My pride doesn't allow it, but...''  ( She 
was born and grown in a rural area. )  

I suggested, ``Let's eat bread instead of rice.''  She got angry and 
said, ``You will be fine!  But, the children won't!''  I think I 
understood why Marie Antoinette was criticized when she said, ``Let 
them eat biscuits instead of bread.''  Arguments over food are 
dangerous.
 
#7

Edible mountain plants are called ``sansai'' in Japanese.  On May 19, 
2013, I traveled from Kikonai to Kaminokuni on a bus excursion.  I saw 
several cars parked in the mountains.  People went into the mountains 
to pick edible mountain plants.

According to a graduation thesis submitted to Faculty of Letters at 
Hokkaidou University, fern pickers used to come Kaminokuni Town from 
Niigata Prefecture every spring.  ( Fern ( ``zen-mai'' in Japanese ) 
and blacken ferns ( ``warabi'' ) are typical edible mountain plants. )

Fern pickers stayed in mountain huts where they collected, boiled, and 
dried ferns.  When the harvest season was over, they rented freight 
trains and shipped dried ferns to Oosaka.

Edible mountain plants grown in snowy regions are large and of good 
quality.  For example, a family in Murakami City, Niigata Prefecture, 
earned over one million yen in one season by picking wild edible 
mountain plants.  ( by Takeo Ichikawa 1993.  ``Four Seasons And Life Of 
Japan ( Nihon-no-shiki-to-kurashi )'' published by Kokon-shoin. )

#8

These are old buildings in Kaminokuni.  ( 1 ) Joukokuji Temple, a 
Buddhist temple built in 1443.  The year was around time that Fort 
Hanazawa ( Hanazawa-date ) was built.

The temple is located at the foot of the hill where where Fort Hanazawa 
stood.  The existing building was rebuild in 1758.  The name 
``Joukoku'' is Japanese.  The meaning is the same as ``Kaminokuni.''  

( 2 ) Tategami-hachiman Shrine, a Shintou shrine built in 1473.  The 
year was around the time that Fort Katsuyama ( Katsuyama-ate ) was 
built.  The names ``Tategami'' and ``Hachiman'' are Japanese.  These 
signify gods of war.

The existing building was rebuild in 1699.  It was moved from the top 
of a hill to the foot of the hill in 1895.  The name was changed from 
Tategami-hachiman Shrine to Kaminokuni-hachiman Shrine.  The current 
location is next to Joukokuji Temple.
 
( 3 ) The Sasanami's residence, which is the former herring mansion 
( ``nishin-goten'' ) ; The location is close to Joukokuji Temple and 
Kaminokuni-hachiman Shrine.  The residence was built in the early 19th 
century, during the Edo era.  This is the oldest residence in Hokkaidou 
( excluding temples and shrines ).

These buildings are designated as National Important Cultural 
Properties ( Juuyou-bunka-zai ).  Regarding the oldest building in 
the country, it's Hou-ryuu-ji Temple in Nara Prefecture.  It was 
rebuilt in the 7th century.

( For Hou-ryu-uji temple, see Hiyama sub-prefecture. )
 
Temples and shrines last longer than ordinary houses.  The main 
buildings of temples and shrines don't have water facilities such as 
kitchens and bathrooms. 
 
There are many Buddhist temples and Shintou shrines in old towns.  
Kaminokuni is one of them.  Kaminokuni was the original place of the 
Matsumae Domain, which ruled Ezo ( now Hokkaidou ) during the Edo era.
 
In the past, monks in temples and priests in shrines were like civil 
servants.  They recorded people's names and place names.  Ordinary 
people couldn't read and write. In addition, in the past, many people 
were born and many people died.  Monks and priests treat funeral 
services.

#9

There are old legends in Kaminokuni Town.  Ookura herring is one of 
them.  The story has been told since the 1590s, before the Edo era ;

``In a certain year, people couldn't catch herring until the end of 
May.  The fishing season was almost over.  At the time, the elderly 
monk Shuukai Ookura said to the villagers, `I will pray for herring 
to come.  If you catch, please share them with me.  I will repair my 
temple.'

Villagers made a promise.  The monk tied up a sacred rope, fasted, and 
prayed.  One week later, seagulls covered the sea, Shikabe 
( albatross ) gathered on the shore, whales spurted water and made big 
waves.  Schools of herring came. 

The catch was larger than usual.  Villagers were very happy.  However, 
a perverse man refused to give his thanks to the monk, saying, `It's 
just late this year, not your prayer.'  He got into a fight with the 
old monk and the monk died.

Villagers prayed to the spirit of the monk.  After the incident, when a 
small number of herring washed up on the shore in June, villagers 
called them `Ookura herring'.''

These are titles of other legends ; ``Upside-down water pine ( yew ),'' 
``Beheaded pine,'' and ``The path of god.''  People may have made 
legends during the long winter.

( For herring fishing, see Yoichi Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture ; Esashi Town, and Otobe Town in Hiyama sub-prefecture. )

( Single of the chapter ; ``Once In Royal David's City'' --> 
  http://youtu.be/PtiCLJvyePw )

|                  |
|      ESASHI      |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 13  |
 ------------------
AREA 110 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 85, CULTIVATED 10 ; COUNTY = HIYAMA ; 
POP 12,234 ( 1990 ), 9,004 ( 2010 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 10.1 C ( AV 
1981-2010 ), PRECIPI 1,197 MM, SNOW 330 CM ; COUNTRY SIGN = SAIL SHIP & 
ROCK.  

LOCATION  

Esashi ( e-sa-shi ) is a town in Hiyama sub-prefecture, south Hokkaidou 
( ``dou-nan'' ).  The area faces the Sea of Japan.  The shape of the 
town is like the letter ``B.''  The narrow part is the mouth of the 
Assabu River.  ( Assabu Town is located in the narrow part. )

( UTF code ; Esashi = \u6C5F\u5DEE. )

As for the original vegetation, the mountains were covered with 
``hiba'' trees ( a kind of cypress ).  In the Edo era, ``hiba'' trees 
were actively felled.  The logs were transported down the Assabu River. 
The county's name ``Hi-yama'' is Japanese, which means ``cypress 
mountain.''  #1

( For ``hiba'' tree, see Hiyama sub-prefecture. )

NAME

The name ``Esashi'' comes from ``e-sa-us-i'' in Ainu language, which 
means ``head, seashore, exist, place.''  The name stands for a cape 
( promontory ).  It signified Kamome Island in the Port of Esashi.  
( Kamome Island is a tied island. )  The etymology is the same as 
Esashi Town in Souya general sub-prefecture.  #2

HISTORY

In 1216, Ubagami-daijinguu Shrine was established.  It's one of the 
oldest Shintou shrines in Hokkaidou.  In the late 17th century, during 
the Edo era, the Matsumae Domain built a port to transport cypress.  
#3  #4

Herring fishing thrived in the Edo era.  When fishing season began, 
fishermen gathered from Honshuu Island.  The Port of Esashi was 
bustling.  Esashi became one of the largest ports of the Matsumae 
Domain along with Fukuyama ( Matsumae ) and Hakodate.  #5
 
In 1868 ( Meiji 1 ), during the Boshin War, the former Tokugawa 
Shogunate forces occupied Esashi.  In 1869 ( Meiji 2 ), the Meiji 
government forces recaptured Esashi.  They advanced to Hakodate and 
defeated the former Tokugawa Shogunate forces.

In 1872 ( Meiji 5 ), Esashi branch of Hokkaidou Development Commission 
( Kaitaku-shi ) was established.  Administrative offices ( ``kochou-
yakuba'' ) were established for 26 towns in the built-up area of 
Esashi.  These were a continuation of town chief ( ``kochou'' ) system 
from the Edo era.

( UTF code ; ``kochou-yakuba'' = \u6238\u9577\u5F79\u5834. )

In 1880 ( Meiji 13 ), a county office ( ``gun-yakusho'' ) for Hiyama 
and Nishi counties was established.  In 1897 ( Meiji 30 ), Hiyama sub-
prefecture ( Hiyama-shichou ) was established.  The county office of 
Hiyama and Nihsi was abolished.

( UTF code ; Nishi = \u723E\u5FD7. ) 

In 1900 ( Meiji 33 ), Esashi Town ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established by the merger of 26 towns in Esashi and Gokatte village.  
In 1955 ( Shouwa 30 ), Esashi Town merged Tomari Village.  The shape of 
Tomari Village was like the letter ``B.''  #6  #7

( UTF code ; Gokatte = \u4E94\u52DD\u624B.  Tomari = \u6CCA. )

( For 1st-class municipality, see the note of Matsumae Town. )

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC

The town hall of Esashi, Hiyama sub-prefecture bureau, and the Port of 
Esashi are located to the south.  The port is the terminus of National 
Route 227 ( Oono-kokudou ) and National Route 228 ( Matsumae-kokudou ).

A ferry line connects the Port of Esashi and Okushiri Island. 
Previously, the Esashi Line connected Esashi, Kikonai, and Hakodate. 
Near the mouth of the Assabu River, National Route 229 ( Hiyama-
kokudou ) branches off from National Route 227.

National Route 229 goes north along the coast.  National Route 227 
turns east, runs along the Assabu River, crosses the ridge, and leads 
to the Oono Plain ( Hakodate Plain ).  It was one of the roads by which 
the Meiji government forces advanced to Hakodate during the Boshin War.

INDUSTRY

Distinctive industries in Esashi are fishery and sightseeing. 
STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 268 hectares in 2015, beans 
( soybeans 112, adzuki beans 20, other beans 1 ), potatoes 48, wheat 
32, broccoli 21, pasture 13, fodder 7, orchards 4, flowers 3, tomatoes 
1, yams 1, other vegetables 35+X, and other grains 28 ; 

( continue ) --> dairy cows X ( concealed ) in 2015.  STATISTICS OF 
FISHERIES = red snow crabs 438 tons in 2017, squid ( flying squid 314, 
other squid 2 ), octopus 59, sea urchins 25, salmon 19, olive flounder 
15, atka mackerel 7, yellowtail 7, 

( continue ) --> seaweed ( ``konbu'' kelp 1, other seaweed 5 ), 
shellfish 6 ( including Ezo-baka-gai ( mactra ), abalone ), Pacific cod 
5, right-eye flounder 4, sea trout 3, shrimp/prawns 3, herring 1, 
sharks 1, other fish 51, and other marine animals 20 ( including sea 
cucumbers ).

These are tourist destinations in Esashi.  ( 1 ) Kamome Island, which 
means ``seagull island'' in Japanese.  ( For tourist destination on 
Kamom Island, see note No. 8 below. )  ( 2 ) Kaiyou Memorial Hall, 
which is a museum that recreates a sunken ship. 

( continue ) --> ( 3 ) The former Nakamura residence, which was an old 
merchant's mansion.  ( 4 ) A marina in the Port of Esashi.  #8  #9  #10 
#11

( For marina, see Tomakomai City in Iburi general sub-prefecture. )
 
NOTE  
  
#1

As for the vegetation in Esashi Town, Hiyama Ancient Forest ( Hiyama-
koji-no-mori ) is designated as National Natural Monument ( Tennen-
kinen-butsu ).  The forest is located near the border with Kaminokuni 
Town.  It's far from the center of Easashi Town.

The main trees in the protected forest are ``hiba'' ( a kind of 
cypress ) and ``ao-todomatsu'' ( blue Sakhalin fir ).  ``Hiba'' is also 
called ``hinoki-asunaro.''

( ``Todomatsu ( Sakhalin fir ) and Ezomatsu ( Yezo spruce ) are typical 
conifers in central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Todomatsu has smooth 
bark and Ezomatsu has rough bark. ) 

Esasi-sou ( Esashi grass ) is a kind of white mullein ( ma'lein ).  The 
plant was imported from Europe to Japan during the Meiji period ( 1868-
1912 ).  It naturalized in Esashi Town after World War II. 

( For ``hiba'' tree, see Hiyama sub-prefecture. ) 
 
#2

As for the name ``Esashi,'' there is another interpretation.  It says 
that the name comes from ``e-sas-us'' in Ainu language, which means 
``eat, ``konbu'' kelp, exist.''  There is a legend like this ; ``Long 
ago, there was a famine ( fe'-min ).  Ainu people in the north coast 
came here.  They could survive by eating `konbu' kelp.''

( For ``konbu'' kelp, see Shin-hidaka Town in Hidaka sub-prefecture. )

#3

The festival of Ubagami-daijinguu Shrine has been held since 1644.  
It's one of the oldest festivals in Hokkaidou.  A high school teacher 
said, ``If you see festivals, you will see the culture.  Dou-nan 
( south Hokkaidou ) is different from other parts of Hokkaidou.'' 

#4

Takeshirou Matsuura was an explorer in the Edo era.  He recorded place 
names and their locations in Ezo ( now Hokkaidou ).  He spent a winter 
in Esashi in 1846.  It was during his 2nd survey of Ezo.  He earned 
travel expenses by carving seals ( ``in'' ) in Esashi.
 
( UTF code ; ``in'' = \u5370. )
  
#5

Esashi-oiwake is a folk song from Esashi.  It was a song for herring 
fishers in the Edo era.  These are example lyrics ;

``Kuni-wo  Hanarete  Ah  Ezoga-shima  Yansano-ee, 
  Ikuyo  Nezameno  Nami-makura.''
  ( I left the country, ah, and came to Ezo Island, yansano-ee.  
    How many nights did I wake up on the pillow of waves? )

``Nani-wo  Yume-mite  Nakuka-yo  Chidori-nee, 
  Koko-wa  Esashi-no  Kari-no-yado.''
  ( What are you calling and dreaming, seagulls?, nee.  
    This is a temporary lodging in Esashi. )

``Esashi-no  Gogatsu-wa  Edo-nimo-naito, 
  Hokoru  Nishin-no  Haru-no-umi.''
  ( May in Esashi is nowhere even in Edo ( now Toukyou ), 
    proud of the spring sea of herring. )

( For the song ``Souran-bushi,'' which was sung during herring fishing, 
  see Yoichi town in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )

In the past, herring shoals spawned off the coast of Hokkaidou from 
March to May.  Herring were processed into herring meal and transported 
to Oosaka by North-bound ship ( Kitamae-bune ).  Fertilizer wholesalers 
purchased herring meal.  North-bound ships departed from Oosaka and 
sailed along the Seto Inland Sea, the Sea of Japan, and Hokkaidou.

In the Edo era, herring meal and dried sardines were called ``kinpi,''  
which means ``gold fertilizer'' or ``money fertilizer'' in Japanese.  
Farmers started to buy fertilizer with money.  Previously, farmers had 
used leaf litter, livestock manure, and human manure as fertilizers.

``Kinpi,'' the so-called money fertilizer, was used for cultivating 
commercial crops, such as indigo, rapeseed, and cotton.  Those crops 
were mainly cultivated in western Japan.

Herring caught in Hokkaidou were also processed into ``migaki'' ( dried 
herring for eat ) and transported to ``naichi ( main island ).''  

( For herring fishing, see Yoichi Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture ; Otobe Town, and Kaminokuni Town in Hiyama sub-
  prefecture. )
  
#6

As for the former Gokatte village, the Gokatte River and the Port of 
Gokatte are located south of the built-up area of Esashi.

Gokkate-ya-youkan is a souvenir from Esashi.  ``Youkan'' is sweet bean 
paste.  In the Edo era, Gokatte-gumi came from Nanbu.  Gokatte-gumi was 
a group of lumberjacks.  Nanbu was what is now part of Iwate and Aomori 
prefectures. 

Gokkate-gumi fell ``hiba'' trees.  They also succeeded in cultivating 
adzuki-beans in Ezo ( old hokkaidou ).  They made sweet adzuki-bean 
paste and presented it to the lord of the Matsumae Domain.
  
#7

As for the former Tomari Village in Esashi Town, the administrative 
office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) of Tomari village was built in 1878 
( Meiji 11 ).  ( by ``2016 Esashi Town Education Promotion Plan ( 2016 
Esashi-chou-kyouiku-suishin-keikaku ).'' )  

In 1906 ( Meiji 39 ), Tomari Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established.  Before 1955, there were three Tomari villages in 
Hokkaidou.  The name ``Tomari'' was introduced to Ainu langage from 
Japanese languge.

( For the name ``Tomari,'' see Tomari Village in Shiribeshi general
  sub-prefecture. )

#8

Kamome Island is a linked island connected to the mainland by a 
tombolo.  The word ``tombolo'' is a geographic term that means a sand 
bar connecting an island with to mainland.  It comes from the Italian 
word for ``pillow.''

These are tourist sites in Kamome Island ; ( 1 ) Heishi Rock.  It looks 
like a religious vase.  ( 2 ) Senjou-jiki.  This is a large flat rock 
eroded by waves.  The name ``Senjou'' means 1,000 ``tatami'' mats.  
( 3 ) Yoshitsune-no-uma-iwa.  This is a white rock that has been seen 
as the horse of Yoshitsune Minamoto. 

( continue ) --> ( 4 ) Benkei-no-ashiato.  This is large two holes on a 
flat rock.  The holes have been seen as the footprints of Benkei 
Musashibou.  Benkei Musashibou was a monk soldier and a vassal of 
Yoshitsune Minamoto.  ( A hole in the rock on the coast is called ``en-
kama.'' )
  
( continue ) --> ( 5 ) Tokugawa Shogunate battery site.  The guns were 
set by the Matsumae Domain in 1852.  Around the time, American whaling 
ships and Russian ships frequently appear in the waters around Japan.  
( 6 ) Sand beaches ; Two sand beaches are open in summer.  

( For the end of isolationist policy, see Hakodate City in Oshima 
  general sub-prefecture. )

( For monk soldier ( ``sou-hei'' ), see Assabu Town in Hiyama sub-
  prefecture. )

( For Yoshitsune legend, see Biratori Town in Hidaka sub-prefecture ; 
  Otobe Town in Hiyama sub-prefecture. )
 
#9  

Kaiyou was a battleship of the Tokugawa Shogunate.  It was made in the 
Netherlands.  During the Boshin War, the former Tokugawa Shogunate 
forces advanced from Hakodate to Matsumae, and then to Esashi.  The 
battleship Kaiyou also set sail from Hakodate to Matsumae, and then to 
Esashi.  

In December 1869 ( Meiji 1 ), after the occupation of Esashi, the 
battleship Kaiyou ran aground and sank.  It was due to a strong wind, 
The wind is called Taba-kaze ( Taba wind ).  It's a local wind that 
blows into Hiyama sub-prefecture during the winter.

The commander of Tokugawa Shogunate forces, Toshizou Hijikata, thought 
of the sunken ship, cried, and hit the trunk of a pine tree.  Later, a 
not appeared on the pine tree.  The not is called ``Toshizou's fist 
( Toshizou-no-kobushi ).''

The pine tree is called the Pine of Lament ( Nagueki-no-matsu ).  It is 
located in front of the old administrative office of Hiyama and Nishi 
counties.
 
( For the Boshin War, see Hakodate City in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. )

#10

The local wind Taba-kaze sank the battleship Kaiyou.  It blows from the 
northwest in winter.  Japanese fishermen feared the wind.  They 
believed that the wind had a soul.  So, they called the wind Tama-kaze 
( soul wind ).  The pronunciation changed from Tama-kaze to Taba-kaze.

Houses in Esashi were surrounded by fences in winter.  The fences were 
made of tree branches.  The sound of the wind hitting the fences was 
called Mogari-bue ( Mogari, whistle ).  ( The word ``mogari'' means 
a fence made of tree branches or bamboo. )  That was the soundscape of 
Esashi in winter.  

Today, windmills stand on the mountains.  They are managed by Esashi 
Wind Power, a subsidiary of J-Power ( Electric Power Development 
Company ).

These are local winds in Hokkaidou Island ; ( 1 ) Taba-kaze in 
Esashi Town, Hiyama sub-prefecture, ( 2 ) Dashi-kaze in Suttsu Town, 
Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture, ( 3 ) Hidaka-shimo-kaze in Samani 
Town, Hidaka sub-prefecture, 

( continue ) --> ( 4 ) Shikata in Erimo Town, Hidaka sub-prefecture, 
( 5 ) Aikaze in Ishikari City, Ishikari sub-prefecture, and ( 6 ) 
Bafun-kaze in the Tokachi Plain, Tokachi general sub-prefecture and 
other places.

In the past, due to a strong wind in early spring, 60 herring fishing 
boats sank.  Also, a novel depicts a bear wind ( Kuma-kaze ), which 
blew when a man-eating bear was killed.

( For shipwreck of herring fishing boat, see Haboro Town in Rumoi sub-
  prefecture. ) 

( For man-eating bear, see Tomamae Town in Rumoi sub-prefecture. )

Long ago, an elderly Ainu woman lived near the Port of Esashi.  Her 
name was Orii.  She was a prophet.  She predicted weather and wind.

Orii also mentioned about the size of fishing net for herring.  The 
size was 5-shaku 3-sun ( 160 centimeters ) in height.  The number of 
meshes was 63.  It was a small, coarse net.

During the Edo era, schools of herring came every year, but the catch 
was declining.  So, some fishers moved to the north.  After the Meiji 
period ( after 1868 ), there were years when schools of herring didn't 
appear. 

As for the size of the net, which Orii mentioned, the average height of 
a man was about 5-shaku ( 152 centimeters ) in the Edo era.  Japanese 
people said, ``Live on your size.''  ( English people said, ``bloom 
where you are planted.'' )
 
#11

As for old buildings in Esashi, the former Nakamura's residence has 
been designated as National Important Cultural Property ( Juuyou-bunka-
zai ).  The residence was built from the end of the Edo era to the 
Meiji period  ( more than 150 years ago ).  The owner was a merchant 
from Oumi ( now Shiga Prefecture ).
 
The Nakamura's residence was a shipping wholesaler.  Buildings in the 
residence are connected by passages.  The architecture is called 
Shipping Wholesaler Architecture ( Kaisen-donya-kenchiku ). 

In the past, along the Port of Esashi, shipping wholesalers were lined 
up, including the Nakamura's residence.  The wholesalers spanned piers 
on the water and transported cargo to ships.

The ships owned by Oumi merchants were called Kitamae-bune ( North-
bound ships ).  The hometown of Oumi merchants was what are now 
Higashi-oumi City and Oumi-hachiman City in Shiga Prefecture.  Esashi 
Town and Higashi-oumi City are sister cities. 
 
North-bound ship was also called ``sengoku'' ship.  The word ``sen-
goku'' means 1,000-koku in Japanese.  ``Koku'' is a unit of volume. 
One ``koku'' equals 150 kg in the case of rice.  So, the ship carried 
150 tons of rice.  ( Larger ships than this size were prohibited by the 
Tokugawa Shognate. )

( In Japanese ; ``Sengoku ship'' is ``sengoku-bune.'' )

( For Oumi merchant, see Matsumae Town in Oshima sub-prefecture ; 
  Iwanai Town in Shiribeshi sub-prefecture. )

One ``koku'' of rice was worth about one ``ryou'' of money in the mid 
Edo era.  So, the sales of one voyage per north-bound ship ( 1,000-koku 
ship ) amounted to 1,000-ryou of money.  One ``ryou'' of money was 
equivalent to about 50,000 yen today. 

( UTF code ; ``kaisen-donya-kenchiku'' = 
  \u56DE\u8239\u554F\u5C4B\u5EFA\u7BC9. )

( For one ``koku'' ( unit ) of herring, see Otobe Town in Hiyama 
  sub-prefecture. )

( For one ``koku'' ( unit ) of rice wine, see Kuriyama Town in Sorachi 
  general sub-prefecture. ) 

( For unit conversion, see Nihei lab 2004.  ``Unit conversion.''  
  HTTP://2242.MOOO.COM/UNIT.TXT )

( Single of the chapter ; ``Hourou-kamome ( Wandering Seagull )'' by 
  Spitz.  --> http://youtu.be/gV-F23mqKhU )

|                  |
|      ASSABU      |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 14  |
 ------------------
AREA 460 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 378, CULTIVATED 35 ; COUNTY = HIYAMA ; 
POP 5,755 ( 1990 ), 4,409 ( 2010 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 8.1 C ( AV 1981-
2010 ), PRECIPI 1,347 MM, SNOW 618 CM ; COUNTRY SIGN = MAY QUEEN.
 
LOCATION

Assabu ( a-ssa-bu ) is a town in Hiyama sub-prefecture, south Hokkaidou 
( ``dou-nan'' ).  The area of Assaabu seems to face the sea, but it's 
an inland town.  The area corresponds to the basin of the Assabu River, 
which flows to the Sea of Japan.  But, the mouth of the river is 
included in Esashi Town.

( UTF code ; Assabu = \u539A\u6CA2\u90E8. )
 
As for vegetation, Assabu is the northern limit of ``hiba'' trees 
( ``hinoki-asunaro'' trees ), which are a kind of cypress 
( sa'i-pres ).  Assabu is also the southern limit of ``todomatsu trees, 
which are a kind of fir.

( For ``hiba'' tree, see Hiyama sub-prefecture. )

NAME 

The name ``Assabu'' is derived from ``hacam-pet'' in Ainu language.  It 
means ``starling, river.''  It signified the Assabu River.  The 
etymology is the same as Hassamu in Nishi Ward, Sapporo City, Ishikari 
sub-prefecture.  #1

HISTORY 

In the Edo era, after the 1670s, the Matsumae Domain cut cypress trees.
Woodcutters from the main island ( now Honshuu Island ) migrated.  In 
the beginning of the Meiji period, in 1868 ( Meiji 1 ), the Matsumae 
Domain built Tate Castle.  The location was in the upper reaches of the 
Assabu River.  The castle was used during the Boshin War ( 1868-1869 ). 
#2  #3  

In 1876 ( Meiji 9 ), the administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) of 
Gamushi village was established.  It was a continuation of village 
chief ( ``kochou'' ) system from the Edo era.  In 1879 ( Meiji 12 ), 
the administrative office of Gamushi village was re-established.  It 
covered four villages ; Akanuma, Gamushi, Mena, and Tsuchihashi.  #4

( UTF code ; Akanuma = \u8D64\u6CBC.  Gamushi = \u4FC4\u866B.  Mena = 
  \u76EE\u540D.  Tsuchihashi = \u571F\u6A4B. )

In 1800 ( Meiji 13 ), the administrative office of Gamushi village 
covered five villages ; Akanuma, An-noro.  Gamushi, Mena, and 
Tsuchihashi.  These five villages had existed in the Edo era for 
forestry. 

( UTF code ; An-noro = \u5B89\u91CE\u5442. )

In 1801 ( Meiji 14 ), the administrative office of Gamush village 
covered seven villages ; Gamushi, Tsuchihashi, Mena, Akanuma, An-noro, 
Tate, and Uzura.  In 1906 ( Meiji 39 ), Assabu Village ( 2nd-class 
municipality ) was established by the merger of the seven villages.  In 
1963 ( Shouwa 38 ), Assabu Town was established.

( UTF code ; Tate = \u9928.  Uzura = \u9D89. )

( For 2nd-class municipality, see the note of Matsumae Town. )

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC 

The town hall of Assabu is located near the confluence of the Assabu 
River and the An-noro River.  The town hall was built at the former 
site of Hiyama Agricultural Experiment Station.

National Route 227 ( Oono-kokudou ) passes the center of Assabu town.  
In the past, the national highway was called the Uzura Mountain Road 
( Uzura-sandou ).  It was one of the routes that the Meiji government 
forces advanced to Hakodate.  ( The word ``uzura'' is Japanese, which 
means quail ( kwe'il ). )

INDUSTRY

Distinctive industries in Assabu are agriculture and forestry. 
STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = beans ( soybeans 484 hectares in 2015, 
adzuki beans 196, other beans 7 ), paddy rice 555, potatoes 489, wheat 
373, buckwheat 171, sugar beets 44, orchards 37 ( grapes 21 ), 

( continue ) --> broccoli 24, pasture 24, cabbages 22, fodder 21, 
barley 17, green onions 7, melons 6, carrots 2, Chinese cabbages 1, 
flowers 1, spinach 1, yams 1, other vegetables 346+X ( including 
pumpkins, asparagus, sweet corn ), and other grains 77 ; dairy cows - 
( zero ) in 2015.  #5

May-queen potatoes are a local specialty.  May-queen is a popular 
potato cultivar along with ``danshaku'' ( baron ).  The country sign 
( gateway sign ) of Assabu is a May-queen boy or girl whose face is 
long and smooth.  #6
 
NOTE
   
#1

The name ``Assabu'' originates from an Ainu word meaning ``starling.'' 
Starling is called Muku-dori in Japanese.  The word ``muku-dori'' means 
``Muku tree, bird.''  Muku tree is a kind of deciduous broad-leaved 
tree.  ( Stalings in Japan are white-cheeked starlings or grey 
starlings. ) 

In Hokkaidou Island, starling is also called Sakura-dori, which means 
``cherry tree, bird.''  The bird migrates from the south during the 
cherry blossom season.

When I lived in Tsukuba City, Ibaraki Prefecture, a huge flock of 
starlings flew across the twilight sky.  I watched it from the balcony.
The sky was filled with birds. 

( For cherry blossom, see Matsumae Town in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. )

#2

Cypress trees in Hokkaidou Island are ``hiba.''  Cypress trees in 
Honshu Island are ``hinoki'' and ``hiba.''  Both are conifer evergreen 
conifers. 

( For cypress tree, see Hiyama sub-prefecture. )

In the past, tree felling started in early winter.  The forest floor 
was covered with snow, providing good visibility.  Woodcutters could 
move smoothly in winter.  ( In summer, bamboo grass and grass are 
plentiful. )

Woodcutters used sleighs to haul logs to the river.  In spring, melting 
snow flooded the river.  Woodcutters floated the logs down the river.

( For stone transportation in winter, see the note of Naie Town in 
  Sorachi general sub-prefecture. )

The woodcutter in Assabu brought Shishi-odori dance ( Dear dance ) from 
their homeland.  The dance became an annual event at Hiyama guardhouse.
( Guardhouses in Ezo ( now Hokkaidou ) were called ``ban-sho'' in 
Japanese.  Those were run by the Matsumae Domain. )
	
A large forest fire occurred in 1695.  Subsequently, the Matsumae 
Domain banned logging from 1770 to 1807 to prevent deforestation.  
Again, a large forest fire occurred in 1876.

Very few natural forests remain.  Today, tourists see huge forests in 
Hokkaidou and think they are natural, however, those were felled and 
burned in the past.

In Assabu Town, a natural forest has designated as National Natural 
Monument ( Tennen-kinen-butsu ) since 1928.  It's ``Uzura-gawa-goyou-
matsu-jisei-hokugen-chitai ( Northern Limit of White Pine Natural 
Growing Area along the Uzura River ).''

( For another natural forest, see Esashi Town in Hiyama sub-
  prefecture. )

As for snowy mountains, a female member of Hokkaidou University 
Mountaineering Club said, ``When it snows, we can go into mountains 
that we cannot reach in summer.  In summer, bamboo grass ( `sasa' ) 
and dwarf pine trees ( `haimatsu' ) block the way.''  Mosquitoes and 
bears also appear in summer. 
 
As for sleigh and sled, in Sapporo City, mothers move their children 
using plastic sleds.  People have used sleigh and sled since antiquity
( anti'-quity ). 

( For sleigh, see Naie Town in Sorach general sub-prefecture. )
  
#3

In 1868 ( Meiji 1 ), during the Boshin War, the Matsumae Domain built 
Tate Castle.  The location was inland to avoid naval artillery.  
Matsumae Castle ( Fukuyama Castle ) was located along the coast. 

( For the same reason, the capital of the Choushuu Domain moved from 
  Hagui to Yamaguchi.  Hagui is located along the Sea of Japan and 
  Yamaguchi is inland.  ( Yamaguchi is now the capital of Yamaguchi 
  Prefecture. )  The relocation took place in 1863.  The Tokugawa 
  Shogunate ended in 1867. )
 
In December 1868 ( Meiji 1, solar calendar ), the former Tokugawa 
Shogunate forces advanced to Matsumae Castle.  The Matsumae Domain's 
forces escaped to Tate Castle.  

( It was November by the lunisolar calendar ( ``taiin-taiyou-reki'' ) 
  used at the time.  It was December in the solar calendar ( ``taiyou-
  reki'' ) currently in use.  The lunisolar calendar was used until 
  1872 ( Meiji 5 ) in Japan. ) 
 
In Tate Castle, Choujun Mikami fought using a cutting board as a 
shield.  He was a monk soldier ( ``sou-hei'' ) of the Matsumae Domain.  
He was killed to allow his allies ( a'laiz ) to escape.  Tate Castle 
was captured by the former Tokugawa Shogunate forces and burned. 

The lord of the Matsumae Domain managed to find a ship in Kumaishi.  He 
and his family set sail to the rough winter sea.  They headed for 
Hirosaki.  Then, the Matsumae Domain's forces surrendered.  ( Kumaishi 
is located in Yakumo Town. )

In May 1869 ( Meiji 2, solar calendar ), the Meiji government forces 
landed near the mouth of the Himekawa River.  They advanced south and 
occupied Esashi.  ( The Himekawa River is located in Otobe Town. )

In June 1869, the Meiji government forces advanced to Hakodate and 
ended the Boshin War.  ( The month was May by the lunisolar calendar 
used at the time. )

( For the Boshin War, see Hakodate City in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. )

After the Boshin War, the Matsumae Domain was renamed the Tate Domain. 
In August 1871 ( Meiji 4, solar calendar ), all domains in Japan were 
abolished.  Prefectures were established.  Tate Prefecture was 
established in the south of Hokkaidou.  The other regions were governed 
by the Hokkaidou Development Commission ( Kaitaku-shi ). 

In October 1871 ( solar calendar ), Aomori Prefecture merged Tate 
Prefecture.  In 1872 ( Meiji 5 ), the Hokkaidou Development Commission 
governed the whole Hokkaidou.  At this, Meiji was a turbulent period.  

* * *

During the Edo era, which preceded ( pre-se'e-ded ) the Meiji period, a 
long period free from war continued.  Japan was left behind from 
Western countries in terms of military.  Western countries experienced 
major battles such as Trafalgar and Waterloo.

The Meiji period was turbulent.  Japanese people imported many things 
from Western countries.  New things emerged in society, such as new 
industries, new schools, new religions, new calendars, and new 
municipalities.  Old things were abandoned.

( For new religion, see Hokkaidou University in Kita Ward, Sapporo 
  City, Ishikari sub-prefecture. )
  
#4

As for the name ``Gamushi,'' there is Gamushi Hot Spring along the 
tributary of the An-noro River.  The name ``Gamushi'' comes from 
``kamuy-us-i'' in Ainu language.  It means ``god, exist, place.''  The 
word ``god'' means bear.  Ainu people considered bears to be gods.

( For bear, see Noboribetsu City in Iburi general sub-prefecture. ) 
   
#5
 
Local specialties of Assabu Town are ``shining black soybeans ( Hikari-
kuro-daizu )'' and ``black-bean pudding ( Kuro-mame-purin ).'' 

When I did field survey in Hokkaidou, I heard that black soybeans are 
well-suited for machine harvesting, because the black color conceals 
the stains that splatters onto the beans when mechanical harvesters cut 
the stems.

( continue ) --> That was around the time when combine harvesters were 
introduced to soybean cultivation.  ( by T. Nihei 2007.  The regional 
bases carrying the upland farming of Tokachi, Hokkaidou.  ``Jinbun-
chirigaku-kenkyuu'' http://www.researchgate.net/publication/388527604 )

#6

The potato cultivar ``May-queen'' was bred in 1900 by the British seed 
company Sutton & Sons.  May-queen was named after the queen who was 
chosen at the May Day festival.  May-queen was imported to Japan from 
the USA during the Taishou period ( 1912-1925 ).  

In 1928 ( Shouwa 3 ), Hiyama Agricultural Experiment Station designated 
May-queen and ``danshaku'' as recommended potato cultivars.  Then, the 
two cultivars spread among farmers.  The agricultural experiment 
station was located in Gamushi district, Assabu Village.  The word 
``danshaku'' means ``baron.''  

( For ``danshaku'' potato, see Imakane Town in Hiyama sub-prefecture ; 
  Kucchan Town in Shiribeshi sub-prefecture. )

May-queen is a popular ingredient in Oden in the Kansai region.  ( Oden 
is winter pot dish.  Kansai is located in the central part of Honshuu 
Island.  Kansai is considered as ``western Japan ( nishi-nihon ).'' )  

May-queen potatoes don't easily fall apart when they are soaked in hot 
soup for a long time.  So, May-queen is suitable for Oden.  ( I grew up 
in eastern Japan ( ``higashi-nihon'' ) and have never seen potatoes in 
Oden. ) 

Sapporo Shusei is a distilling company.  The head office is located in 
Nishi Ward, Sapporo City, Ishikari sub-prefecture.  The main factory of 
the company is located along the Assabu River.  The main product of the 
company is Sapporo Soft ( spirit ( ``shouchuu'' ) ).

Sapporo Shusei in Assabu Town produces the spirit ( ``shouchuu'' ) that 
is distilled from potatoes and sweet potatoes.  Those potatoes are 
cultivated in south Hokkaidou.  Next to the distilling factory is a 
potato shipping center run by agricultural cooperative. 

( For spirit ( ``shouchuu'' ), see Nishi Ward in Sapporo City, Ishikari 
  sub-prefecture. )

Until I learned about the distilling company, I didn't know that sweet 
potatoes were cultivated in Hokkaidou as a commercial crop.  ( Sweet 
potatoes are cultivated in Sapporo City for agri-tourism. ) 

Sweet potatoes grow under a warm climate.  The seed potatoes are 
sensitive to low temperatures.  In my hometown ( north Kantou region ), 
the seed potatoes were preserved in deep holes during the winter.  The 
hole was about the height of a man.  

( For sweet potato cultivation, see T. Nihei et al. 2000.  Regional 
  characteristics of production and distribution of sweet potatoes in 
  Hitachi-naka City. ``Chiiki-chousa-houkoku'' http://
  www.researchgate.net/publication/386245461 )

( Single of the chapter ; ``Light My Fire'' by Doors.  --> 
  http://youtu.be/jKU74Uns9_0 )
