|                  |
|   KUROMATSUNAI   |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 20  |
 ------------------
AREA 345 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 301, CULTIVATED 18 ; COUNTY = SUTTSU ; 
POP 3,927 ( 1990 ), 3,250 ( 2010 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 7.4 C ( AV 1981-
2010 ), PRECIPI 1,462 MM, SNOW 966 CM ( AV 1982-2010 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = 
BEECH & WOODPECKER.  

LOCATION  
 
Kuromatsunai ( ku-ro-ma-tsu-na-i ) is a town in Shiribeshi general sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Kuromatsunai is located 
at the root of the Oshima Peninsula. 

( UTF code ; Kuromatsunai = \u9ED2\u677E\u5185. )

The area seems to reach the sea, but it's an inland town.  The location 
is the middle to the upper reaches of the Shubuto River, which flows to 
the Sea of Japan.  Forests occupy 87 percent of the area.  The basin is 
called the Kuromatsunai Basin.  The northern limit of beech forest is 
located in Kuromatsunai Town.  #1  #2 
 
NAME  

The name ``Kuromatsunai'' comes from ``kur-mat-nay'' in Ainu language.  
It means ``person ( Japanese ), women, river.''  The etymology is 
similar to that of Matsumae Town in Oshima general sub-prefecture. 

In tha Edo era,  Japanese women crossed to Ezo Island ( now Hokkaidou 
Island ) following Japanese fishermen.  The ship carrying the women was 
wrecked, and they stayed in Kuromatsunai.  ( ``History Of 
Kuromatsunai'' by Kuromatsunai Tourist Association.  http://
www.bunasatotourism.com/history  last accessed in August 2026. )

In the Edo era, villages where Japanese women lived were unusual in Ezo 
Island.  Women weren't allowed to travel east from the Shakotan 
Peninsula.  #3 

( For the ban on traveling east, see Suttsu Town in Shiribeshi general 
  sub-prefecture. )  

HISTORY 

In the Edo era, Kuromatsunai Mountain Road ( Kuromatsunai-sandou ) 
opened.  It connected Utasutsu along the Sea of Japan and Oshamanbe 
along Uchiura Bay ( Pacific Ocean ).  #4
  
( For Utasutsu ( place ), see Suttsu Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

In the Meiji period, in 1871 ( Meiji 3 ), settlers came from Date-
monbetsu.  ( Date-monbetsu is located in Date City, Iburi general sub-
prefecture. )  They were former samurai in the Sendai Domain, Miyagui 
Prefecture.  ( Samurai are feudal warriors. )
 
In the same year, settlers also came from the Tonami Domain in Aomori 
Prefecture.  They were former samurai in the Aizu Domain, Fukushima 
Prefecture.  They at first moved to the Tonami Domain in the beggining 
of the Meiji period.  ( Samurai lost jobs in the Meiji period. )

( UTF code ; Tonami = \u6597\u5357. ) 
 
In 1879 ( Meiji 12 ), administrative offices ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) were 
established in Kuromatsunai village and Tarukishi village.  In 1897 
( Meiji 30 ), Suttsu sub-prefecture was established.  It covered  
counties of Isoya, Shimamaki, Suttsu, and Utasutsu.  Suttsu county 
included Kuromatsunai and Tarukishi villages.

( UTF code ; Tarukishi = \u6A3D\u5CB8. )

In 1900 ( Meiji 33 ), an administrative office was established for 
Neppu and Sakkai villages.  In 1903 ( Meiji 36 ), Kuromatsunai Station 
on the Hokkaidou Railway opened ( now Hakodate Main Line ).  At 
the time, the Hokkaidou Raiway was called Kan-son-tetsudou, which 
meant ``Hakodate And Otaru Railway.''  #5

( UTF code ; Kanson-tetsudou = \u51FD\u6A3D\u9244\u9053.  Neppu = 
  \u71B1\u90DB.  Sakkai = \u4F5C\u958B. )
 
In 1907 ( Meiji 40 ), Kuromatsunai Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) 
was established.  In 1915 ( Taishou 4 ), Neppu Village ( 2nd-class 
municipality ) was established by the merger of Neppu village and 
Sakkai village.  

( For 2nd-class municipality, see the note of Matsumae Town. )

In 1910 ( Meiji 43 ), Suttsu sub-prefecture was merged to Shiribeshi 
sub-prefecture.  In 1923 ( Taishou 12 ), Tarukishi Village ( 2nd-class 
municipality ) was established.  

In 1955 ( Shouwa 30 ), Miwa Village was established by the merger of 
Kuromatsunai Village, Neppu Village, and Tarukishi Village.  The name 
``Miwa'' means ``three harmony'' in Japanese.  The naming is like 
Kyouwa Town in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture.  

( UTF code ; Miwa = \u4E09\u548C. ) 

In January 1959 ( Shouwa 34 ), Miwa Town was established.  In May 1959, 
the name was changed to Kuromatsunai.  There were many requests for the 
restoration of the old name, Kuromatsunai.  

Governmental offices and facilities bearing the name Kuromatsunai 
included the forestry office, the post office, schools, and the 
railway station ( Japanese National Railways ).  Kuromatsunai forestry 
office ( Kuromatsunai-eirinsho ) was the largest one.

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC  
 
The town hall of Kuromatsunai is located near the confluence of the 
Kuromatsunai River and the Neppu River.  The Hakodate Main Line passes 
near the town hall.  In the past, the Suttsu Railway connected 
Kuromatsunai Station and Suttsu Station along the Sea of Japan. 
 
National Route 5 passes the outskirts of the town center.  The 
Hokkaidou Expressway ( Dou-ou-dou ) passes through the mountains.  #6
 
INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industries in Kuromatsunai are animal husbandry and 
sightseeing.   STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE =  pasture 1,184 hectares in 
2015, beans ( soybeans 90, adzuki beans 44, other beans 3 ), buckwheat 
124, fodder 108, wheat 88, potatoes 77, paddy rice 45, carrots 1, 

( continued ) --> orchards 1, and other vegetables 16+X ; dairy cows 
1,247 head in 2015, beef cattle X ( concealed ), and pigs and sows - 
( zero in the census, but one company runs pig farms ).  #7
 
Kuromatsunai's mineral water is sold at Seicomart and Hokkaidou 
University Coop.  ( Seicomart is a convenience store chain in 
Hokkaidou. )  The water accumulates through shell fossil strata.  
#8  #9

NOTE 
 
#1

The Kuromatsunai Plain has a valley floor divide.  It divides the Sea 
of Japan side and the Pacific Ocean side.  The elevation of the divide 
is 90 meters.  ( Map --> http://
maps.gsi.go.jp/#14/42.631685/140.313606/ )

( In Japanese ; ``Valley floor divide'' is ``kokuchuu-bunsui-kai.'' )

( UTF code ; ``kokucyuu-bunsui-kai'' = 
  \u8C37\u4E2D\u5206\u6C34\u754C. )

The town hall of Kuromatsunai is located four kilometers north of the 
divide.  The elevation is 25 meters.  In the past, the Kuromatsunai 
Plain was a strait ( a narrow channel of the sea ).  The strait
connected Suttsu Bay and Uchiura Bay.  

The strata of the Kuromatsunai Plain were created between 1.2 million 
to 600,000 years ago.  Its geological chronology is Quaternary.  The 
strata contain shellfish fossils.  

( In Japanese ; ``Quaternary' is Dai-yon-ki. )

( For geological chronology, see Nihei lab 2024-2026_e. )

#2 

The beech forests lined between the Kuromatsunai Basin and the 
Tsukikoshi Mountains in Suttsu Town mark the species' northern limit.
This was reported by ``Empire Of Japan Forest Report ( Dainippon-
sanrin-kaihou )'' published in 1897 ( Meiji 30 ).  

From the Edo era to the Meiji period, beech trees were cut for boiling 
herring.  Professor Yoshi-nao Niijima ( 1871-1943 ), who was the frist 
professor of Department of Forestry, Sapporo Agricultural College ( now 
Hokkaidou University ), advocated to protect the northernmost beech 
forests. 

In 1928 ( Shouwa 3 ), a beech forest in the Utasai district of 
Kuromatsunai Village was designated as National Natural Monument 
( Tennen-kinen-butsu ).  It was named ``Utasai Northernmost Natural 
Beech Habitat Area ( Utasai-buna-jisei-hokuguen-chitai ).'' 

( UTF code ; Utasai = \u6B4C\u624D. )

The northern limit of beech forests is moving north.  In 2013, a beech 
forest was discovered in the Niseko Mountains of Rankoshi Town.  ( by 
``150 Years Of The Northernmost Beech Forest'' http://
www.hk-curators.jp/archives/3754  last accessed in August 2026. ) 

On the campus of Hokkaidou University, there is an artificial beech 
forest.  The trunk of beech is smooth and silver.  It's like a leg of 
an elephant.

* * *

On Mt. Takao ( 599 meters ) in Toukyou Prefecture, beech trees and blue 
beech trees grow near the top of the mountain.  Blue beech trees have 
spotted and silver bark ( by Nihei lab 2024-2025a ).  Mt. Wagakuni 
( 518 meters ) is located in my hometown in Ibaraki Prefecture.  It has 
beech trees at the top of the mountain ( by Nihei 2018 ).  

( In Japapnese ; ``Beech'' is ``buna.''  ``Blue beech'' is 
  ``inu-buna.'' )

Mt. Takao is located at 35 degrees north latitude.  Mt. Wagakuni is 
located at 36 degrees north latitude.  Utasai Northernmost Natural 
Beech Habitat Area is located at 42 degrees north latitude.  Its 
altitude is 70 meters.  ( Map --> http://
maps.gsi.go.jp/#14/42.647502/140.336394 )  

The southern limit of beech forests is located around the top of Mt. 
Takakuma-san, Kagoshima Prefecture.  The altitude of the mountain is 
1,145 meters.  Leaves of beech trees are larger in the north.  It 
reminds me of Bergmann's rule. 

( UTF code ; Takakuma-san = \u9AD8\u9688\u5C71. )

In the past, beech wood was considered to be of low quality.  It wasn't 
used as lumbers because it would warp after processing.  As for 
traditional lacquerware, high-quality items were made from Zelkova 
wood.  Common ones were made from beech wood.  Beech is softer than 
Zelkova. 

( In Japanese ; ``Lacquerware'' is ``shikki.''  ``Zelkova'' is 
  ``keyaki.'' )

After the Second World War, many beech forests were changed to cedar 
forests and larch forests.  Cedars and larches were cash trees.  After 
the high economic growth period, the importance of beech trees in the 
natural environment was recognized.  

( In Japanese ; ``Cedar'' is ``sugui.''  ``Larch'' is ``karamatsu.'' )

( For cedar tree in Honshuu Island, see Nihei lab 2023-2026b. )

( During the high economic growth period, which continued from the 
  late 1960s to the early 1970s, the Ministry of Environment was a 
  division that treated air pollution and water pollution. ) 
 
One large beech tree can irrigate 1-tan ( 0.1 hectares ) of rice paddy  
( by Takeo Ichikawa et al. 1984. ``Beech Belt Culture Of Japan ( Nihon-
no-buna-tai-bunka )'' published by Asakura-shoten, ISBN 4254163428 ).  
The soft and water-rich beech trees became a symbol of the modern 
environmental protection activities. 

In 1993, beech forests in the Shirakami Mountains were selected as a 
World Heritage Site.  It was the first world heritage in the country, 
along with cedar forests in Yakushima Island.  The Shirakami Mountains 
are located at the border with Aomori Prefecture and Akita Prefecture.  
The word ``border'' is the antonyms of ``center.''

( For beech tree, see Shimamaki Village in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. ) 
 
The Beech Belt Culture ( Buna-tai-bunka ) represents gathering and 
hunting in northern Japan.  Meanwhile, the Evergreen Broad-leaved 
Forest Culture ( ``Shouyou-jurin-bunka'' ) represents paddy rice 
cultivation in western Japan.   

In Germany, beech trees were planted.  Beech acorns were used as feed 
for pigs.  Professor Hiroshi Sasaki wrote about ``the dignity of the 
German foresters ( Gueruman-shinrin-kan-no-iguen ).''  ( by H. Sasaki 
2017.  ``Gegrapher Hiroshi Sasaki'' published by Book-com.  ARCHIVE --> 
HTTP://2242.MOOO.COM/PAPER/707.PDF ) 

( For beech acorn, see Nanae Town in Oshima general sub-prefecture. )
  
#3

The names Kuromatsunai and Matsumae are associated with the Ainu 
language that means ``Japanese, women, live.''  ( Kuromatsunai Town is 
located in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture.  Matsumae Town is located 
in Oshima general sub-prefecture. )  

These names signified that the places belonged to Japanese people.  
They could reproduce there.  ( In Ezo Island ( now Hokkaidou Island ), 
most Japanese people were seasonal fishermen. )  

In Honshuu Island, ``sakaki'' trees are displayed on god alters 
( ``kamidana'' ).  ``Sakaki'' is a kind of forever-green broad-leaved 
tree.  A place where ``sakaki'' trees grew signified ``the place they 
can reproduce.'' ( by Nihei lab 2023-2026_t ).  

Reproduction is expressed as ``give birth'' in English and ``give light 
( dar a luz )'' in Portuguese.  People need light and freedom.  
 
A Japanese baby has a blue mark on the buttocks.  The birthmark is 
called Mongolian spot.  I know a Japanese woman who was born in 
Germany.  She said, ``When I was born, doctors and nurses at the 
hospital came around me to see the blue mark.''

#4  

In 1821, during the Edo era, Ootori Shrine was built in Kuromatsunai.  
It was the beginning of a Japanese village.  In 1833, Mr. Rizaemon 
Hanaoka opened Hanaoka inn.  He came from the Matsumae Domain in the 
south.  Before Hanaoka inn, there was a lodge run by Ainu people.  ( by 
``Fishing And Journy In Douhoku'' http://
www.kitakaido.com/douow/tavi-10.html  last accessed in August 2026. )
 
In 1856, Kuromatsunai Mountain Road opened.  It connected the Pacific 
Ocean side and the Sea of Japan side.  Hanaoka inn became a post 
station on the road.  The post station continued after the Meiji 
period.

( In Japanese ; ``Post station'' in the Edo era is ``shuku.''  ``Post 
  station'' in the Meiji period is ``ekitei.'' )

#5 
 
There are two train stations in Kuromatsunai Town.  They are 
Kuromatsunai and Neppu on the Hakodate Main Line.  The name ``Neppu'' 
comes from ``kunne-net-pet'' in Ainu language.  It means ``black, 
driftwood, river.''  The two Kanji characters for ``Kuro-matsu'' also 
mean ``black, pine tree.''  Who chose the Kanji characters?

On the map published by Takeshirou Matsuura in 1859, during the 
Edo era ( http://koukita.github.io/touzaiezo/map/ ), this place is 
written in seven Katakana characters, ``ku-ro-ma-to-ma-na-i.''  There
were no Kanji characters for the place, as it didn't belonged to 
Japanese people.

#6 

Kuromatsunai Roadside Station is located along National Route 5.  The 
location is close to Kuromatsunai Interchange.  The shops sell pizza 
and breads.  The Margherita is a popular pizza ( not only in the shop 
but also in the world ).  The toppings represent Italian national 
flag ; tomatoes for red, basil for green, and mozzarella for white.

Toit Vert ( to'wa-veeha ) is located in the suburb.  The name ``Toit 
Vert'' means ``green roof'' in French.  They process and sell local 
specialties, such as cheese, ham, bacon, sausage, and ice cream.  The 
building is surrounded by lawns and woods.  It's like a European 
castle.

Near Toit Vert, there is a geological stratum containing shellfish 
fossils.  Professor Yukito Kurihara of the Paleontology Laboratory at 
Mie University excavates there.

#7

As for swine ( pig ) farming in Kuromatsunai Town, Interfarm 
Kuromatsunai raises 1,400 sows for production ( as of 2019 ).  
Interfarm Kuromatsunai is a company of the Nippon Ham Group.  
 
A sow is an adult female hog.  It's called ``boton'' in Japanese, which 
means ``mother, pig.''  A sow gives birth to 20 to 30 piglets at a 
time.  It gives birth four to five times per two year.  The lifespan of 
a sow is four to five years. 

( UTF code ; ``boton'' = \u6BCD\u8C5A. )

According to statistics, the largest pig producing municipality in 
Hokkaidou is Mori Town in Oshima general sub-prefecture.  The Nippon 
Ham Group also operates pig farms there. 

Most Japanese pigs are British breeds.  The cool climate of Hokkaidou 
is suitable for raising pigs.  Under a warm climate, the mortality rate 
of piglets increases.  

Kagoshima Prefecture in Kyuushuu Island is famous for pig farming.  The 
breed ``Kagoshima-kuro-buta,'' which means ``Kagoshima Black Pig,'' is 
the Berkshire.  It was developed in the county of Berkshire, England, 
which is located at 51 degrees north latitude.

( Sapporo City is located at 43 degrees north latitude.  Toukyou 
  ( Shinjuku Ward ) is located at 35 degrees north latitude.  Kagoshima 
  City is located at 31 degrees north latitude. )

( For pig industry, see Toubetsu Town in Ishikari sub-prefecture. ) 

#8 
 
As for water, Kuromatsunai Meisui Co. sells bottled mineral water.  
It's the water that has passed through fossil-bearing strata.  Near 
the water factory, there is Shellfish Bridge ( Kaigara-bashi ). 
 
The hardness of the Kuromatsunai Meisui's water is 105 mg/L.  
( Hardness refers to the weight of CaCo3 ( calcium carbonate ) per 
liter of water. ) 

The water of Kuromatsunai is classified as ``moderately hard.''  
Moderately hard and hard mineral water is unique in the country.  Most 
mineral water is soft water.  Rainwater flows quickly into the sea.  
The water contains very few minerals.

Hot spring water contains many minerals.  Sometimes, cups are placed 
at the hot spring source for people to drink.  The popular hard mineral 
water is Evian, which is imported from France.  

( For soft water, see Kyougoku Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

( For clean river water, see Rankoshi Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

( For natural sparkling water, see Shimamaki Village in Shiribeshi 
  general sub-prefecture. )

#9

Kuromatsunai Town has welfare facilities, such as for child welfare, 
for children with intellectual disabilities, and for persons with 
physically disabilities.  Kyuu Sakamoto ( 1941-1985 ) was the singer 
who supported the facility for child welfare in Kuromatsunai.

( In Japanese ; ``Facility for child welfare'' is ``jidou-yougo-
  shisetsu.''  ``Facility for children intellectual disability'' is 
  ``seishin-hakujakuji-shisetsu.''  ``Facility for persons with 
  physically disabilities'' is ``shintai-shougaisha-shisetu.'' )

( For Kyuu Sakamoto, see Kuriyama Town in Sorachi general sub-
  prefecture. )

( Single of the chapter ; ``Hacelo Por La Vieja ( Do It For Your 
  Mom )'' by Angelis.  --> http://youtu.be/PfTVvs6Uugo )
 __________________
|                  |
|      SUTTSU      |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 21  |
 ------------------
AREA 95 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 86, CULTIVATED 1 ; COUNTY = SUTTSU ; POP 
4,858 ( 1990 ), 3,443 ( 2010 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 8.6 C ( AV 1981-
2010 ), PRECIPI 1,177 MM, SNOW 546 CM ; COUNTRY SIGN = WINDMILL. 
 
LOCATION  

Suttsu ( su-ttsu ) is a town in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture, 
central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Suttsu is a small municipality 
( under 100 square kilometers ).  The area faces the Sea of Japan.  
Forests occupy 91 percent of the area.  #1

( UTF code ; Suttsu = \u5BFF\u90FD. )

The shape of Suttsu is like a butterfly.  The left part consists of 
Suttsu and Tarukishi districts.  The right part consists of Utasutsu 
and Isoya districts.  The butterfly shape was formed in 1955 by 
the merger of four municipalities ( see the HISTORY section below ). 

NAME
 
The name ``Suttsu'' is derived from ``supki-pet'' in Ainu language.  It 
means ``reed grass, river.''  It signified the Shubuto River, which 
empties in Suttsu Bay.  The reed grass called ``supki'' in Ainu 
language is a hard variety that was used for arrow shafts. 
 
HISTORY  
 
During the Edo era, trading places ( ``basho'' ) were established by 
the Matsume Domain.  They were located in Suttsu, Otasutsu, and Isoya. 

During the Kyouhou period ( 1716-1736) of the Edo era, Japanese fishers 
increased, because the herring catches decreased in the south.  ( The 
south is around the area that is now Esashi Town, Hiyama sub-
prefecture. )  #2
  
After the Meiji period, in 1872 ( Meiji 5 ), an administrative office 
( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) were set in three counties.  They were Suttsu, 
Utasutsu, and Isoya counties.  Their administrative offices were a 
continuation of village chief ( ``kochou'' ) system from the Edo era. 

( In Japanese ; ``County'' is ``gun.'' ) 

In 1879 ( Meiji 12 ), an administrative office was established in 
Suttsu county.  It covered Isoya, Utasutsu, and Shimamaki counties.  In 
1897 ( Meiji 30 ), Suttsu sub-prefecture was established.  In 1900 
( Meiji 33 ), Suttsu Town ( 1st-class municipality ) was established.

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Matsumae 
  Town. )  

In 1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Isoya Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1906 ( Meiji 39 ), Utasutsu Village ( 2nd-class 
municipality ) was established.  In 1909 ( Meiji 42 ), Isoya Village 
( 1st-class municipality ) was established. 

( UTF code ; Isoya = \u78ef\u8c37.  Utasutsu = \u6B4C\u68C4. )

In 1910 ( Meiji 43 ), Shiribeshi sub-prefecture merged Suttsu sub-
prefecture.  In 1923 ( Taishou 12 ), Masadomari Village ( 2nd-class 
municipality ) and Tarukishi Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) were 
established.  In 1933 ( Shouwa 8 ), Suttsu Town merged Masadomari 
Village.  #3 

( UTF code ; Masadomari = \u653F\u6CCA.  Tarukishi = \u6A3D\u5CB8. ) 

In 1955 ( Shouwa 30 ), Suttsu Town merged villages of Utasutsu, 
Tarukishi, and Isoya.  Part of Tarukishi Village was merged to Miwa 
Village ( now Kuromatsunai Town ).  Part of Isoya Village was merged to 
Rankoshi Town.  The area is now Minato-machi district in Rankoshi Town.

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC  

The town hall of Suttsu is located near Suttsu fishing port.  The port 
is located in the western part of the butterfly-shaped region.  Route 
229 ( Raiden-kokudou ) runs along the coast.  The name ``Raiden'' 
refers to Cape Raiden, located north from Suttsu. 

( For the name ``Raiden,'' see Iwanai Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )
  
In the past, the Suttsu Railway ran.  It connected Suttsu Station and 
Kuromatsunai Station on the Hakodate Main Line.  It was in operation 
from 1920 to 1968.  Trains transported herring from Suttsu fishing port 
and zinc and lead from Suttsu Mine.  #4

INDUSTRY

Distinctive industries in Suttsu are fishery and sightseeing. 
STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE =  X ( concealed ) for all crops and 
animals in 2015.  STATISTICS OF FISHERIES = salmon 217 tons in 2017, 
atka mackerel 214, squid ( flying squid 145, other squid 8 ), sand 
lance 136, octopus 91, yellowtail 82, Pacific cod 43, sea urchins 36, 

( continued ) --> right-eye flounder 34, olive flounder 25, sea trout 
5, herring 4, mackerel 4, shellfish 4, seaweed 3, shrimp/prawns 3, 
crabs 1, puffer fish 1, other fish 181, and other marine animals 44. 
  
Cultured oysters are sold as ``Kotobuki-kaki.''  The word ``kotobuki''
is another pronunciation for the Kanji character ``su'' in Su-ttsu.  
The harvest season of Kotobuki-kaki is from April to June.  It's 
differs from ordinary oysters.  

( Generally, oysters are harvested in autumn and winter.  The months 
  with ``r'' are for oysters, namely, September, October, November, 
  December, January, February, March, and April. )

( For oyster, see Akkeshi Town in Kushiro sub-prefecture. )

As for sightseeing, two old herring mansions ( ``nishin-goten'' ) are 
preserved.  One belonged to the Hashimoto family, who engaged in 
herring trading and money lending.  The other belonged to the Satou 
family, who ran trading places ( ``basho'' ) for herring fishing.  Both 
herring mansions were built in the 1890s, during the Meiji period.  #5

Suttsu is a windy town.  Windmills stand along Suttsu Bay.  In geology, 
the lowland extending through Suttsu, Kuromatsunai, and Oshamanbe was a 
channel.  The wind ``Dashi-kaze'' blows through the lowland.  #6  #7

( For fossil from the ancient sea, see Kuromatsunai Town in Shiribeshi 
  general sub-prefecture. )  

NOTE  

#1

According to Koeppen's climate classification, the coast of Suttsu 
Town is the oceanic climate ( by K. Kasai & K. Kimura 2013.  Climate 
change analysis of Hokkaidou by mesh data using Koeppen Climate 
Classification.  http://doi.org/10.7886/hgs.88.37 )  ( Koeppen is a 
German surname. )

Beech trees grow in Suttsu Town, so the climate is also called as a 
beech climate.  Suttsu is a transitional zone between humid subtropical 
climate and continental climate.  ( In general, Honshuu Island has a 
humid subtropical climate and Hokkaidou Island a continental climate. ) 

( In Japanese ; ``Beech'' is ``buna.''  ``Continental climate'' is 
  ``tairiku-sei-kikou.''  ``Humid subtropical climate'' is ``ondan-
  shitsujun-kikou. )

( For beech tree, see Kuromatsunai Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

#2 

Suttsu-basho ( a trading place ) was built during the Edo era.  The 
office was located at the present-day Suttsu fishing port.  With the 
establishment of the trading place, an Ainu village was moved from the 
mouth of the Shubuto River to the fishing port. 

( In Ainu language ; ``Ainu village'' is ``kotan.'' )

( In Japanese ; The office of a trading place in the Edo era is 
 ``un-jou-ya.'' )

( UTF code ; ``un-jou-ya'' = \u904B\u4E0A\u5C4B. ) 

Isoya-basho ( a trading place ) was also built during the Edo era.  The 
office was sited near the mouth of the Shiribetsu River.  The location 
was what is now Minato-machi district in Rankoshi Town. 

In 1688, during the Edo era, the Matsumae Domain banned women from 
traveling east from Cape Kamui, which is located on the Shakotan 
Peninsula.  People said, ``A goddess lives on the seabed off Cape 
Kamui.  She gets angry if a boat carries a woman and overturns it.''

Suttsu and Isoya were located before the sea route that rounded Cape 
Kamui.  Japanese people who stayed these places increased.  Easahi-
oiwake is a folk song for fishermen in Ezo ( old Hokkaidou ).  These 
are lyrics from Esashi-oiwake.  It has been song since the Edo era. 

``Tsurete-iku-kiwa  Yama-yama-nare-do  Onnawo-tousanu  Bashoga-aru.''
  ( My desire to take you with me is as unshakable as a mountain, but 
    there is a place where women are not permitted to pass. )
 
``Shinoro-takashima  Oyobimo-naiga  Semete-utasutsu-isoya-made.''
  ( I cannot take you to Shinoro and Takashima ( now Otaru City ), but 
    I want to take you at least Utasutsu and Isoya ( now Suttsu Town 
    and Rankoshi Town ). 

Many shipwrecks occurred near Cape Kamui on the Shakotan Peninsula. 
There is Sai-no-kawara Coast on the paninusla.  The pronunciation 
``sai-no-kawara'' in Japanese means a shore between this world and 
afterlife.  Sunken ships washed up on Sai-no-kawara Coast.  Fishermen 
have slept with the goddess on the seabed. 

( For herring fishing, see Yoichi Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

( For Sai-no-kawara Coast, see Kamoenai Village in Shiribeshi general 
  sub-prefecture. )

( For the legend of the woman on the seabed, see Shakotan Town in 
  Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )
 
The ban on women traveling to the east of the peninsula was lifted 
around 1856, during the Ansei period of the Edo era.  ( The Edo period 
continued from 1603 to 1868. ) 
  
#3
 
As for the old municipalities in Suttu Town, namely, Isoya, Masadomari, 
Tarukishi, and Utasutu, the name ``Masadomari'' comes from ``mata-
tomari'' in Ainu language.  It means ``winter, port.''  The Ainu word 
``tomari'' was derived from the Japanese word ``tomari,'' which means a 
natural small port. 

( For the name ``Isoya,'' see Rankoshi Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

( For the name ``tomari,'' see Tomari Village in Shiribeshi general 
  sub-prefecture. )

The name ``Tarukishi'' comes from ``taor-kes'' in Ainu language.  It 
means ``high cliff, end.''  The name ``Utasutsu'' comes from ``ota-
sut'' in Ainu language.  It means ``sand beach, end.''  It signified a 
sand beach near a rocky coast in Utasutsu district.  The Kanji 
characters for Utasutsu means ``song, abandon.''  

There are three Utasutsu districts in Hokkaidou.  One is in Oshamanbe 
Town, Oshima general sub-prefecture.  One is in Suttsu Town, Shiribeshi 
general sub-prefecture.  One is in Furubira Town in Shiribeshi general 
sub-prefecture. 

#4

Cape Benkei along National Route 229 is a tourist destination.  The 
location is the former Masadomari Village ( now Suttsu Town ).  A 
statue of Benkei stands on Cape Benkei.  Benkei was a monk soldier in 
the 12th century.  He served Yoshitsune Minamoto.  Cape Benkei is one 
of Yoshitsune legends in Hokkaidou.

( For Yoshitsune legend, see Biratori Town in Hidaka sub-prefecture ; 
  Esashi Town and Otobe Town in Hiyama sub-prefecture ; Iwanai Town, 
  Otaru City, Tomari Village, and Shakotan Town in Shiribeshi general 
  sub-prefecture. )

As for the name ``Benkei,'' it may have come from ``perke-i'' in Ainu 
language, which means ``tear, place.''  The terrain of the cape appears 
to be torn apart.

#5
 
As for the Hashimoto family, who were the owners of a herring mansion, 
they also owned three trading ships.  Those were the type of north-
bound ship.

( In Japanese ; ``Herring mansion'' is ``nishin-goten.''  ``North-bound 
  ship'' is ``kitamae-bune.'' )

( For north-bound ship, see Esashi Town in Hiyama sub-prefecture. )

The Hashimoto family came from Yoshizaki in Echizen country ( now Awara 
City in Fukui Prefecture ).  Their herring mansion was made of the wood 
shipped from Echizen. 

They used one Zelkova tree for the main pillar and 78 cypress trees for 
other pillars.  It took three years to collect pillars and four years 
to build.  Not a single nail was used. 

( In Japanese ; ``Cypress'' tree is ``hinoki'' or ``hiba.''  ``Main 
  pillar'' is ``daikoku-bashira.''  ``Zelkova'' is ``keyaki.'' )

As for the Satou family, who were the owners of another herring 
mansion, they also manged two trading places for herring.  One was 
Utasutsu-basho and the other was Isoya-basho.

In the Edo era, Utasutsu and Isoya were ``sengoku-basho.''  The word 
``sengoku-basho'' meant that the trading place boasted 1,000-koku 
catches of herring.  ( One ``koku'' of herring is equivalent to 200 kg 
of fresh herring. ) 

The Satou family's herring mansion don't have rooms for seasonal 
fishermen.  They stayed another building.  Generally, herring mansions 
include rooms for seasonal fishermen.

( For trading place with 1,000-koku catches of herring, see Otobe Town 
  in Hiyama sub-prefecture. )  

( For herring fishing, see Yoichi Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )
 
#6   
  
From the summer to the autumn, a local wind blows.  The wind is called 
``Dashi-kaze.''  The word ``Dashi-kaze'' means ``pushing, wind'' in 
Japanese.  The wind blows from the land to the sea, pushing fishing 
boats out to open water.

In the past, the wind prevented fishing boats from reaching the port, 
and at times, the boats suffered shipwreck.  The situation that a 
fishing boat cannot reach the port is called ``oki-dome'' in Japanese.

Itsukushima Shrine is located at Utasutsu district in Suttsu Town.  At 
the shrine, there is a statue of the god of the wind.  The god holds a 
sickle in its hand.  

In the yard of fisherman's houses in Suttsu Town, there stood a poll 
with a sickle on the top.  It was a talisman to keep out the local 
wind. 

A sickle has the magical power to dodge the wind.  In the past, a 
sickle was stuck on the roof of the pagoda at Houryuuji Temple, Nara 
Prefecture. 

Today, wind farms are lined in the Tsukikoshi Mountains near Suttsu 
Bay.  The massive blades of wind turbines are sickles. 

( For local wind, see Esashi Town in Hiyama sub-prefecture. ) 
 
#7 

Many confectionery shops existed in the center of Suttsu Town.  Many 
seasonal fishermen worked there.  They preferred sweets. 

( Cf.  Many confectionery shops existed in Sunagawa City in Sorachi 
  general sub-prefecture and Obihiro City in Tokachi general sub-
  prefecture.  Sunagawa was a city of coal mining.  Obihiro is a 
  city of field crop farming. )

Wakasa-imo is a souvenir from Hokkaidou.  It's a confection made from 
beans, ``konbu'' kelp, and sugar.  The flavor is like sweet-potato.
  
Four companies produce Wakasa-imo.  They are located in ( 1 ) Suttsu 
Town in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture, ( 2 ) Touyako Town and ( 3 ) 
Noboribetsu City in Iburi general sub-prefecture, and ( 4 ) Sapporo 
City in Ishikari sub-prefecture. 

In Hokkaidou, farmers couldn't cultivate sweet-potatoes, because of a 
cold climate.  So, people mimicked the flavor of sweet-potatoes using 
beans, ``konbu'' kelp, and sugar.  ( Today, Sweet-potatoes are 
cultivated in south Hokkaidou, where the climate is mild.  Also, they
are cultivated in a hobby farm in Sapporo City, Iburi sub-prefecture. )

The birthplaces of Wakasa-imo is Suttsu Town.  The name ``Wakasa'' 
means the family name of the founder, Guen-shich Wakasa.  He came to 
Hokkaidou from Ishikawa Prefecture around 1882, and started to sell
Wakasa-imo in 1909.  ( by ``Mistery Of Wakasa-imo ( Wakasa-imo-no-
nazo )''  http://
minami-siribesi.world.coocan.jp/contents/suttu_wakasaimo.htm  last 
accessed May 2019. )
 
In geography, the name Wakasa refers to Fukui Prefecture.  Both Fukui 
and Ishikawa prefectures belong to the Hokuriku region.  Many migrants 
came to Hokkaidou from the Hokuriku region.  Even today, I met many 
students from the Hokuriku region at Hokkaidou University.

( Single of the chapter ; ``Dreamer'' by Spangle Call Lilli Line.  -->
  http://youtu.be/oGY-ojR_59g )

|                  |
|     RANKOSHI     |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 22  |
 ------------------
AREA 450 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 376, CULTIVATED 35 ; COUNTY = ISOYA ; POP 
6,986 ( 1990 ), 5,292 ( 2010 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 7.6 C ( AV 1981-
2010 ), PRECIPI 1,203 MM, SNOW 813 CM ( AV 1982-2010 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = 
HOT SPRING & WOMAN. 

LOCATION   

Rankoshi ( ra-n-ko-shi ) is a town in Shiribeshi general sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Rankoshi is located in 
the lower reaches of the Shiribetsu River.  The Shiribetsu River is 
the one of the cleanest river in the country.  #1

( UTF code ; Rankoshi = \u862D\u8D8A. )

NAME  
  
The name ``Rankoshi'' is derived from ``ranko-us-i'' in Ainu language.  
It means ```katsura' tree, many, place.''  It signified the Rankoshi 
River, which joins the Shiribetsu River near the town hall.  #2 

In the Edo era, the Matsumae Domain established Isoya-basho ( trading 
place ) at the mouth of the Shiribetsu River.  In the Meiji period, two 
villages were established in Isoya county.  Those were Isoya village 
along the coast and Minami-shiribetsu village inland. 

( UTF code ; Isoya = \u78EF\u8C37.  Minami-shiribetsu = 
  \u5357\u5c3b\u5225. ) 

Around 1882 ( Meiji 15 ), a group of settlers from Ishikawa Prefecture 
settled along the Shiribetsu River.  In 1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Isoya 
Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was established.

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Matsumae 
  Town in Oshima general sub-prefecture. )  

In 1904 ( Meiji 37 ), the Hokkaidou Railway opened ( now Hakodate Main 
Line ).  Stations of Isoya, Rankoshi, and Konbu opened.  ( Isoya 
Station is now Mena Station. )  In 1909 ( Meiji 42 ), Isoya Village 
( 1st-class municipality ) and Minami-shiribetsu Village ( 2nd-class 
municipality ) were established.  #3  #4 
 
In 1914 ( Taishou 3 ), the village hall of Minami-shiribeshi relocated 
from Mena to Rankoshi Station.  The former village hall was located at 
the confluence of the Shiribetsu River and the Mena Rvier.  It was far 
from Mena Station and Rankoshi Station on the Hakodate Main Line.  In 
the past, many people lived in the countryside. 
 
In 1940 ( Shouwa 15 ), Minami-shiribeshi Village was established.  In 
1954 ( Shouwa 29 ), Rankoshi Town was established.  Minami-shiribeshi 
was renamed Rankoshi.  

1955 ( Shouwa 30 ), Rankoshi Town merged Kita-shiribetsu district of 
Isoya Village.  The area corresponds to the present-day Minato-machi 
district.  At the time, other districts of Isoya Village were merged to 
Suttsu Town.  And, Isoya Village along the sea disappeared.  In the 
past, many people lived along the sea. 
 
TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC 

The town hall of Rankoshi is located along the Shiribetsu River.  Route 
5 ( Youtei-kokudou ) and the Hakodate Main Line run through the 
districts of Mena, Rankoshi, and Konbu.  Along the Sea of Japan, 
National Route 229 ( Raiden-kokudou ) runs through Minato-machi 
district. 

INDUSTRY  

Distinctive industry in Rankoshi is agriculture.  STATISTICS OF 
AGRICULTURE = paddy rice 1,875 hectares 2015, buckwheat 560, beans 
( soybeans 209, adzuki beans 54, other beans 5 ), wheat 119, potatoes 
93, pasture 45, melons 17, onions 17, tomatoes 13, sugar beets 10, 

( continue ) --> fodder 6, orchards 3, strawberries 1, other vegetables 
72+X ( pumpkins, asparagus ), and other grains 68 ; dairy cows X 
( concealed ).  STATISTICS OF FISHERIES = octopus 4 tons in 2017 and 
sea urchins 3. 

The specialty of Rankoshi Town is rice.  It's grown with plenty of 
melting water from the Niseko Mountains.  Rankoshi Town is designated 
as a Special Heavy Snowfall Zone.

( In Japanese ; ``Special Heavy Snowfall Zone'' is ``Tokubetsu-
  gousetsu-chitai.'' )

The rice paddies cover 3,000 hectares.  They stretch along the 
Shiribetsu River.  After the rice-acreage reduction policy, wheat and 
soybeans are also grown in rice paddies. 

( In Japanese ; ``Rice-acreage reduction policy'' is ``guentan-
  seisaku.'' )

As for sightseeing, hot springs are located on the foot of the Niseko 
Mountains.  They are Goshiki, Yumoto, and Konbu hot springs.  Konbu Hot 
Spring is located on the border with Niseko Town.  

As for Konbu Hot Spring, ski clubs from Hokkaidou Imperial University 
( now Hokkaidou University ) and Otaru High Commercial School ( now 
Otaru University of Commerce ) had ski camps there.  The period was 
from the the 1910s to the 1950s.

( For pre-war mountain skiing, see Niseko Town in Shiribeshi general 
  sub-prefecture. )  

Along the Sea of Japan, the Shellfish Museum opened in Minato-machi 
district in 1991.  It's located in the former Isoya Village.  Isoya 
is sung about in the fisherman's song Esashi-oiwake.
 
( For the lyrics of Esashi-oiwake, see Suttsu Town in Shiribeshi 
  general sub-prefecture. )
  
NOTE

#1

The Shiribetsu River is a clean river.  Every year, a report ``Current 
State Of Water Quality Of First Class Rivers In The Country'' is 
published by the Ministry of Land, Infrastructure, Transport and 
Tourism.

( In Japanese ; ``Current State Of Water Quality Of First Class Rivers 
  In the Country'' is Zenkoku-ikkyuu-kasen-no-suishitsu-guenkyou. )

According to the report, the Shiribetsu River has been ranked as the 
cleanest river in the country more than ten times.  Other rivers in 
Hokkaidou have also ranked as the cleanest.  They were the rivers of 
the Shiribeshi-toshibetsu, the Mukawa, and the Saru.

( The Shiribeshi-toshibetsu River is located in Hiyama sub-prefecture, 
  The Mukawa River and the Saru River are in Hidaka sup-prefecture. )

( For water, see Kuromatsunai Town in Shiribeshi sub-prefecture. ) 
 
The Shiribetsu River originates near Lake Shikotsu.  It flows around 
the north foot of Mt. Youtei-zan and  the south foot of Mt. Niseko-
annupuri, and empties in the Sea of Japan.  

The Shiribetsu River flows through six municipalities ; the towns of 
Kimobetsu, Kyougoku, Kucchan, Niseko, and Rankoshi in Shiribeshi 
general sub-prefecture, and the former Ootaki Village ( now Date 
City ) in Iburi general sub-prefecture. 
  
( For the name ``Shiribetsu,'' see Siribeshi general sub-prefecture. ) 

In the Shiribetsu River, ``itou'' trout ( Sakhalin taimen ) lives.  It 
was called a phantom fish because its number decreased due to 
development.  Itou is the largest freshwater fish in the country.  It 
lives 20 years.  It grows slowly and largely.  The largest one was 2 
meters.  

Many kinds of fish inhabit in the Shiribetsu River, such as ``itou,'' 
redfin dace ( da'is ), lamprey, and sweetfish.  The river is the 
southern limit of the habitat of ``itou.''  Today, ``itou'' is hatched 
artificially.  Sweetfish is a typical food fish in Honshuu Island.

( In Japanese ; ``Lamprey'' is ``yatsume-unagui.''  ``Redfin dace'' is 
  ``ugu-i.''  ``Sweetfish'' is ``ayu.'' )

( For ``itou'' ( fish ), see Niki Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

#2  
 
As for the name ``Ranko-shi,'' ``ranko'' means ``katsura'' tree in Ainu 
language.  ``Katsura'' trees are planted in parks and on university 
campuses.  Their leaves are heart-shaped.  

The wood of ``katsura'' is good for carving.  It isn't crack after 
processing.  It has been used for sculptures and furniture.  Ainu 
people made dugout canoes from ``katsura'' trees.  

A dugout canoe is displayed at the Ainu Museum in Nibudani district.
Biratori Town, Hidaka sub-prefecture.  The canoe is made of a 
``katsura'' tree.  The wood of ``katsura'' is light and easy to float.  
The canoe is easy to maneuver.  

Ainu people believed that ``ranko'' ( ``katsura'' in Japanese ) has a 
good spirit.  ``Ranko'' trees have magical powers and help people.  

On the other hand, there are trees that have evil spirits.  One of them 
is ``doronoki'' tree.  In English, it's called Japanese popular.  In 
Ainu language, it's called ``yay-ni'' ( just, tree ) or ``kur-ni'' 
( evil, tree ).  There are good things and bad things.  

``Doronoki'' tree is bad because the wood is soft for building houses. 
The wood isn't suitable for firewood.  It damages the saw blade 
although the wood is soft.  The name ``doronoki'' in Japanese means 
``mad, of, tree.''  It's said that the tree absorbs mud from its roots. 
 
Ainu people believed that everything had a soul.  They talked to 
trees.  ( When I wrote this part, I listened to the song ``I Talk To 
The Wind.''  --> http://youtu.be/FEKVMTwW9S0 ( by Opus III ) ; http://
youtu.be/UlKrH07au6E ( by King Crimson ) )
 
( For Ainu culture, see Biratori Town in Hidaka sub-prefecture. )

( For ``doronoki'' tree, see Monbetsu City in Okhotsk general sub-
  prefecture. )

( For good spirit tree, see Shikabe Town in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. )
 
#3
  
As for the place name ``Konbu'' in Rankoshi Town, it signifies 
``konbu'' kelp.  An old Ainu chief said that there was seaweed 
( ``konbu'' kelp ) in the Konbu river ( by ``Ainu Language Place Names 
( Ainugo-chimei-list )'' http://
www.pref.hokkaido.lg.jp/ks/ass/new_timeilist.htm  last accessed in June 
2019.  ARCHIVE --> http://www.researchgate.net/publication/415203713 )  

The Konbu River meets the Shiribeshtsu River.  The confluence is 
located 15 kilometers inland from the Sea of Japan.  The legend by the 
old Ainu chief may hint at a memory a huge tidal wave.  Or, he may 
have mentioned a pun.

According to the book ``The Origin Of Station Names ( Eikmei-no-
kiguen )'' published in 1929 by Sapporo Bureau of the Japan National
 Railways, the name Konbu Station came from the Ainu word ``tok-kopu-
nupuri,'' which means ``top, hump, mountain.''

It siginified Mt. Konbu-dake ( 1,045 meters ) in the upper reaches of 
the Konbu River.  The mountain is located between Niseko Town in 
Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture and Toyoura Town in Iburi general 
sub-prefecture.  It has a hump on the top.  It was called Mt. Konpo-
dake in the past.

( UTF code ; Konbu-dake = \u6606\u5E03\u5CB3.  Konpo-dake = 
  \u6DF7\u4FDD\u5CB3. )

( For ``konbu'' kelp, see Shin-hidaka Town, Hidaka sub-prefecture. )

#4 

Minato-machi district in Rankoshi Town was part of the formar Isoya 
Village.  Although Isoya Village disappeared, Isoya county ( Isoya-
gun ) is still exist.  The name ``Isoya'' comes from ``iso-ya'' in Ainu 
language, which means ``rocky, shore.''  

The Kanji characters for Isoya ( \u78EF\u8C37 ) is a Japanese family 
name.   ``Iso-ya'' in Japanese means ``shore, valley.''  But, the 
place name ``Isoya'' exists only in northern Japan.  They are located 
in Suttsu Town and Hakodate City in Hokkaidou Prefecture and in Sai 
Village in Shimokita Peninsula, Aomori Prefecture.

( Single of the chapter ; ``El Entrerriano ( The Person From Engre 
  Rios )'' by Firpo.  --> http://youtu.be/II-RfselNys )

|                  |
|      NISEKO      |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 23  |
 ------------------
AREA 197 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 144, CULTIVATED 22 ; COUNTY = ABUTA ; POP 
4,511 ( 1990 ), 4,823 ( 2010 ) ( + ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = RED SKIER. 

( UTF code ; Abuta = \u867B\u7530. )

LOCATION 

Niseko ( ni-se-ko ) is a town in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture, 
central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Niseko is located in the middle 
reaches of the Shiribetsu River.  

The area includes the south slope of Mt. Niseko-an-nupuri ( 1,308 
meters ), the west slope of Mt. Youtei-zan ( 1,898 meters ), and the 
north slope of Mt. Konbu-dake ( 1,045 meters ).   

NAME
 
The name ``Niseko'' came from ``nisey-ko-an-pet'' in Ainu language.  It 
means ``cliff, towards, exist, river.''  It signified the Niseko-
anbetsu River, which is a tributary of the Shiribetsu River.  Mt. 
Niseko-an-nupuri is a famous mountain among skiers.  It means ``cliff, 
towards, exist, mountain'' in Ainu language.  #1 

The Niseko-anbetsu River is far from the center of Niseko Town.  It 
flows in the western part, making the border with Rankoshi Town.  Konbu 
Hot Spring is located along the river.  According to ``Niseko Town 
Centennial History ( Niseko-chou-hyakunan-shi )'' published in 2002,  
there is a magical waterfall on the upstream of the Niseko-anbetsu 
River.  #2

In the past, Niseko Town was called Kari-buto Town.  The name ``Kari-
buto'' was derived from ``mak-kari-buto'' in Ainu language, which means 
``behind, turning, river end.''  In the area of Niseko Town, the 
Makkari River ends.  It meets the Shiribeshi River. 

HISTORY  

In 1894 ( Meiji 27 ), the Colonial Land Division was established in 
Makkari-betsu wilderness.  The land was delimited like a chessboard. 
In 1896 ( Meiji 29 ), Matsuoka Farm was established.  In 1897 ( Meiji 
30 ), the Hokkaidou State-owned Undeveloped Land Disposal Law was 
enacted.  

( In Japanese ; ``Colonial Land Division'' is Shokumin-chi-kukaku.  
  ``Hokkaidou State-owned Undeveloped Land Disposal Law'' is Hokkaidou-
  kokuyuu-mikaichi-shobun-hou.  ``Makkari-betsu wilderness'' is 
  Makkari-betsu-guen-ya. )

( UTF code ; Shokuminchi-kukaku = \u6B96\u6C11\u5730\u533A\u753B. )

In 1898 ( Meiji 31 ), Miyata Farm was established.  In 1899 ( Meiji 
32 ), Arishima Farm was established.  Those were large farms managed by 
absentee landlords.  #3

( In Japanese ; ``Absentee landlord'' is ``fuzai-jinushi.'' )

( For the Hokkaidou Land Sale Act ( Hokkaidou-tochi-haraisague-kisoku ) 
  enacted in 1886, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi general sub-prefecture. )

In 1901 ( Meiji 34 ), an administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) 
was established in Kaributo village.  It was separated from Makkari 
village ( now Rusutsu Village ).  In 1904 ( Meiji 37 ), Makkari Station 
on the Hokkaidou Railway opened ( now Niseko Station on the Hakodate 
Main Line ). 

In 1906 ( Meiji 39 ), Kaributo Village ( 2nd-class municpality ) was 
established.  The station's name changed from Makkari to Kaributo.  In 
1950 ( Shouwa 25 ), Kaributo Town was established.  In 1964 ( Shouwa 
39 ), the town's name was changed from Kariboto to Niseko.  It was 
changed following the designation of Niseko-shakotan-otaru Coast Quasi-
National Park in 1963.  #4  #5

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC 

The town hall of Niseko is located near the confluence of the 
Shiribetsu River and the Makkari River.  The Hakodate Main Line and 
National Route 5 ( Youtei-kokudou ) run near the town hall.  #6

INDUSTRY  

Distinctive industries in Niseko are sightseeing and agriculture. 
STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = pasture 434 hectares in 2015, beans 
( soybeans 280, adzuki beans 93, other beans 10 ), paddy rice 343, 
potatoes 304, wheat 115, fodder 103, buckwheat 23, carrots 22, 

( continue ) --> broccoli 19, melons 17, sugar beets 17, tomatoes 7, 
Chinese cabbages 1, flowers 1, green onions 1, orchards 1, other grains 
5, and other vegetables 134+X ( sweet corn, asparagus ) ; dairy cows 
847 head in 2015. 

As for sightseeing, three of the five ski resorts around Mt. Niseko-
an-nupuri are located in Niseko Town.  Those are Niseko Village, Niseko 
Annupuri International, and Niseko Moiwa.  Other two, Grand Hirafu and 
Hanazono, are located in Kucchan Town.  Among them, Niseko Moiwa is the 
oldest.  Mountain skiing started before World War II. 

At the time, skiers climbed Mt. Moiwa ( 839 meters ) with anti-slip 
stickers on ski bottoms.  When reaching the summit, they peeled off 
anti-slip stickers and went down the mountain.  They could ski only 
once a day.  ( by ``Niseko Town History ( Niseko-chou-shi )'' published 
in 1982. )  #7  #8  #9

NOTE 
 
#1

During World War II, on the top of Mt. Niseko-an-nupuri, there was an 
icing observatory.  Dr. Ukichirou Nakaya of Hokkaidou Imperial 
University ( now Hokkaidou University ) established the observatory to 
study airplane icing.  He placed a Zero fighter ( Zero-sen ) on it.

Dr. Nakatani was a physicist and essayist.  He established the 
Institute of Low Temperature Science, Hokkaidou University.  He said, 
``Snow is a letter from heaven.''

After the war, the Zero fighter was abandoned in the valley.  Its 
engines and fuselage were disappeared.  In 1990, a right wing was 
discovered.  In 2004, the Fuudokan Museum in Kucchan Town acquired the 
right wing.  The museum exhibits it. 

Most of the fighter's parts were taken away.  It was a time of 
postwar shortages.  Steels and duralumins from the fighter may have 
been processed into pots, pans, and tobacco pipes.  ( During the war, 
pots, pans, and bronze statues were confiscated.  In the past, people 
smoked cigarettes with pipes. )  

In the second half of the 20th century, Japan became one of the world's 
largest steel producing nations.  They also produce automobiles from 
steel.  

Today, all desks, chairs, and bookshelves in the office are made of 
steel.  I liked the wooden desk and bookshelf I saw in professor's 
rooms in Sao Paulo. 
 
#2

As for a magical waterfall, ``Niseko Town Centennial History ( Niseko-
chou-hyakunan-shi )'' published in 2002 stats the following. 

``There is a magical waterfall in the upper reaches of the Niseko-
  anbetsu River.  In 1978, an elderly man from Otaru City went missing.
  He went into the deep mountains to pick wild vegetables.  

  Two days later, he was found and said, ``There was a fairyland.  A 
  white waterfall cascaded.  There was a white snake in the waterfall 
  basin.

  ( In Japanese ; ``Waterfall basin'' is ```taki-tsubo.'' )

  There are several waterfalls in the south slope of Mt. Niseko-an-
  nupuri.  The largest one is 15 meter's high and 8 meter's width.  
  There was a plan to create a summer tourist destination by utilizing 
  the magical waterfall, but it ended up as an illusion.'' 

#3 

Miyata Farm was located in what is now Niseko Town.  It was managed by 
an absentee landlord.  According to ``Niseko Town History ( Niseko-
chou-shi )'' published in 1982 ) ;  

``Many farmers in Miyata Farm migrated from Ibaraki Prefecture.  They 
  sometimes made rain-begging rituals from the Meiji period to the 
  Taishou period.  In 1908 ( Meiji 41 ), the fire of a rain-begging 
  ritual spread.  A forest fire broke out on Mt. Youtei-zan.''

( In Japanese ; ``Rain-begging ritual'' is ``amagoi.'' )
 
Ibaraki is an agricultural prefecture, where farmers cultivate rice in 
the lowlands and other crops in the upland fields.  In the past, they 
burned timbers during rain-begging rituals to pray for a good harvest.
 
There is Amabiki Temple in the prefecture.  The name ``Amabiki'' means 
``rain, pull.''  The temple was famous for rain begging.  Fire makes 
smoke, smoke makes clouds, and clouds make the rain.  Today, people 
think that isn't scientific. 

There is a similar story in the USA.  During the pioneering years, 
farmers in the Great Plains believed this phrase, ``Rain follows the 
plow.''  This means that if you cultivate more land ( plow ), moisture 
in the soil evaporates into the air, creating clouds, and make the 
rain.  ( by N. Yagasaki et al. eds. 2003.  ``American High Plains'' 
published by Kokon-soin, ISBN 4772215727 ; by Nihei lab 2026_w. )  

These stories about burning wood tips and plowing land to attract rain 
are nonsense.  But, how can we laugh at them?  If someone a hundred 
years from now were to look at our society, they would say the same 
thing.  

As for forest fire, there are two mountains named Mt. Yake-yama in 
Sapporo City.  ``Yake-yama'' means ``burning, mountain'' in Japanese.  
Today, the vegetation of the mountains looks natural, but in the 
pioneering years ( more than 100 years ago ), agricultural fire spread 
and turned into forest fires.

I felt like seeing a forest fire.  But I quit the idea after seeing 
forest fires in California and Brazilian savanna.  ( I was afraid and 
satisfied. ) 

Currently, in this country, we cannot burn garbage and leftover harvest 
crops outdoors.  It's prohibited by law.  In autumn, I sometimes miss 
the smell of burning rice straw.  When I smelled it, I felt as if I was 
floating in the air. 

#4   
  
According to ``Niseko Town History ( Niseko-chou-shi )'' published in 
1982 ; 
``The principal of an elementary school visited Toukyou in 1913 
   ( Taishou 2 ).  He brought back a box of caramel.  The price was 
  five Sen ( about 100 Yen today ).  School children were surprised by 
  the high price.  

  After that, caramel spread widely in the country.  In 1915 ( Taishou 
  4 ), for caramel promotion, a free movie was screened at Fujimiza 
  theater in Kaributo Village ( now Niseko Town ).'' 

According to a former high school teacher I met during a bus excursion, 
a box of caramel was 20 yen in the 1950s.  When he was a junior high 
school student, he engaged in helping farming on a potato farm as a 
school event.  He was given a box of caramel at the time.  He thought 
it was a cheap wage.

( In Japanese ; ``Helping farming'' is ``en-nou.'' )

( UTF code ; ``en-nou'' = \u63F4\u8FB2. ) 

( For helping farming by students, see Yuubetsu Town in Okhotsuk 
  general sub-prefecture. ) 

#5   
 
Arishima Farm was located in what is now Niseko Town.  It was a large 
farm with 450 hectares of land.  The owner was an absentee landlord.
The novelist Takeo Arishima ( 1878-1923 ) was the son of the landlord.
  
Takeo Arishima graduated from Sapporo Agriculture Collage ( now 
Hokkaidou University ).  In 1922 ( Taishou 11 ), he released Arishima 
Farm to tenant farmers.  There is the Arishima Memorial Museum in 
Niseko Town.  

Takeo Arishima published ``The Descendants Of Cain ( Kain-no-
matsuei ).''  This novel depicts a couple who migrated to a farm near 
Kucchan.  The farm is likely Arishima Farm in Kaributo Village ( now 
Niseko Town ).  The couple lost their money, their horse, and their 
son.  They left the farm with nothing.  

( Cain was the first murdererof mankind.  He killed his younger brother 
  Abel out of jealousy.  Cain is the ancestor of mankind. ) 

Takeo Arishima also published ``Clara Becomes A Nun ( Clara-no-
shukke ).''  Clara was born into a wealthy family.  She was tormented 
by dreams of carnal desire.  ``She felt a deep shame.  Sweat beaded 
at her hairline.''  She became a nun.  ( To sell a novel, sexual 
allusion was needed. )

Takeo Arishima committed a double suicide with a married woman.  The 
lives of novelists are like novels.  I can't stop reading their 
Wikipedia pages.
   
( For novelist, see the Otaru West section of Otaru City, Shiribeshi 
  general sub-prefecture. )
  
#6 

Young people moved to Niseko Town and neighboring Kucchan Town.  Famous 
ski resorts in Mt. Niseko-an-nupuri attracted them.  They often came 
from wealthy families.  Due to tourism-related migration, the 
population of Niseko Town increased.  

But, the population of Kucchan Town decreased.  Kucchan is a town of 
government and agriculture, and has a larger population than Niseko.  
Government offices were restructured and small-scale farmers gave up
farming. 
 
I met a couple who own their lodge in Niseko Town.  They moved from 
Toukyou because they loved skiing.  Mt. Niseko-an-nupuri is famous for 
its powder snow.  The couple hired foreign employees from Southeast 
Asian countries to serve foreign customers.  

They said, ``Niseko is a good place to raise ( re'ize ) children.  But, 
we worry about their education as they grow up.''
 
#7 

Moiwa Ski Resort was the oldest ski ground around Mt. Niseko-an-nupuri. 
In 1916 ( Taishou 5 ), a ski ground was established by the ski club of 
Faculty of Agriculture, Touhoku Imperial University ( now Hokkaidou 
University Ski Club ).  The ski ground was called Hokudai Slope. 

The ski club camped at Konbu Hot Spring every winter from 1919 to 1951. 
( Konbu Onsen is located in the foot of the Mt. Moiwa. )  Next to 
Hokudai Slope was Koushou Slope, which the ski club of Otaru High 
School of Commerce established.  ( Otaru High School of Commerce ( now 
Otaru University of Commerce ) was called Otaru-koushou. )

In 1962, rope tows were installed at Moiwa ski ground by Kaributo Town 
( now Niseko Town ).  In 1966, Moiwa Ski Resort was opened by a 
construction company in Sapporo City.  

The owner of Moiwa Ski Resort changed several times, including 
Taiheiyou Club ( a golf resort company in Toukyou ) and a real estate 
company in Toukyou.  Resort management is speculative. 

As for the name ``Moiwa,'' it comes from ``mo-iwa'' in Ainu language, 
which means ``small, mountain.''  In Sapporo City, too, There is Moiwa-
yama Ski Ground ( Moiwa-yama-skii-jou ).  ( Sapporo City is located in 
Ishikari sub-prefecture. ) 
  
#8

Niseko Annupuri International Ski Resort opened in 1950 as Kaributo 
Town Ski Slope ( Kaributo-chouei-kassou-jou ).  The ski slope had no 
rope tow or chairlift.  

In 1972, Hokkaidou Chuuou Bus installed lifts and opened Niseko 
Annupuri International Ski Resort.  ( In the year, the Winter Olympics 
were held in Sapporo City.  Okinawa Island was returned from the USA.  
Prime Minister Kakuei Tanaka published the report ``A Plan For 
Remodeling The Japan Archipelago.''  Land speculation increased. ) 

( In Japanese ; ``A Plan For Remodeling The Japan Archipelago'' is 
  Nihon-rettou-kaizou-ron. )

Although the management of ski resorts on Mt. Niseko-an-nupuri has 
changed frequently, Niseko Annupuri Ski Resort has been managed 
consistently by the same company.

#9 

Niseko Village Ski Resort opened in 1982 as Higashi-yama Ski Resort.  
( Higashi-yama is a distict in Niseko Town. )  It was developed by 
Kokudo, which was a real estate compnay of the Seibu Group.  The 
company also developed Higashi-yama Prince Hotel and a pension village. 

The Seibu Group was the symbol of the Bubble economy from the late 
1980s to the early 1990s.  In 2006, the management of Niseko Village 
Ski Resort changed to Citigroup from the USA.  In 2008, Higashi-yama 
Prince Hotel became Hilton Niseko Vilalge.  ( Hilton is also an 
American company. )  

In 2010, a construction company from Malaysia, YTL Corporation, 
purchased Niseko Village Ski Resort.  Resort operations in Hokkaidou 
are international and speculative. 

( For ski resort, see Kucchan Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. ) 

( Single of the chapter ; ``Eye'' by Spangle Call Lilli Line.  -->
  http://youtu.be/0X0NQplwrLE )

|                  |
|     MAKKARI      |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 24  |
 ------------------
AREA 114 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 78, CULTIVATED 27 ; COUNTY = ABUTA ; POP 
2,826 ( 1990 ), 2,189 ( 2010 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 5.5 C ( AV 1981-
2010 ), PRECIPI 1,322 MM ; COUNTRY SIGN = MT YOUTEI.  

( UTF code ; Abuta = \u867B\u7530. )

LOCATION   
 
Makkari ( ma-k-ka-ri ) is a village in Shiribeshi general sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Makkari is an inland 
village at the foot of Mt. Youtei-zan ( 1,898 meters ).  The area 
corresponds to the basin of the Makkari River, a tributary of the 
Shiribetsu River.  #1
 
( UTF code ; Makkari = \u771F\u72E9. )

NAME  

The name ``Makkari'' is derived from ``mak-kari-pet'' in Ainu language. 
It means ``behind, rotating, river.''  It signified the Makkari River, 
which flows around the south foot of Mt. Youtei-zan. 

HISTORY 

In 1894 ( Meiji 27 ), the Colonial Land Division was established in 
Makkari widlerness.  The land was delimited like a chessboard.  In 1895 
( Meiji 28 ), five families migrated from Kagawa Prefecture.  #2

( In Japanese ; ``The Colonial Land Division'' is Shokuminchi-kukaku.  
  ``Makkari wilderness'' is Makkari-guen-ya. )  

( UTF code ; Shokuminchi-kukaku = \u6B96\u6C11\u5730\u533A\u753B. ) 
 
In 1922 ( Taishou 11 ), Makkari-betsu Village ( 2nd-class 
municipality ) was established.  It was separated from Makkari Village 
( now Rusutsu Village ).  In 1925 ( Taishou 14 ), Makkari Village was 
renamed Rusutsu Village.  In 1941 ( Shouwa 16 ), Makkari-betsu Village 
was renamed Makkari Village. 

( For 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Matsumae Town. ) 
 
TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC  
 
The town hall of Makkari is located along the Makkari River.  
Prefectural Route 66 and Prefectural Route 97 intersect in the center 
of the village.  These are scenic roads that skirt the foot of 
Mt. Youtei-zan.  #3  #4
 
INDUSTRY  

Distinctive industries in Makkari are agriculture and food processing. 
STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = potatoes 498 hectares in 2015, beans 
( adzuki beans 339, soybeans 87, other beans 2 ), pasture 285, 

( continue ) --> sugar beets 192, carrots 159, wheat 124, fodder 115, 
broccoli 63, yams 19, paddy rice 8, tomatoes 1, and other vegetables X 
( concealed, including lily bulbs ) ; dairy cows 882 head in 2015. 
 
The production of lily bulbs for food is the largest in the country.  
Lily bulbs are shipped mainly to the Kansai region in western Japan.  
To harvest a lily bulb, it takes five years.  

Snow-covered carrots are also a local specialty.  As for food 
processing industry, agricultural cooperative operates a starch factory 
that uses local potatoes.  #5  #6  #7  #8  #9

( In Japanese ; ``Snow-covered carrot'' is ``yukishita-ninjin.'' )  

NOTE 
 
#1  

Mt. Youtei-zan is a cone-shaped stratovolcano.  The latest eruption 
happened about 1,000 years ago.  Mt. Youtei-zan is also called Ezo 
Fuji, which means Mt. Fuji in Ezo Island ( now Hokkaidou Island ).  Mt. 
Youtei-zan stands out like Mt. Fuji because it's an isolated peak 
rising from a plain.  ( Mt. Fuji is also a cone-shaped stratovolcano. )

In the past, Mt. Youtei-zan was called Mt. Shiribeshi.  The mountain's 
name ``Shiribeshi'' was documented in The Chronicle of Japan ( Nihon-
shoki ) edited in 659. 

( UTF code ; Shiribeshi = \u5f8c\u65b9\u7f8a\u8e44. )
 
The name ``Shiribeshi'' comes from ``sir-pet'' in Ainu language.  It 
means ``mountain, river.''  It signified the Shiribetsu River, which 
flows around the north slope of Mt. Youtei-zan.  
 
Ainu people called Mt. Shiribeshi ( now Mt. Youtei-zan ) ``mak-kari-
nupuri,'' which means ``behind, rotating, mountain.''  It signified the 
Makkari River that flows around the south slope of Mt. Youtei-zan.

As for the mountain's old name ``Shiribeshi,'' the last two Kanji 
characters are pronounced in a single syllable, ``shi.''  This is an 
unusual case in Japanese.  According to Kyuuya Fukada's ``Japan 100 
Mountains,'' the ``shi'' is derived from ``guishi-guishi,'' which means 
dock ( a plant ).

( UTF code ; ``shi'' = \u7f8a\u8e44. )

( For Japan 100 Mountains, see Shari Town in Okhotsk general sub-
  prefecture. )

( For the name ``Shiribeshi,'' see Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )

In 1950, Mt. Shiribeshi was designated as part of Shikotsu-touya 
National Park.  The name became known throughout the country.  Then, 
Kucchan Town changed the mountain name from Shiribeshi to Youtei, 
because the pronunciation of the original Kanji characters are very 
difficult. 

( UTF code ; Youtei = \u7f8a\u8e44. )

The original Kanji characters were shortened.  The pronunciation was 
changed to the Japanese reading ( ``kun-yomi'' ).  Thus, the name 
changed from Mt. Shiribeshi-yama to Mt. Youtei-zan.  Since 1969, the 
name ``Mt. Youtei-zan'' was printed on the map published by 
Geographical Survey Institute ( now Geospatial Information Authority of 
Japan ). 

#2

The Colonial Land Division ( Shokuminchi-kukaku ) is a chessboard-like 
land division.  In Makkari Village, lines were drawn based on the base 
line that connects the center of the village to the summit of the Mt. 
Youtei-zan.  

These are examples of the Colonial Land Divisions. 
( 1 ) Tokku-guen-ya ( now Shin-totsukawa Town in Sorachi general sub-
prefecture ), established in 1889 ( Meiji 22 ).

( 2 ) Nae-guen-ya ( now Naie Town in Sorachi general sub-prefecture ), 
established in 1891 ( Meiji 24 ).
 
( 3 ) Horomui-guen-ya ( now Nanporo Town in Sorachi general sub-
prefecture ) and Kucchan-guen-ya ( now Kucchan Town in Shiribeshi 
general sub-prefecture ), established in 1892 ( Meiji 25 ).

( 4 ) Maoi-guen-ya ( now Naganuma Town in Sorachi general sub-
prefecture ) and Yuubetsu-guen-ya ( now Yuubetsu Town in Okhotsk 
general sub-prefecture ), established in 1893 ( Meiji 26 ). 

( 5 ) Makkari-guen-ya ( now Makkari Village, Shiribeshi general sub-
prefecture ) and Pippu-guen-ya ( now Pippu Town, Kamikawa general sub-
prefecture ), established in 1894 ( Meiji 27 ).

( 6 ) Furano-guen-ya ( now Furano City, Kami-furano Town, and Naka-
fuano Town in Kamikawa general sub-prefecture ) and Tokachi-koku-guen-
ya ( now Obihiro City in Tokachi general sub-prefecure ), established 
in 1896 ( Meiji 29 ).

( 7 ) Nishibetsu-geunya ( now Betsukai Town in Nemuro sub-prefecture ), 
established in 1898 ( Meiji 31 ). 

According to this list, the development of Makkari Village began later 
than that of the Ishikari Plain, but earlier than that of the Tokachi 
Plain.  ( The Ishikari Plain is located in Sorachi general sub-
prefecture and Ishikari sub-prefecture.  The Tokachi Plain is located 
in Tokachi general sub-prefecture. )

( For the Colonial Land Division, see Shin-totsukawa Town in Sorachi 
  general sub-prefecture. )

Today, upland fields extend around the foot of Mt. Youtei-zan.  The 
landscape resembles the Tokachi Plain and the Kitami Basin.  But around 
Mt. Youtei-zan, the portion of vegetables is larger.  ( The Tokachi 
Plain is located in Tokachi general sub-prefecture.  The Kitai Basin 
is located in Okhotsk general sub-prefecture respectively. )  

( In Japanese ; ``Upland field'' is ``hatake.'' )

The center of Sapporo City also has a chessboard-like land division.  
But, it isn't the Colonial Land Division.  The Colonial Land Divisions 
were established in rural areas in Hokkaidou Island.  

The Colonial Land Division was modeled after the American township land 
division system.  In the USA, the roads in a township run in a true 
north and a true south, but the roads in Colonial Land Division in 
Hokkaidou Island don't.

The roads in central Sapporo City run a north-south direction, but they 
aren't a true north-south direction.  They were constructed parallel to 
the artificial Sousei River.  The river may have been constructed to 
flow the direction of magnetic north. 
 
#3

In Makkari Village Community Center ( Makkari-mura-koumin-kan ), a 
stuffed dog is displayed.  The name is Pochi.  It was the Hokkaidou 
dog.  ( The Hokkaidou dog is also called the Ainu dog ).

A mailman in Makkari Village named the dog Pochi.  A day in January, 
1918 ( Taishou 7 ), the mailman delivered a telegram.  Pochi went with 
him.  On the way back, the weather changed to a snow storm, he was 
unable to move and died.  Pochi guarded his body.  The next day, a 
villager found his corpse because Pochi was barking. 

Stuffed Pochi was exhibited at the Telecommunication Museum in Chiyoda 
Ward, Toukyou Prefecture.  In 1987, Stuffed Pochi was moved to Makkari 
Village.

( In Japanese ; ``The Telecommunication Museum'' is Teishin-sougou-
  hakubutsu-kan. ) 

The name ``Pochi'' is common for dogs.  The name ``Tama'' is common 
for cats.  There's the song ``Pochi And Tama.''  This was printed in 
the textbook for ordinary elementary school around 1905.

( In Japanese ; ``Ordinary elementary school'' is ``jinjou-shou-
  gakkou.'' )

These are the lyrics. 
``Konoko-wa  Pochi-to  Moushi-masu  
  ( This puppy is Pochi. )

  Chinchin-oazuke  Mina-jouzu  
  ( Stand up!  Wait!  Everything's fine. ) 

  Imani-otonani-natta-nara  
  ( When it grows up, )

  Gomon-no-banwo  Yoku-shimashou    
  ( It will guard the gate well. ) 

  Konoko-wa  Tama-to  Moushi-masu  
  ( This kitten is Tama. ) 

  Mainichi-guenki-ni  Asobi-masu  
  ( It plays cheerfully every day. ) 

  Imani-otonani-natta-nara  
  ( When it grows up, ) 

  Nezumi-no-banwo  Yoku-shimashou 
  ( It will watch out for rats well. )''

( In the Age of Sail, cats were on board to hunt rats.  They were 
  savers of grain storage ( by Nihei lab 2023-2026b ). )  

( For dog, see Setana Town in Hiyama sub-prefecture ; Furubira Town and 
  Makkari Village in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture ; Naie Town in 
  Sorachi general sub-prefectures. ) 
  
#4  

There is a bronze statue of Takashi Hosokawa in Makkari Village.  He is 
an Enka singer.  Enka is a Traditional Japanese musical style.  Makkari 
is his hometown.  These lyrics are from one of his hit songs.

``Watashi-baka-yo-ne  Obaka-sanyo-ne
  ( I am fool.  I am very fool. )

  Anata-hitori-ni  Inochi-wo-kakete  Taetekita-no-yo''
  ( I watched only you, with my very life.  I endured. )

( For Enka singer, see Shiriuchi Town in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. )

#5  
 
As for lily bulb cultivation in Makkari, in 1961, Yukio Saitou started 
breeding lily bulbs.  He used native lily varieties.  His lilies were 
sold as Saitou Lily ( Saitou-yuri ).  In 1966, a group of lily bulb 
producers was established. 

Today, the main lily variety is ``Haku-guin,'' which means ``white 
silver.''  It was bread from small tiger lily.  The bitterness of the 
bulb is milder.

( In Japanese ; ``Small tiger lily'' is ''ko-oni-yuri.'' )
 
Ainu people ate bulbs of ``oo-uba-yuri,'' which is a kind of lily.  On 
the campus of Hokkaidou University, where Ainu people lived in the 
past, there are colonies of ``oo-uba-yuri.''  The lily produces a lot 
of tiny seeds in autumn.  Children play by shaking the stem to drop 
the seeds.  It looks like snow falling. 

( In scientific name ; ``Oo-uba-yuri'' is ``Cardiocrinum cordatum var. 
  glehnii.'' )  

( For ``oo-uba-yuri,'' see Hokkaidou University, Kita Ward, Sapporo 
  City, Ishikari sub-prefecture. )

Students from the Kansai region said that they ate lily bulbs in 
``chawan-mushi'' and ``osechi.''  ( ``Chawan-mushi'' is an egg custard. 
It's written as ``tea cup, steam.''  ``Osechi'' is New Year's box 
dishes. )

In Hokkaidou, ``chawan-mushi'' and ``sekihan'' are sweet.  
( ``Sekihan'' is steamed rice with beans.  It's written as ``red, 
rice.''  Generally, ``chawan-mushi'' and ``sekihan'' are salty. )

* * *

In my hometown, goldband lilies bloomed in the mountains.  When I was 
a child, I ate the bulbs of goldband lily.  They were bitter.  Today, 
no goldband lilies grow in the mountains.  Wild boars increased.  They 
ate lily bulbs.

( In Japanese ; ``Goldband lily'' is ``yama-yuri,'' which means 
  ``mountain lily.'' )  

In the Tama Hills, where I live now, many Easter lilies bloom in 
August.  There are Easter lily clusters.  The fragrance drifts on the 
air in the evening.

( In Japanese ; ``Easter lily'' is ``teppou-yuri,'' which means ``gun, 
  lily,'' because the flower is like an old fashioned gun. )

Easter lily bears long white flowers.  Goldband lily bears white 
flowers with freckles.  Tiger lily bears orange flowers with freckles.

( In Japanese ; ``Tiger lily'' is ``oni-yuri,'' which means ogre 
  ( o'o-gar ) lily. )
 
My mother said that the pollen of lily is hard to be washed.  It's used 
s lily dyeing.  The pollen is yellow.  Before World War II, Japan 
exported lily bulbs.  Those were for flowers, not for eating. 

( For lily bulb export, see Nanae Town in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. )

( For poem including lily, see the note of Akabira City in Sorachi 
  general sub-prefecture. ) 
 
#6

As for snow-covered carrots ( ``yukishita-ninjin'' ) in Makkari 
Village, the carrots are harvested in winter.  An excavator removes the 
snow and carrots are harvested.  Snow-covered carrots become sweet 
because their sugar content increases to prevent being frozen.

Wintering Danshaku potatoes are also a specialty of the village.  
Danshaku is a popular potato variety for cooking.  Danshaku potatoes 
are stored at low temperatures during the winter and their taste
becomes better.

( For Danshaku potato, see Kucchan Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. ) 

This is a crop calendar for Makkari Village ( as of the mid-2010s ) ; 
  May-June          = asparagus.  ( White asparagus and green 
     asparagus. )
  June-July         = radishes and white turnips.
  August-September  = sweet corn.  ( The varieties are Amain-desu and 
     Pure-white. )

  September-October = potatoes for cooking.  ( The varieties are 
     Danshaku, May-queen, Kita-akari, and Inca-no-mezame. ) 
                    = potatoes for processing.
                    = garlic.
  November-December = lily bulbs. 

  ( Potatoes for cooking are sold until April.  Potatoes for processing 
    are used in a starch factory.  See Note No. 9 below. )   
 
  December          = leeks ( Western green onions ). 
  January-February  = wintering Danshaku.  ( Danshaku is a potato 
     variety. ) 
  February-March    = snow-covered carrots.
  May-June          = wintering yams.

Sweet corn is immature corn.  It can be eaten like vegetable.  In 
Hokkadidou, sweet corn is harvested from August to September.  

I visited Oodori Park in Sapporo City in early July 1992.  I bought a 
roasted sweet corn ( ``yaki-toumorokoshi'' ).  The seller asked, 
``Where are you from?''  I said, ``Ibaraki.''  The seller said, 
``Really?  This corn is also from Ibaraki.'' 

As for potatoes, they are harvested from September to October in 
Hokkaidou.  Their flowers are cute.  When I conducted a field survey in 
the Tokachi Plain in July 2006, I heard that the potatoes with purple 
flowers were for cooking and the ones with white flowers were for 
processing. 

#7  

In Makkari Village, there is a potato farm that cultivates ``the 
original of the original variety.''  It takes five to six years to 
harvest the original of the original variety. 

( In Japanese ; ``The original of the original variety'' is ``guen-
  guen-shu.'' )

The original of the original variety is sold to the farms that 
cultivate the original potato variety.  Then, the original potato 
variety is sold to the farms that cultivate seed potatoes.  Then, seed 
potatoes are soled to ordinary potato farms.

( In Japanese ; ``Seed potato'' is ``tane-imo.''  ``The original 
  variety'' is ``guen-shu.'' )

Potatoes can be harvested in a single year, but it takes eight to nine 
years counting from ``the original of the original variety.''
( The original of the original variety --> The original variety -->  
  Seed potatoes --> Potatoes. ) 

( In Kyushu Island, potatoes can be harvested twice in a year, so it's 
  called ``nido-imo,'' which means ``twice, potato.'' )

#8
 
There are many farming successors in Makkari Village.  A graduation 
thesis submitted to Faculty of Letters, Hokkaidou University, examined 
the reasons.  The following is the summary.

( 1 ) Farmers can earn a stable income.  Their operations are based on 
mechanized agriculture, the introduction of new technologies, and an 
organized distribution system. 

( continue ) --> ( 2 ) Young successors can enjoy skiing and snow-
mobiling in the winter.  ( 3 ) The location is not too far from 
Sapporo, the largest city in Hokkaidou.  ( 4 ) The scenery around Mt. 
Youtei-zan is beautiful. 

#9 
 
Starch factories stand out on the map, because they have rectangular 
ponds.  ( MAP --> http://maps.gsi.go.jp/#15/42.780661/140.773637/ ).  
The ponds are for sedimentation.  In Makkari Village, the starch 
factory is managed by JA ( Japan Agricultural Cooperatives ).  

The factory produces starch from late August to early November, using 
potatoes cultivated not only in Makkari Village but also in 
municipalities in Hiyama sup-prefecture and Oshima general sub-
prefecture in south Hokkaidou.  There is no starch factory in south 
Hokkaidou. 

In the past, the area around Mt. Youtei-zan was the main producing 
area of starch in the country.  The starch was produced at water 
wheels.  

In Makkari Village, farmers owned water wheels for starch production 
until the 1960s.  The number was 117.  In 1966, agricultural 
cooperative established a modern starch factory, and water wheels were 
abolished.  ( by T. Inoue et al. 1968.  Control of the growth of 
slimy fungi.  ``Hokkaidou-ritsu-eisei-kenkyuujo-nenpou''  http://
www.iph.pref.hokkaido.jp/Kankobutsu/Shoho/annual18/shoho180128.pdf  
last accessed in Jun 2019. )

( For starch production, see Touyako Town in Iburi general sub-
  prefecture ; Shibetsu City in Kamikawa general sub-prefecture ; 
  Shihoro Town in Tokachi general sub-prefecture ; Shari Town, Kiyosato 
  Town, and Koshimizu Town in Okhotsk general sub-prefecture. )

In Honshuu Island, Kujuu-kuri Beach in Chiba prefecture was the major 
producer of starch.  The starch factories used locally produced sweet 
potatoes.  The factories were closed because of the importation of corn 
starch and the increase in vegetable cultivation for residents of the 
Toukyou metropolitan area.  ( by T. Nihei 2002.  Technological 
innovations in the continuation of greenhouse horticulture in Asahi 
City. ``Geographical Review Of Japan'' http://
www.research gate.net/publication/270038030 )

Sugar factories in Hokaidou Island also stand out on the map because 
they have rectangular ponds and large buildings. 

( For sugar factory, see Memuro Town in Tokachi general sub-
  prefecture. )

( Single of the chapter ; ``Palida Sombra ( Pale Shadow )'' by Firpo. 
  --> http://youtu.be/CuemIaJscrE )
