AREA 71 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 57, CURLTIVATED 3 ; COUNTY = IWANAY ; POP 
19,372 ( 1990 ), 14,451 ( 2010 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = WALLEYE POLLOCK, SEA, 
& ROCK.  

LOCATION   
 
Iwanai ( i-wa-na-i ) is a town in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture, 
central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Iwanai is a small town ( under 100 
square kilometers ).  The area faces the Sea of Japan ( Nihon-kai ).
To the south, the area of Iwanai Town is separated from Rankoshi Town 
by the Niseko Mountains.  #1 

( UTF code ; Iwanai = \u5CA9\u5185. )
 
To the east, the area of Iwanai Town is linearly separated from Kyouwa 
Town.  The line is 140 degrees 32 minutes east longitude ( by GSI Maps 
--> http://maps.gsi.go.jp/#13/42.961510/140.532560/ ).  The built-up 
area of Iwanai extends beyond the line and into Kyouwa Town.  #2 

NAME   

The name ``Iwanai'' comes from ``iwa-nay'' in Ainu language.  It means 
``hill, river.''  Or, it comes from ``iwaw-nay'' in Ainu language.  It 
means ``sulfur, river.''  

Both signify the Iwanai River, which flowed in front of the trading 
post of Iwanai in the Edo era.  Currently, the Pon-iwanai River flows 
near the town hall.  The word ``pon'' means ``small'' in Ainu language. 

( A trading post in the Edo era is called ``unjou-ya'' in Japanese.  
  The word ``unjou'' meant ``tax'' in the Edo era. ) 
 
According to ``Iwanai Town History ( Iwanai-chou-shi )'' published in 
1966, the name ``iwaw-nay'' ( sulfur, river ) may state Mt. Iwao-nupuri 
( 1,116 meters ) in the Niseko Mountains.  There was a sulfur mine on 
the mountain. 

( For Mt. Iwao-nupuri, see Kucchan Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )
  
HISTORY  
 
In the 18th century, during the Edo era, the Matsumae Domain built 
Iwanai-basho ( a trading place ).  This was for herring fishing and 
trade with Ainu people.  Oumi merchants operated the trading place.  
( Oumi merchants came from what is now Shiga Prefecture. )  

( For Oumi merchant, see Esashi Town in Hiyama sub-prefecture. )

The trading place became ``sengoku-basho.''  ( ``Sengoku-basho'' was a 
a trading place with catches of 1,000-koku of herring.  One ``koku'' is 
200 kg of fresh herring. )  In 1856, during the Edo era, the Raiden 
Mountain Road ( Raiden-sandou ) opened.  It connected Iwanai and Isoya 
( now Suttsu Town ).  #3 

( For ``sengoku-basho,'' see Otobe Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )
  
After the Meiji period, in 1869 ( Meiji 2 ), Iwanai branch of the 
Hokkaidou Development Commission ( Kaitaku-shi ) was established.  In 
1900 ( Meiji 33 ), Iwanai Town ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established.  

( For 1st-class municipality, see the note of Matsumae Town in Oshima 
  general sub-prefecture. )

In 1955 ( Shouwa 30 ), Iwanai Town merged Shimano Village.  It was 
during the municipal mergers in the Shouwa period.  The area of Shimano 
Village was 67.2 square kilometers.  The area of the former Iwanai Town 
was 3.4 square kilometers 

The area of the former Iwanai Town was small.  It consisted only of a 
built-up area around the Port of Iwanai.  Keeping small, it became a 
1st-class town.  #4  $5

( UTF code ; Shimano = \u5CF6\u91CE. )
  
TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC
 
The town hall of Iwanai is located in front of the Port of Iwanai.  
National Route 229 ( Raiden-kokudou ) and National Route 276 intersect 
near the town hall.

Formerly, the Iwanai Line connected Iwanai Station and Kozawa Station.
( Kozawa Station on the Hakodate Main Line is located in neighboring 
Kyouwa Town. )  The railroad ran along the Route 276.  FormerIy, there 
was the ferry line that connected the Port of Iwanai and the Port of 
Naoetsu in Niigata Prefecture.  #6  #7
 
INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industries in Iwanai are fishery, food processing, 
sightseeing, and paddy rice cultivation.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = 
pasture 94 hectares in 2015, paddy rice 91, fodder 17, orchards 1, and 
other vegetables 4+X ; dairy cows 126 head in 2015.  STATISTICS OF 
FISHERIES = salmon 328 tons in 2017, 

( continue ) --> squid ( flying squid 297, other squid 7 ), atka 
mackerel 77, Pacific cod 71, sand lance 53, walleye pollock 52, octopus 
61, right-eye flounder 61, olive flounder 49, yellowtail 29, sea 
urchins 5, crabs 1, mackerel 1, puffer fish 1, sea trout 1, seaweed 1, 
herring 0, other fish 198, and other marine animals 21. 

Asparagus cultivation and canned asparagus industry in Japan were 
started in Iwanai.  Nihon Asparagus, a joint-stock company, is 
headquartered in Iwanai Town.  It currently sells beverages instead of 
asparagus.  #8 

( In Japanese ; ``Joint-stock'' company is ``kabushiki-gaisha.'' ) 
at Mt. Iwanai-dake ( 1,085 meters ) in the Niseko Mountains.  The 
management of the ski resort was recovered because of the increase in 
foreign tourists.  #9 
 
Raiden Coast near Cape Raiden has a picturesque landscape.  The name 
``Raiden'' comes from ``ra-en-rum'' in Ainu language, which means 
``low, protruding, head.''  It signified a small cape along Raiden 
coast.  Or, it comes from ``rai-ni'' in Ainu language, which means 
``dead, tree.'' 

On the map published by Takeshirou Matsuura in 1859, during the Edo 
era ``Raiden'' is marked as a Japanese name ( ``wa-mei'' ).  ( Map --> 
http://koukita.github.io/touzaiezo/map/ )  

NOTE

#1
  
The Niseko Mountains is also called the Niseko volcanic group.  
Volcanoes are lined up in a row.  To the west is Mt. Raiden-yama 
( 1,211 meters ) and to the east is Mt. Niseko-an-nupuri ( 1,308 
meters ).

( In Japanese ; ``Volcanic group'' is ``kazan-gun.'' )

Mt. Raiden-yama to the west is the oldest volcano.  It was active 
between 1.4 million and 800,000 years ago.  Mt. Niseko-an-nupuri to the 
east is a new volcano.  It was active between 700,000 and 250,000 years 
ago. 

The volcanic activity is moving eastward.  Mt. Youtei-zan is located to 
the east from the Niseko Mountains.  It's a newer volcano, which 
erupted about 1,000 years ago.  Mt. Youtei-zan is a corn-shaped 
mountain.  The slopes aren't as eroded as the volcanoes of the Niseko 
Mountains.  

These years of eruptions are categorized as the Quaternary period in 
the geological chronicle.  The Quaternary period includes the present 
day. Most volcanoes in the country erupted in the Quaternary period.  
The period before the Quaternary is the Tertiary.  

( In Japanese ; ``Quaternary'' is ``Dai-yon-ki.''  ``Tertiary'' is 
  ``Dai-san-ki.'' )

The word ``Tertiary'' become old.  New words are Neogene and Paleogene. 
Things become complicated.  People will forget them.  

( For geological chronicle, see Nihei lab 2024-2026_e. ) 

#2

GSI Maps has been available since 2013 by the Geospatial Information 
Authority of Japan ( Kokudo-chiri-in ), whose former name was 
Geographical Survey Institute ( GSI ).  

The governmental organization has published Japanese topographic maps.  
Land is the base of a nation. Unprofitable projects, including making 
topographic maps, are carried by public organizations.

( In Japanese ; ``Topographic map'' is ``chikei-zu.'' )

The first Japanese topographic maps were published from 1880 ( Meiji 
13 ) to 1886 ( Meiji 19 ).  The number was 921.  The name of maps were 
``Jinsoku-zu,'' which means ``quickly-made map,'' because they were 
drawn without triangulation.  

( In Japanese ; ``Triangulation'' is ``sankaku-sokuryou.'' )

( For triangulation, see the note of Tomakomai City, Iburi general sub-
  prefecture. )

Topographic maps published before 1955 were for military use.  
From 1892 to 2002, topographical maps were made based on Japanese 
geodetic datum ( Nihon-sokuchi-kei ).  Since 2002, topographic maps has 
been made based on World Geodetic System ( Sekai-sokuchi-kei ).  

Between old maps ( 1892-2002 ) and new maps ( after 2002 ), there is a 
difference of 400 to 450 meters.  In Toukyou, the latitude and 
longitude of old maps are located about 450 meters to the northwest.  
In Hokkaidou, they are located about 400 meters to the northwest.  

( by ``Developing And Providing Information On The National Land, 
  Serving As The Foundation For Social Activities'' http://
  www.gsi.go.jp/kikakuchousei/kikakuchousei40034.html  last accessed in 
  August 2026 ).

( by ``Japanese Geodetic System And World Geodetic System'' http://
  www.gsi.go.jp/LAW/G2000-g2000-h3.htm  last accessed in August 2026. )

( by ``Let's Learn Map Symbols Together'' http://
  www.gsi.go.jp/KIDS/KIDS05_00001.html  last accessed in August 2026. )

#3

During the Edo era, the Raiden Mountain Road ( Raiden-sandou ) was 
counted as one of the three treacherous roads in Ezo Island.  The 
road connected what are now Iwanai Town and Suttsu Town.  ( Iwanai and 
Suttsu are located in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )
 
The other two treacherous roads were the Rebungue Mountain Road 
( Rebungue-sandou ), between Oshamanbe Town and Toyoura Town, and the 
Saruru Mountain Road ( Saruru-sandou ) in Erimo Town.  ( Erimo is 
located in Hidaka sub-prefecture.  Oshamambe is in Oshima general sub-
prefecture.  Toyoura is in Iburi general sub-prefecture. )  

#4
 
As for the former Shimano Village, in 1880 ( Meiji 13 ), an 
administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) was established for 
Shikishima-nai village and Nozuka village.  In 1909 ( Meiji 42 ), 
Shimano Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was established by the 
merger of Shikishimanai and Nozuka villages.  The name ``Shimano'' was 
a contraction of Shiki-shima-nai and No-zuka. 

( UTF code ; Nozuka = \u91CE\u675F.  Shikishima-nai = 
  \u6577\u5CF6\u5185. )

#5

The Port of Iwanai flourished through herring fishery and the shipping 
of minerals.  ( Nearby towns included Kunitomi Mine and Iwao-nupri 
Sulfur Mine. )  The Port of Iwanai was also a port of call toward the 
Shakotan Peninsula.  In the past, navigating the waters around the 
peninsula was difficult.  

In Japanese, the port of call is called ``kaza-machi-kou,'' which means 
``wind, wait, port.''  Sailors waited for a good wind.  There also was 
a red-light district in Iwanai.  

( UTF code ; ``kaza-machi-kou'' = \u98A8\u5F85\u6E2F. )

( For mineral shipping, see Note No. 6 below. )

( For red-light district, see Note No. 7 below. )

( For shipwreck around the Shakotan Peninsula, see Kamoenai Village in 
  Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )

#6 

The Iwanai Line was operated by a state-owned railway company.  The 
railway was in operation between 1912 and 1985.  The abolition was two 
years before the privatization of the state-owned company.  ( The 
state-owned railway company was Japanese National Railways ( JNR ), 
which was called Kokutesu. )   

In the past, Kunitomi Mine operated in neighboring Kozawa district, 
Kyouwa Town.  Also, Iwao-nupri Sulfur Mine operated on Mt. Iwao-nupuri 
in the Niseko Mountains.  The train carried copper and sulfur from 
these mines to the Port of Iwanai. 
  
A ferry line connected Iwanai and Naoetsu between 1990 and 1999.  The 
former country sign ( gateway sign ) of Iwanai featured a ferry and 
Katana-kake-iwa Rock.  
 
The name ``Katana-kake-iwa'' means ``sword stand, rock.''  This comes 
from the legend that the monk soldier Benkei placed his sword on the 
rock.  It's one of Yoshitune legends in Hokkaidou.  ( Yoshitsune 
refers to the warlord Yoshitsune Minamoto in the 12th century.  Benkei 
Musashibou was his retainer. ) 
 
Near Katana-kake-iwa Rock, there is Kasa-iwa Rock.  The word ``Kasa-
iwa'' means ``Umbrella Rock.''  This strangely shaped rock stands along 
the former national highway.  The current national highway runs through
the Benkei Tunnel.  So, today, ordinary tourists cannot reach the 
umbrella rock.

( Kasa-iwa --> http://maps.gsi.go.jp/#15/42.938690/140.412419/  
  Katana-kake-iwa --> http://maps.gsi.go.jp/#15/42.922916/140.387056/ )

( For monk soldier ( ``sou-hei'' ), see Assabu Town in Hiyama sub-
  prefecture.  )

( For Yoshitsune legend, see Biratori Town in Hidaka sub-prefecture ; 
  Otaru City, Shakotan Town, Suttsu Town, and Tomari Town in Shiribeshi 
  general sub-prefecture. )
 
#7  

The novelist Tsutomu Mizukami ( 1919-2004 ) published a detective novel 
``A Fugitive From The Past'' in 1962.  The Japanese name is Kiga-
kaikyou, which means ``Hunger Strait.''  The novel depicts poverty, 
disasters, and post-war culture, including Iwanai Great Fire ( Iwanai-
taika ), Touya-maru shipwreck, and Asahi Hot Spring.  

Asahi Hot Spring in Iwanai Town is also called Raiden Hot Spring.  It 
opened in 1844, during the Kouka period of the Edo era.  The name of 
the hot spring is listed on the map publihed by Takeshirou Matsuura in 
1859, during the Ansei period of the Edo era ( http://
koukita.github.io/touzaiezo/map/ ).

Iwanai Great Fire occurred in 1954 ( Shouwa 29 ).  It was driven by 
the wind of Typhoon Marie.  About 80 percent of the houses in the 
center of Iwanai Town were burned.  Typhoon Marie is also called 
Touya-maru Typhoon.  Touya-maru shipwreck occurred during the typhoon 
and 1,155 people died.

( For Touya-maru shipwreck, see Hakodate City in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. )

Another Iwanai Great Fire had occurred in 1894 ( Meiji 27).  After the 
fire, Susukida red-light district was established.  A red-light 
district is an urban area where sex-oriented businesses is found.  It's 
also called red-line ( ``aka-sen'' ) district in Japan.  

( UTF code ; Susukida = \u8584\u7530. )

In Iwanai, sex-oriented businesses were scattered in the built-up area. 
They were concentrated in a district after the great fire in the Meiji 
period.  That was urban planning.  

* * * 
 
As for literature, the novelist Souseki Natsume ( 1867-1916 ) moved his 
family register ( ``koseki'' ) from Toukyou to Iwanai, Hokkaidou.  
Nominally, he was a resident of Iwanai, during the time from his 
graduated from Imperial University ( now the University of Tokyo ) 
until two years before his death.
 
Souseki Natsume avoided military service.  He transferred only his 
family register to Iwanai and continued to live in Toukyou.  At times, 
he lived in Ehime and Kumamoto prefectures, and also in the UK. 

During the Meiji period, non-military residents in Hokkaidou were 
exempt from military service, because they had to develop the land.  
But residents in military settlers' colonies ( Tonden-heison ) in 
Hokkaidou went to wars.

* * *

A novel ``Arising Worries ( Umare-izuru-nayami )'' was written by Takeo 
Arishima ( 1878-1923 ), who graduated from Sapporo Agricultural College 
( now Hokkaidou University ).  

The novel depicts Kinjirou Kida ( 1893-1962 ), who was a painter and 
fisher in Iwanai Town.  The painter made a solo exhibition in Sapporo 
in 1953.  Many of his paintings were burned during Iwanai Great Fire in 
1954. 
  
( Kinjirou Kida --> http://
  ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%9C%A8%E7%94%B0%E9%87%91%E6%AC%A1%E9%83%8E )

( For Takeo Arishima, see Niseko Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

#8 

In 1921, Kikuzou Shimoda bred a cold-resistant asparagus variety in 
Iwanai Town.  In 1922, he succeeded in asparagus cultivation in Maeda 
Village ( now Kyouwa Town ).  In 1924, he founded a company called 
Nihon Asparagus in Iwanai Town.  It exported canned asparagus to the 
USA. 

White asparagus was used in the canned asparagus, rather than green 
asparagus, because the color of the green changed badly in the can.  
White asparagus is cultivated by covering it with soil.

( Kikuzou Shimoda ( 1895-1970 ) was born in Iwanai.  In 1912, he 
  graduated from Private Pharmacy School in Toukyou ( now Toukyou 
  University of Pharmacy and Life Sciences ).  In 1948, he got a Doctor 
  of Agriculture from Hokkaidou University for his study on crayfish.
  
  As for crayfish study, in 2018, Mr. Kazunori Tanaka in Regional 
  Science Course, Graduate School of Letters, Hokkaidou University, 
  submitted a doctoral dissertation named ``Regional Ecology In The 
  Conservation Of Imperiled Japanese Crayfish ( `Cambar oides 
  japonicas' ) In Hokkaidou.''  I was one of examiners. )

* * *

Dr. Katsuhiko Yamashita, who is Professor Emeritus of Hokkaidou 
University of Education, said that he had seen old photographs of 
asparagus cultivation in Iwanai Town.  They showed that the white 
asparagus was harvested around sunrise, because asparagus turns green 
during the day.

The photograph also showed that students cut asparagus, putting their 
hands under the ground.  In the past, students helped farming as part 
of school school events.  It was called ``en-nou'' ( supporting, 
agriculture ). 

( For supporting agriculture by student, see Yuubetsu Town in Okhotsk 
  general sub-prefecture ). 

Asparagus is a plant in the lily family.  It was imported to Japan 
during the Edo era.  At the time, asparagus was an ornamental plant. 

Nihon Asparagus in Iwanai Town has ceased selling canned asparagus 
since 1996.  Currently, the company sells processed vegetables and 
beverages, including coffee, tea, black tea, and carbonated drinks 
( Fanta and Guarana ).

( UTF code ; Guarana = Guaran\u00E1. )

( For Guarana, see Nanae Town in Oshima general sub-prefecture. )

Nihon Asparagas has two factories.  They are located in Iwanai Town, 
Shiribesi general sub-prefecture, and Chitose City, Iburi general sub-
prefecture.  Iwanai factory produces processed vegetables.  Chitose 
factory produces beverages including Suntory's products.

The company once owned a factory in Biratori Town in Hidaka sub-
prefecture.  The factory was handed over Biratori Town and now produces 
tomato juice.

#9  
 
In 2001, the company operating Niseko Iwanai International Ski Resort 
withdrew due to a decline in visitor numbers.  In 2002, local 
volunteers began operating the ski resort.  The number of visitors from 
Taiwan Island and the Chinese continent gradually increased.  In 2017, 
a company took over the operation of the ski resort.  

And so, Niseko Iwanai International Ski Resort was revived.  This ski 
resort features panoramic view of the Sea of Japan and the Shakotan 
Peninsula.
  
* * *

A ski boom occurred during the bubble economy period, which lasted from 
the late 1980s to the early 1990s.  It was a time when the children of 
the baby boomers were becoming university students.  

( In Japanese, the children of the baby boomers are calld ``dankai-
  junia.''  The word ``dankai'' means the baby boomers who were born 
  just after the Second World War. )  

During the ski boom, at famous ski resorts like Naeba, skiers had to 
wait an hour to board a small lift.  Even an indoor ski ground opened 
in Urayasu City, where Toukyou Disneyland is located.  

Professor Touru Ikeda of Faculty of Letters, Hokkaidou University, 
said, ``During the ski boom in Hokkaidou, ski resorts could earn their 
annual operation costs in December.  Income from January to March 
resulted in a profit.'' 

( For ski resorts, see Kucchan Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

( Single of the chapter ; `Orlando Goni ( Orlad Goni's Tango )'' by 
  Troilo --> http://youtu.be/lKf68q5B4Nk )

|                  |
|      TOMARI      |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 31  |
 ------------------
REA 82 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 77, CULTIVATED X ; COUNTY = FURUU ; POP 
2,376 ( 1990 ), 1,883 ( 2010 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = WARRIOR'S HELMET. 

( UTF code ; Furuu = \u53E4\u5B87. ) 

LOCATION   
 
Tomari ( To-ma-ri ) is a village in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture, 
central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Tomari is located at the root of the 
Shakotan Peninsula.  The area is small and mountainous.  Forests occupy 
94 percent. 

( UTF code ; Tomari = \u6CCA. )

NAME  

The name ``Tomari'' comes from ``moyre-tomari'' in Ainu language.  It 
means ``quiet, wharf.''  It signified the inlet of the Moheru River. 

The name ``Tomari'' was introduced into Ainu language from Japanese 
language.  It signifies a good anchorage.  ( The word ``anchorage'' 
means a place for vessels to anchor. )  #1

( In Japanese ; ``Anchorage'' is ``funama.'' ( old ) )

( UTF code ; ``funama'' = \u821F\u6F97. )

HISTORY  
 
From the Edo era to the Taishou period, this place prospered from 
herring fishing.  The first record of herring fishing dates back to 
1594, during the Azuchi-momoyama period ( before the Edo era ). 
 
After the Meiji period, in 1880 ( Meiji 13 ), an administrative office 
( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) was established for four villages ; Horikappu, 
Kayanuma, Shibui, and Usubetsu.  Another administrative office was 
established for three villages ; Okishinai, Sakazuki, and Tomari.

( UTF code ; Horikappu = \u5800\u682A.  Kayanuma = \u8305\u6CBC. 
  Okishinai = \u8208\u5FD7\u5185.  Sakazuki = \u76C3.  Shibui = 
  \u6E0B\u4E95.  Usubetsu = \u81FC\u5225. ) 
 
In 1882 ( Meiji 15 ), Kayanuma village merged Usubetsu village.  
Horikappu village merged Shibui village.  In 1909 ( Meiji 42 ), Tomari 
Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was established by the merger of 
Horikappu, Kayanuma, Okishinai, Sakazuki, and Tomari villages. 

In 1923 ( Taishou 12 ), Tomari Village ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established. 

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Matsumae 
  Town. )

VILLAGE HALL, TRAFFIC, POPULATION  

The village hall of Tomari is located along the coast of the Sea of 
Japan.  It's located near the mouth of the Moheru River.  National 
Route 229 ( Kabuto Line ) runs along the coast.  It's the only road 
that connects Tomari Town with the neighboring municipalities ; 
Kamoenai Village and Kyouwa Town.  #2
 
In the center of the village, herring mansions were built in the 
Meiji and the Taishou periods.  Two of them are preserved in Tomari 
Village.  One was moved to Otaru City in Shiribeshi general sub-
prefecture.  #3 

Tomari was also a coal mining village.  Kayanuma Coal Mine was in 
operation from 1856 to 1964.  It was the oldest coal mine in Hokkaidou 
Island along with Sekitan-misaki in Shiranuka Town, Kushiro general 
sub-prefecture.  The population of Tomari Village peaked at 9,439 in 
1950 ( by Population Census ).  #4

INDUSTRY   

Distinctive industries in Tomari are fishing, power generation, and 
sightseeing.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = all crops and animals are X 
( concealed ) in 2015.  STATISTICS OF FISHERIES = yellowtail 253 tons 
in 2017, salmon 190, squid ( flying squid 166, other squid 3 ), atka 
mackerel 65, puffer fish 44, Pacific cod 43, right-eye flounder 35, 

( continue ) --> olive flounder 26, octopus 17, sea urchins 13, walleye 
pollock 4, sea trout 3, mackerel 3, sand lance 3, crab 1, seaweed 1, 
tuna 1, herring 0, other fish 91+X, other marine animals X. 

The Tomari Nuclear Power Plant was completed in 1989.  It's the only 
nuclear power plant in Hokkaidou Island.  It has three reactors and 
boasts the highest power output in the island, but it has been stopped 
since the accident of the Fukushima Daiichi Nuclear Power Station in 
2011.  The reactors are maintained.  #5 

NOTE 

#1 
 
Before 1955, there were three Tomari villages in Hokkaidou.  ( 1 ) 
Tomari Village in Furuu county, Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture.  
( This is mentioned in this chapter. ) ( 2 ) Tomari Village in Hiyama 
county, Hiyama sub-prefecture.  This was merged to Esashi Town in 1955.
 
( 3 ) Tomari Village in Kunashiri county.  This is located in Kunashir 
Island.  Russian people live there after 1945.  This is a nominal 
municipality.  

There also are Tomari districts in Shimamaki Village and Shakotan Town 
in Shiribeshi sub-prefecture.  Other than these, there are many places 
including the word ``Tomari'' in Hokkaidou.

* * *

As for Kunashir Island, I taught the geography of Japan at Hokkaidou
University.  Exchange students from Russia took the course.  One day, 
one of them pointed to a large map that I hung on the wall and said ; 
``This map is funny.  I don't care, but Russians live in the island.  
If they see this map, they feel sad.''

She pointed to Kunashir Island.  I replied, ``Hokkaidou University is a 
national university.  I could buy only this map.''  Kunashir Island is 
a disputed island.

#2  

The name ``Kabuto'' means ``warrior's helmet'' in Japanese.  Cape 
Kabuto is a scenic site in the Shakotan Penninsula.   Kabuto Rock 
protrudes from Cape Kabuto.  These are two legends about the rock ; 

``A long time ago, Yoshitsune Minamoto left his helmet on the cape.  
  The helmet became a rock.'' 

``A long time ago, an Ainu woman hid a golden helmet in a cave.  She 
  died without telling anyone about it.  The golden helmet turned into 
  a rock and appeared on the surface.''

( For gold, see Naka-tonbetsu Town in Souya general sub-prefecture ; 
  Akaigawa Village and Shimamaki Town in Shiribehi general sub-
  prefecture. )
 
( For Yoshitsune legend, see Biratori Town in Hidaka sub-prefecture ; 
  Iwanai Town, Otaru City, Shakotan Town, and Suttsu Town in 
  Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )
 
#3

In Tomari Village, there were more than 50 herring houses ( ``nishin-
ban-ya'' ) during the Meiji Period.  Among them, large ones were called 
herring mansions ( ``nishin-goten'' ).  Some of the herring mansions 
have been preserved to this day.  The roof of a herring mansion has an 
observatory ( ``bourou'' ) for spotting schools of herring.

( UTF code ; ``bourou'' = \u671B\u697C.  ``nishin-ban-ya'' = 
  \u9C0A\u756A\u5C4B.  ``nishin-goten'' = \u9C0A\u5FA1\u6BBF. )
  
Herring mansions are made of wood.  Pillars and beams are large and 
heavy.  They are made of local trees such as ash and castor aralia.  

( In Japanese ; ``Ash'' tree is ``tamo.''  ``Castor aralia'' tree is 
``sen'' or ``hariguiri.'' )

Today, It's difficult to find such large and good trees in Hokkaidou 
Island, because they were exported to abroad and Honshuu Island.  
( Cf. A herring mansion in Suttsu Town is made of wood imported from 
Honshuu Island. )

( For large and good tree, see the chapter of Hokkaidou University 
  Ranch in Shin-hidaka Town, Hidaka sub-prefecture. ) 
 
A herring mansion has rooms for the family of the fishing ground owner. 
The owner was called ``oyakata'' or ``amimoto'' in Japanese.  A typical 
herring mansion also has large rooms for migrant fishermen.  

The seasonal fishermen were called ``yan-shuu'' in Japanese.  Migrant 
fishermen stayed in herring mansions during the fishing season, which 
lasted from March to May.  Within the yard of a herring mansion, there 
are processing facilities.  Herring were boiled, dried, and become 
herring fertilizer.
 
( In Japanese ; ``Herring fertilizer'' is ``nishin-kasu.'' ) 

( For herring fishing, see Yoichi Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

( For migrant fisherman and farewell party ( ``ago-kakure'' ), see 
  Kamoenai Village in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )

( For the name ``yan-shuu,'' see Kamoenai Village in Shiribeshi general
  sub-prefecture. ) 

#4
 
Kayanuma Coal Mine in Tomari Village opened in 1864, during the Edo 
period.  The coal was sold to American and English steam ships.  In the 
Meiji period, a British engineer observed Japanese labors at the coal 
mine.  They were using pickaxes and rope basket.  So, the engineer 
suggested to use a railroad to carry the coal.  

( UTF code ; Kayanuma = \u8305\u6CBC. ) 

( In Japanese ; ``Pickaxes'' is ``tsuruhashi.''  ``Rope basket'' is 
  ``mokko.'' )

The Meiji government built the Kayanuma Coal Mine Railway ( Kayanuma-
tankou-kidou ) in 1869 ( Meiji 2 ).  Although it was a minecart, it 
was the first railway in Japan.  

( In Japanese ; ``Minecart'' is ``torokko.'' )

#5 

The once-flourishing herring fishing and coal mining ended.  Tomari 
Village invited a nuclear power station.  The following is excerpted 
from ``Tomari Village History 2 ( Tomari-son-shi 2 )'' published in 
2001.

The village's financial results reached a record high of 5.2 billion 
yen in 1993, following the open of the nuclear power plant.  Of this, 
71 percent was government's subsidy and tax revenue from the plant.  
The subsidy was the Grant for Measures to Promote Power Plant Siting 
( Denguen-ricchi-sokushin-taisaku-koufu-kin ).

In Hokkaidou, these four municipalities attracted a nuclear power 
plant ;  
( 1 ) Tomari Village in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture ; In 1979, 
the construction of a nuclear power plant was decided.  The site was 
Horikappu district.  In 1989, the power plant started operation.

( 2 ) Kyouwa Town in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture ; Kashiwagui 
in Miyaoka district in Kyouwa Town was the final competing candidate. 
Kashiwagui is adjacent to Horikappu district in Tomari Village, where 
the power plant was constructed.  Kashiwagui has a fault on the 
geological map.

( In Japanese ; ``Geological fault'' is ``dan-sou.'' )

( 3 ) Shimamaki Village in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture ; The 
location is far from Sapporo City, which is the major consumer of 
electricity in Hokkaidou Island.

( 4 ) Hamamasu Village ( now Ishikari City ) in Ishikari sub-
prefecture ; The ground is weak for the construction of a nuclear power 
plant. 
  
Before the construction of the nuclear power plant, there were 
opposition movements.  In December 1981, a four-day public hearing was 
held.  During those days, 7,900 protesters, using 122 buses, gathered 
in Tomari Village.  

A lunch box shop in the village sold 4,000 meals over 4 days.  The 
number of temporary toilets was 110.  Among them, Hokkaidou Prefectural 
Police ( Dou-kei ) prepared 102.  All Hokkaidou Council of Workers' 
Union ( Zen-dou-roukyou ), which was the major protest organization, 
prepared only 8.

* * *
 
Atomic energy was a dream for the small country.  Japanese people have 
imported fossil fuels such as oil, gus, and coal.  Atomic energy isn't 
a fossil fuel.  It's like a sun.  After a large earthquake, all 
nuclear power plants in the country stopped.  Atomic energy was too 
early for them to use. 

( Single of the chapter ; ``Azure'' by Spangle Call Lilli Line.  -->
  http://youtu.be/OlqC1BBVk70 )

|     KAMOENAI     |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 32  |
 ------------------
AREA 148 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 138, CULTIVATED X ; COUNTRY = FURUU ; POP 
1,596 ( 1990 ), 1,122 ( 2010 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 8.9 C ( AV 1981-
2010 ), PRECIPI 1,201 MM ; COUNTRY SIGN = DRAGON. 

( UTF code ; Furuu = \u53E4\u5B87. ) 

LOCATION  
 
Kamoenai ( ka-mo-e-na-i ) is a village in Shiribeshi general sub-
prefecure, Central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Kamoenai is located in 
the western tip of the Shakotan Peninsula.  

( UTF code ; Kamoenai = \u795E\u6075\u5185. )

Ninety-three percent of the area is covered by forest.  Ninety percent 
of the forest is national forest.  The dominant vegetation consists of 
broad-leaved deciduous trees.  

NAME 

The name ``Kamoenai'' is derived from ``kamuy-nay'' in Ainu language.  
It means ``god, stream.''  According to ``Place Names Of Hokkaidou 
( Hokkaidou-no-chimei )'' published by Hidezou Yamada in 1984, ``kamuy-
nay'' means a stream in Nagaya-sawa district near the mouth of the 
Furuu River.  

The word ``kamuy'' in Ainu language means god.  It often refers to 
bear.  Bears may have appeared along the stream.  #1 

HISTORY 

In the beginning of the 17th century, during the Edo era, a trading 
place called Furuu-basho was established.  The location was near the 
mouth of the Furuu River.  The place flourished for herring fishing. 

After the Meiji period, in 1872 ( Meiji 5 ), administrative offices 
( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) were established in Kamoenai village to the south 
and also in Sannai village to the north.  In 1888 ( Meiji 21 ), 
Kamoenai merged Sannai.  In 1906 ( Meiji 39 ), Kamoenai Village ( 2nd-
class municipality ) was established. 

( UTF code ; Sannai = \u73CA\u5185. )

( For 2nd-class municipality, see the note of Matsumae Town. )

VILLAGE HALL, TRAFFIC, POPULATION 

The village hall of Kamoenai is located near the mouth of the Furuu 
River.  National Route 299 passes between the sea and the mountains of
the Shakotan Peninsula.  This municipality thrived through herring 
fishing before the 1920s.  There are four fishing ports today ; 
Akaishi, Kamoenai, Kawashira, and Sannai.  #2  #3

( UTF code ; Akaishi = \u8D64\u77F3.  Kawashira = \u5DDD\u767D. )

The population of Kamoenai Village is the second smallest among the 
municipalities in Hokkaidou Prefecture.  ( The smallest is Otoineppu 
Village in Kamikawa general sub-prefecture. )  The Kamoenai Village's 
website features information on migration, saying ``The unexplored area 
closest to Sapporo ( Sapporo-kara-ichiban-chikai-hikyou ).''

( In Japanese ; ``Unexplored area'' is ``hikyou.'' ) 
 
INDUSTRY   

Distinctive industry in Kamoenai is fishery.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE 
= all crops and animals are - ( zero ) in 2015 ; STATISTICS OF 
FISHERIES = salmon 94 tons in 2017, Pacific cod 61, yellowtail 44, atka 
mackerel 44, octopus 36, right-eye flounder 30, sand lance 26, 
 
( continue ) --> sea urchins 26, olive flounder 21, squid ( flying 
squid 17, other squid 3 ), shrimp/prawns 3, seaweed 2, mackerel 1, sea 
trout 1, shellfish 1, herring - ( zero ), other fish 112, and other 
marine animals 10. 

The roadside station on National Route 299 is named ``Osu-koi ! 
Kamoenai.''  The word ``osukoi'' was a chant by fishermen during 
herring fishing.  The country sign ( gateway sign ) of Kamoenai shows 
the dragon god that fishermen prayed for safety.  The dragon looks like 
Shenron ( dragon god ) from the cartoon Dragon Ball.  #4 

( As for Dragon Ball, it was a famous Japanese cartoon.  When I visited 
  the USA in 1999, I turned on the TV in my motel room and found that 
  Dragon Ball was broadcast.  When I visited Brazil in 2001, I turned 
  the TV in my hotel room and found the same situation.  Many 
  international students in Japan like Japanese cartoons. )

NOTE 
 
#1
 
The Kanji characters for Kamoenai are difficult to pronounce.  The word 
``kamuy-nay'' in Ainu language means ``god, stream.''  The Kanji 
characters for ``kamo-e-nai'' means ``god, bless, inside.''  I don't 
know other case where the Kanji character for ``god'' ( ``kami'' ) is 
pronounced as ``kamo.''

( UTF code ; ``kami'' = \u795E. )

The county's name is Furuu.  The Kanji characters  for Furuu are also 
difficult to pronounce.  The name comes from ``fure-nay'' in Ainu 
language, which means ``red, stream.''  In the north of Kamoenai 
Village, there is Akaishi-mura district.  The word ``akaishi'' is 
Japanese and means ``red, stone.''

( For place name including ``red,'' see Akaigawa Village in Shiribeshi 
  general sub-prefecture. )
   
#2

National Route 229 runs along the Sea of Japan.  It connects Otaru City 
in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture and Esashi Town in Oshima general 
sub-prefecture.  It runs along the coast of the Shakotan Peninsula.  

Construction of the national highway was completed in 1996.  The final 
phase took place at the border between Kamoenai Village and Shakotan 
Town. 

Along national Route 229 in Kamoenai Village, there are Sai-no-kawara 
Coast and Shishamo-nai Falls.  The name ``Sai-no-kawara'' is Japanese, 
which means ``west, of, shore.''  The pronunciation is the same as the 
word that means a shore between this world and the afterlife. 
 
In the past, the waters around the Shakotan Peninsula were treacherous 
for navigation.  Shipwrecks were washed ashore at Sai-no-kawara Coast. 

( UTF code ; Sai-no-kawara ( west, of, shore ) in Kamoenai = 
  \u897F\u306E\u6CB3\u539F.  ``sai-no-kawara'' ( pray, of, shore ), 
  which means a shore between this world and the afterlife = 
  \u8CFD\u306E\u6CB3\u539F. )

( ``The Tale Of The Taira Family ( Heike-mono-gatari )'' is an oral 
  tale from the 12th century.  In the tale, the afterlife is located in 
  the west.  It's called ``joudo,'' which means ``paradise.'')  

( For The Tale Of The Taira Family, see Nihei lab 2026_w ; Otaru City 
  in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. ) 

Shishamonai Falls is located near Sai-no-kawara Coast.  The name 
``Shihamo-nai'' comes from ``si-samo-nai'' in Ainu language, which 
means ``true, Japanese people, stream.''  Ainu people said, ``Japanese 
people died there and bad Ainu people ate the flesh.''

National Route 229 that runs around the Shakotan Peninsula opened in 
1996.  Even using the national highway, ordinary tourists cannot access 
Sai-no-kawara Coast and Shishamonai Falls, because the motorway runs 
through a tunnel.  

The Port of Suttsu and the port of Iwanai are located to the south.  
They were ports of call before the Shakotan Peninsula.  ( Suttsu and 
Iwanai towns are located in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )

( In Japanese ; ``Port of call'' is ``kaza-machi-kou,'' which means 
  ''wind, waiting, port.'' )

( For port of call, see Suttsu Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )
 
* * *

As for shipwreck, SOS is a distress signal in Morse code.  Morse code 
is a telecommunication method that encodes text characters.  It was 
developed in the 1830s in the USA.  It became an international standard 
in 1865 ( http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morse_code ). 

SOS is interpreted as Save Our Ship.  SOS was transmitted from the 
Titanic that sank in April 1912 ( http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SOS ).  
About 1,500 people died.  SOS was also transmitted from the Touya-maru 
that sank in September 1954.  1,155 people died.  

( For Touya-maru, see Hakodate City in Oshima general sub-prefecture. ) 

#3 

Herring pens ( herring pools ) still remain along the coast in Kamoenai 
Village and neighboring Shakotan Town.  Those were used to preserve the 
herring before processing.  Herring shoals came to the coast to spawn.  
Big catches continued.  
 
( In Japanese ; ``Herring pen'' is ``fukuroma.'' )
 
The owners of fishing grounds borrowed money and constructed herring 
pens.  Stones were quarried in winter.  Herring pens were constructed 
from early spring.  ( by ``History Of Kamoenai'' http://
www.vill.kamoenai.hokkaido.jp/hotnews/detail/00000203.html  last 
accessed in August 2026. )

( For herring pen, see Shakotan Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

( For stone transportation in winter, see the note of Naie Town in 
  Sorachi general sub-prefecture. )

#4   

Herring fishing took place from spring to early summer.  Seasonal 
fishermen came to ports in Hokkaidou Island.  They came mainly from 
the Touhoku region and the Hokuriku regions in the Honshuu Island.  
Seasonal fishermen were called ``yan-shuu,'' ``yan-shu,'' or 
``ami-ko.'' 

( UTF code ; ``amiko'' = \u7DB2\u5B50.  ``yan-shuu'' = ``yan-shu'' = 
  \u30E4\u30F3\u8846. )

As for the name ``yan-shuu,'' the word ``yan'' comes from ``ya-un'' in 
Ainu language, which means ``land, exist.''  It signified the people 
who came from Honshuu Island.  The word ``shuu'' is Japanese, which 
means ``group.'' 

The name ``ami-ko'' is Japanese, which means ``net, children.''  It 
signified employees hired by fishing ground owners.  The owners were 
called  ``oya-kata'' ( parent, side )  or ``ami-moto'' ( net, origin ).

( UTF code ; ``ami-moto'' = \u7DB2\u5143.  ``oya-kata'' = 
  \u89AA\u65B9. )

In the Hokkaidou dialect, a farewell party is called ``ago-kakure.'' It 
comes from ``amiko-kakure,'' which means ``amiko'' ( seasonal 
fishermen ), ``kakure'' ( hide ).  It's a coastal language.

( In Japanese ; ``Coastal language'' is ``hama-kotoba.'' ) 

( UTF code ; ``hama-kotoba'' = \u6D5C\u8A00\u8449. )

( For herring fishing, see Yoichi Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. ) 

The following is a verse from Souran-bushi, which is a fisherman's song 
sung during herring fishing ;
 
``Yaaren-souran-souran  Souran-souran-souran  Hai-hai,
  Okino-kamomega  Mono-iu-naraba  Tayori-kiitari-kikasetari''

  ( Yaaren-souran-souran  Souran-souran-souran  Hai-hai,
    If seagulls offshore could talk, I would send and receive 
    messages ).

Seasonal fishermen sang this song as they pulled their nets.  There 
were no telegrams or electronic mails.  They wanted to send messages to 
their girlfriends.

( For Souran-bushi ( old song for herrig fishery ), see Yoichi Town in 
  Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )

Songs were born at ports.  Famous one is Tango from Buenos Aires.  The 
rhythm is passionate, but it has a mood of melancholy, which evokes 
memories of the distant homeland.  An example is ``La Cumparsita ( The 
Small Parade )'' by Firpo.  --> http://youtu.be/cYKl79RQIz4 

( Single of the chapter ; ``El Chino Pantaleon ( Curly-haired Lion-like 
  Man's Tango )'' by Canaro.  --> http://youtu.be/eoBPG7D1ME8 )

|                  |
|     SHAKOTAN     |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 33  |
 ------------------
AREA 238 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 187, CULTIVATED 8 ; COUNTY = SHAKOTAN ; 
POP 4,012 ( 1990 ), 2,516 ( 2010 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 7.5 C ( AV 1981-
2010 ), PRECIPI 1,882 MM ; COUNTRY SIGN = CAPE & FLOWER. 
 
LOCATION 

Shakotan ( sha-ko-tan ) is a town in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture, 
central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Shakotan is located at the tip of 
the Shakotan Peninsula. 

( UTF code ; Shakotan = \u7A4D\u4E39. )
 
NAME

The name ``Shakotan'' comes from ``sak-kotan'' in Ainu language.  It 
means ``summer, village.''  It signified the mouth of the Shakotan 
River.  Ainu people stayed there in summer for fishing.  

HISOTRY  

In 1706, during the Edo era, the Matsumae Domain established two 
trading places ( ``basho'' ).  They were Shakotan-basho near Cape 
Shakotan and Bikuni-basho to the east.  Herring fishing flourished from 
the early 18th century to the early 20th century. 

After the Meiji period, in 1880 ( Meiji 13 ), the county office 
( ``gun-yakusho'' ) of Bikuni was established in Kodomari village.  It 
covered other four villages ; Atsutoma, Fumi, Funama, and Horomui.

( UTF code ; Atsutoma = \u539A\u82EB.  Bikuni = \u7F8E\u56FD.  Fumi = 
  \u5A66\u7F8E.  Funama = \u8239\u6F97.  Horomui = \u5E4C\u6B66\u610F. 
  Kodomari = \u5C0F\u6CCA. ) 

In the same year, an administrative office ( ``kouchou-yakuba'' ) was 
established in Hizukasa village, Shakotan county.  It covered other 
four villages ; Desaki, Irika, Nishikawa, and Nozuka.

( UTF code ; Desaki = \u51FA\u5CAC.  Hizukasa = \u65E5\u53F8.  Irika = 
  \u5165\u8238.  Nishikawa = \u897F\u6CB3.  Nozuka = \u91CE\u585A. )  

In the same year, an administrative office was established in Raikishi 
village, Shakotan county.  It covered other two villages ; Kouzaki 
and Yobetsu.  

( UTF code ; Kouzaki = \u795E\u5CAC.  Raikishi = \u6765\u5CB8. 
  Yobetsu = \u4F59\u5225. ) 

In 1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Bikuni Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established by the merger of five villages in Bikuni county ( Atsutoma, 
Bikuni, Fumi, Funama, and Horomui ).

In 1906 ( Meiji 39 ), Irika Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established by the merger of four villages in Shakotan county ( Desaki, 
Hizukasa, Irika, and Nozuka ).  

In the same year, Yobetu Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established by the merger of four villages in Shakotan county 
( Kouzaki, Nishikawa, Raikishi, and Yobetsu ).  

In 1909 ( Meiji 42 ), Bikuni Town ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established.  In 1956 ( Shouwa 31 ), Shakotan Town was established by 
the merger of Bikuni Town, Irika Village, and Yobetsu Village.  

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Matsumae 
  Town. ) 
 
TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC   

The town hall of Shakotan is located near the mouth of the Bikuni 
River.  It's near the ridge that makes a border with Furubira Town.  
National Route 229 ( Raiden-kokudou ) runs along the coast.  The 
national highway connects built-up areas in the former municipalities 
on the Shakotan Peninsula.  #1  #2

The former municipalities flourished from herring fishing.  In spring, 
shoals of herring came to the shore for spawn.  The blue water turned 
white due to sperm (su-pe'rm).

INDUSTRY  

Distinctive industries in Shakotan are fishing and sightseeing.  
STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = pasture 443 hectares in 2015, fodder 52, 
tomatoes 3, beans 1, other vegetables 51+X ( pumpkins, sweet corn, 
asparagus ), and paddy rice - ( zero ) ; dairy cows 361 head in 2015. 

STATISTICS OF FISHERIES = squid ( flying squid 749 tons in 2017, other 
squid 12 ), yallowtail 389, atka mackerel 363, walleye pollock 108, sea 
urchins 99, right-eye flounder 97, octopus 95, Pacific cod 52, 

( continue ) --> mackerel 49, shrimp/prawns 47, sand lance 46, salmon 
45, olive flounder 18, puffer fish 11, crabs 3, abalone 2, sea trout 1, 
sharks 1, herring 0, other fish 253+X, and other marine animals X 
( concealed ).  
 
Schools of herring have disappeared since 1933.  Herring fishing was 
like a bubble economy.  As for sightseeing, the sea urchin bowl 
( ``uni-don'' ) is a specialty.  The coast of the Shakotan Peninsula 
has been designated as Niseko-shakotoan-otaru Coast Quasi National Park 
since 1963.  #3  #4

A coastline has steep cliffs and continues along the Shakotan 
Peninsula.  The water is called Shakotan Blue in summer.  #5  #6

NOTE 

#1  
  
On the Shakotan Peninsula, the mountains press against the sea.  The 
construction of the national highway ( National Route 229 ) was 
delayed.  The peninsula has undeveloped areas.  Signs warning of brown 
bears are installed along the walking trails. 

( In Japanese ; ``Brown bear'' is ``higuma.'' ) 

( For National Route 229, see Kamoenai Village in Shiribeshi general 
  sub-prefecture. )j
 
These are odd-shaped rocks on the Shakotan Paninsula ; ( 1 ) Takara-
jima ( Treasure Island ) in front of Cape Ougon ( Cape Gold ) in Bikuni 
district, ( 2 ) Jorokko-iwa ( Woman Rock ) at Cape Shakotan, and ( 3 ) 
kamui-iwa ( God Rock or Bear Rock ) at Cape Kamui. 
  
This is a legend about Takara-jima ; ``A daughter of Ainu chief fell in 
love with an Ainu retainer and met a tragic death.''  ( The name 
Takara-jima,'' which means a treasure island in Japanese, comes from 
the shoals of herring that brought money. ) 

This is a legend about Jorokko-iwa ; ``A daughter of Ainu chief fell in 
love with Yoshitsune Minamoto and met a tragic death.''  ( Yoshitsune 
Minamoto was a Japanese samurai. )

( For Yoshitsune legend, see Biratori Town in Hidaka sub-prefecture ; 
  Otaru City, Iwanai Town, Suttsu Town, and Tomari Village in 
  Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )

In the Edo era, the Matsumae Domain prohibited women from going east 
from the peninsula.  This was said ; ``The spirits of women who died 
tragically cast curses on other women who crossed the waters around the 
Shakotan Peninsula.'' 

The coast east from the Shakotan Peninsula was a frontier during the 
Edo era.  It included present-day Otaru City.  The region had a high 
proportion of men.  ( For a song about the man who wants to take his 
girlfriend east, see Suttsu Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
prefecture. ) 

In the USA, as well, the frontier had a high proportion of men.  The 
population pyramid is called the ``deck gun profile''  ( by N. Yagasaki 
et al. eds. 2003.  ``American High Plains'' published by Kokon-shoin, 
ISBN 4772215727 ).

This is a pyramid-shaped population pyramid.

  Man        #|#     Woman
             #|#
            ##|## 
           ###|### 
          ####|#### 
        ######|###### 
     #########|######### 
  ############|############

This is a deck gun profile-type population pyramid.

  Man         |      Woman
              | 
             #|#
             #| 
     #########|# 
 #############|# 
            ##| 
              |

( For population pyramid, see Otaru City in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. ) 
 
#2 
  
Small fishing ports distribute at streams on the Shakotan Peninsula. 
They thrived from herring fishing.  Some of them were called ``sengoku-
basho ( 1000-koku place )'' in Japanese.  It means that the port had 
1,000 ``koku'' catches of herring per season.  One ``koku'' was 
equivalent to 200 kg of fresh herring.

( Today, fishers use power boats.  The total fish catch in Shakotan 
  Town is 2,498 tons in 2017. ) 
  
( For one ``koku'' of herring, see Otobe Town in Hiyama sub-
  prefecture. )

In the past, during the herring fishing season, conflicts arose among
fishing ground owners.  One of them employed a swordsman named Juutarou 
Sunayama as a bodyguard.  He was killed during a quarrel.

Chiharu Matsuyama, a singer-songwriter and the great-grandson of 
Juutarou Sunayama, wrote a poem to him.  A stone monument engraved with 
the poem is installed at Cape Ougon, in Bikuni district, Shakotan 
Town.  It reads ; 

``Sora-wo-motome  Umi-wo-motome, 
  ( Seeking the sky, seeking the sea, ) 
 
  Bikuni-no-satoni  Nemurini-tsuku,
  ( Falling asleep in the village of Bikuni. ) 

  Aiwo-wasurena  Yumewo-wasurena.''
  ( Forget love.  Forget dreams. )

#3 

``Souran-bushi'' is a fisher's song from the Edo era.  It was sung by 
fishermen when they removed herring from the net.  The song originated 
in fishing villages distributed from the Shakotan Peninsula to present-
day Yoichi Town.  These are some lyrics ; 

``Yaaren-souran-souran  Souran-souran-souran  Hai-hai,
  Koyoi-hitoyo-wa  Donsu-no-makura  Asuwa-defune-no  Nami-makura.''

  ( Yaaren-souran-souran  Souran-souran-souran  Hai-hai,
    Tonight, I sleep on a pillow of damask.  Tomorrow, I board a boat, 
    a pillow of waves. )

  ( The phrase ``a pillow of damask'' alludes ``deai-jaya'' in the Edo 
    era, which means ``meeting, cafe.''  It was like today's ``love 
    hotel.''  http//en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Love_hotel )

``Yaaren-souran-souran  Souran-souran-souran  Hai-hai,
  Otoko-dokyou-nara  Goshaku-no-karada  Donto-noridase  Namino-ue.''
  ( Yaaren-souran-souran  Souran-souran-souran  Hai-hai,
    You are a brave man.  150 centimeters tall.  Get on the waves! ).

  ( As for ``150 centimeters tall,'' the average Japanese man was about 
    150 centimeters in the Edo era.  They didn't drink milk.  ( Dutch 
    people, who drink the most milk in the world, are the tallest in 
    the world. ) )

The season of herring fishing continued from spring to early summer.  
The seasonal fishermen were called ``yan-shuu.''  `It's a coastal 
language ( dialect ) in Hokkaidou.

( In Japanese ; ``Coastal language is ``hama-kotoba.'' )

( For Souran-bushi ( fisher's song ), see Yoichi Town in Shiribeshi 
  general sub-prefecture. )  

( For ``yan-shuu'' ( seasonal fishermen ), see Kamoenai Village in 
  Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )
 
As for fisher's song, this is ``Funa-uta,'' a song from the Shouwa 
period ;  

``Osake-wa  Nurumeno-kanga-ii, 
  ( Lukewarm rice wine is fine, )

  Sakana-wa  Abutta-ika-de-ii, 
  ( For side dish, baked squid is fine, )    

  Akari-wa  Bonyari-tomorya-ii, 
  ( Dim light is fine, )
 
  Shimijimi-nomeba  Shimijimi-to  Omoide-dake-ga  Iki-sugiru,
  ( Sipping slowly, only memories drift, )

  Namidaga-porori-to  Koboreta-ra  Utaidasu-no-sa  Funa-uta-wo.'' 
  ( When a tear drops, I sing a fisher's song. ) 

#4 
  
There are herring pens ( herring pools ) along the coast of Shakotan 
Town and neighboring Kamoenai Village.  Herring pens were used for 
preserving herring during the fishing season that continued from spring 
to early summer.  Herring pens are called ``fukuroma.''  The word 
``fukuro-ma'' is Japanese and means ``bag, pond.''
 
( UTF code ; Fukuro-ma = \u888B\u6F97. )

In the past, when spring came, schools of herring would arrived to 
the rocky reefs to spawn.  Fishers constructed herring pens because the 
quantity of herring was too large to catch all at once.  The herring 
were placed in herring pens for seven to ten days before processing. 
 
Herring pens were also used as evacuation ports for fishing boats.  In 
early spring, the southwest wind blows strongly, the sea is rough. 
Fishing boats weren't equipped with engines.

( For herring pen, see Kamoenai Village in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

( For the shipwreck of herring boats, see Haboro Town in Rumoi sub-
  prefecture. ) 
  
#5 

I heard the word ``Shakotan Blue'' for the first time after I moved to 
Sapporo.  When I was eating ``miso-ramen'' at Chinman, a Chinese 
restaurant next to Hokkaidou University, two young businessmen were 
talking about their summer vacation and Shakotan Blue.

After I came back to my office, I searchd the oldest web-site that 
mentioned the word ``Shakotan Bule.''  These are the results ;

( 1 )  ``Livedoor Blog Diary ; Collecting Country Signs 3'' published 
in July 7th, 1999.  
http://blog.livedoor.jp/kent6tm/archives/50602672.html last accessed in 
June 2019. 

( 2 ) ``Nippon Rent-A-Car Drive Guide ; Summer On The Shakotan 
Peninsula'' published in July 15, 2002.  
http://www.nipponrentacar.co.jp/freeroad/syakotan.htm  last accessed in 
June 2019. 

The word ``Shakotan Blue'' may be new, but the blueness of the sea has 
been a tourist attraction for a long time.  I found the following 
sentences in ``Shakotan Town History ( Shakotan-chou-shi )'' published 
in 1985. 
   
Page 628 ; ``Here, you can fully enjoy the true blue of the sea —- a 
color that is disappearing elsewhere.  Beyond strange rocks and sheer 
cliffs, the cobalt-blue surface of the sea stretches endlessly, while 
the spray of crashing waves contrasts with the deep blue waters, and 
Woman Rock ( Jorokko-iwa ) rises near the beautiful coastline.

Page 627 ; ``Chatsu Beach, with its blue waters clear enough to the 
seabed, bustles with people enjoying swimming and camping in summer.''

( UTF code ; Chatsu = \u8336\u6D25. )

Page 630 ; ``The scenic view of Cape Shakotan and the cobalt-blue sea, 
enjoyed from the newly built observation deck, is truly exceptional.''

The herring fishing was over.  The municipality begun promoting 
tourism.

( When I wrote this section, I listened to ``True Blue'' by Madonna.
  --> http://youtu.be/P51LunEV3Sk )
 
#6

There was a natural aquarium in Bikuni district, Shakotan Town.  It 
opened in 1956.  The closing year is unknown.  A photograph of the 
aquarium appears in ``Shakotan Town History ( Shakotan-chou-shi ),'' 
published in 1985.  The aquarium was build using a sea cave. 
 
Sea caves are called ``kaishoku-dou'' in Japanese.  They are called 
``dondo-ana'' in coastal languages, which means ``don, hall.''  The 
word ``don'' represents the sound of waves splashing into a sea cave.

( UTF code ; Kaishoku-dou = \u6D77\u8755\u6D1E. )

( Photograph of a sea cave --> HTTP://2242.MOOO.COM/POSI/1459.JPG )

The natural aquarium in Shakotan Town was designed by engineer Mr. 
Kawai.  He also designed an aquarium in Mashike Town, Rumoi sub-
prefecture.  Mashike's aquarium opened in 1956, the same year as 
Shakotan's aquarium.  Mashike's aquarium closed in 1964. 
  
There also was a natural aquarium in Yoichi Town, Shiribeshi general 
sub-prefecture.  It opened in September 1954 and closed in 1964.  The 
first aquarium in Hokkaidou is Muroran City Aquarium.  It has been open 
since 1953. 

Otaru Aquarium and Sun-piazza Aquarium are popular among Sapporo 
residents.  Otaru Aquarium is located in Otaru City, Shiribeshi general 
sub-prefecture.  It opened in 1958.  Sun-piazza Aquarium is located in 
Atsubetsu Ward, Sapporo City, Ishikari sub-prefecture.  It opened in 
1982. 

I like the nostalgic signboard of Sun-piazza Aquarium.  It's made of 
spangles.  They sway in the wind.  ``Spangle'' is called ``span-call'' 
in Japanese English.  There used to be span-call signboards along 
national highways in the 1970s.  They didn't use electricity. 
( Spanglle --> http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sequin ) ( se'e-quin )

( When I wrote this section, I listened to ``Cast A Apell On Her'' by 
  Spangle Call Lilli Line.  --> http://youtu.be/9yenonvwXyE )

|                  |
|     FURUBIRA     |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 34  |
 ------------------
AREA 188 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 162, CULTIVATED 1 ; COUNTY = FURUBIRA ; 
POP 4,967 ( 1990 ), 3,611 ( 2010 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = CAMP SITE, SEA, & 
ROCK.  #1
 
LOCATION   

Furubira ( fu-ru-bi-ra ) is a town in Shiribeshi general sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Furubira is located in 
the east of the Shakotan Peninsula.  Most of the area is the basin of 
the Furubira River, which flows to the Sea of Japan.  Forests occupy 86 
percent of the area.  

( UTF code ; Furubira = \u53E4\u5E73. )

NAME  

The name ``Furubira'' is derived from ``hure-pira'' in Ainu language, 
which means ``red, cliff.''  It signified a cliff along the Furubira 
River near the town hall.  #2

HISTORY

In the Edo era, the Matsumae Domain established Furubira-basho ( a 
trading place ).  The place prospered from herring fishing.  After the 
Meiji period, in 1869 ( Meiji 2 ), Furubira county was established.  
The county hall was located in Furubira village.  It also served as 
Furubira branch of Hokkaidou Development Commission.  #3

The population of Furubira county was recorded in 1875.  The following 
are the results ;
  581 people for three villages ; Hamanaka-mura, Oki-mura, Sawae-
      mura, and Utasutsu-mura. 
  446 people for one town and two villages ; Irifune-chou, Kuki-
      mura, and Tarumi-mura. 
   95 people for Komin ( Ainu people ).  

( by Furubira Town 1998.  History of Furubira reading through the 
  chronology ( Nenpyou-de-yomu-furubira-no-rekishi ).  ``Setakamui'' 
  No. 108,  http://www.town.furubira.lg.jp/
  common/img/content/setakamui/setakamui-108.pdf  last accesed in 
  August 2026. )

( UTF code ; Hamanaka = \u6D5C\u4E2D.  Irifune = \u5165\u8239.  Komin = 
  \u53E4\u6C11.  Kuki = \u7FA4\u6765.  Oki = \u6C96.  Sawae = 
  \u6CA2\u6C5F.  Tarumi = \u5782\u7F8E.  Utasutsu = \u6B4C\u68C4. ) 

( There was another Utasutsu Village in the area that is now Suttsu 
  Town, Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )

In 1879 ( Meiji 12 ), five towns were established in the area that is 
now near the town hall of Furubira.  They were Hama-chou ( renamed from 
Hamanaka-mura ), Irifune-chou, Maruyama-chou, Minato-machi ( renamed 
from Tarumi-mura ), and Shinchi-chou. 

( UTF code ; Hama-chou = \u6D5C.  Maruyama-chou = \u4E38\u5C71. 
  Minato-machi = \u6E2F.  Shinchi-chou = \u65B0\u5730. )

In 1880 ( Meiji 13 ), three administrative offices ( ``kochou-
yakuba'' ) were established ; 
   One governed two towns ; Hama-chou and Minato-machi.
   One governed three towns and one village ; Irifune-chou, Maruyama-
      chou, Shinchi-chou, and Kuki-mura.
   One governed three villages ; Oki-mura, Sawae-mura, and 
      Utasutsu-mura.  
   ( --> In total, there were five towns and four villages. ) 

In 1899 ( Meiji 22 ), the administrative office of Hama-chou was 
established.  It governed the five towns and four villages above.  In 
1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Furubira Town ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established by the merger of nine municipalities.  In 1907 ( Meiji 
40 ), Furubira Town ( 1st-class municipality ) was established.  
 
( See the note of Matsumae Town for 1st-class and 2nd class 
  municipalities. )
 
TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC, POPULATION

The town hall of Furubira is located near the mouth of the Furubira 
River.  National Route 229 ( Seta-kamui Line ) runs along the coast. 
The national highway passes the tunnels that were constructed under 
steep sea cliffs at the borders with other municipalities.  ( They are 
Yoichi and Shakotan towns ).  #4
 
The population peaked at 10,073 in 1955 ( by Population Census ).  At 
the time, the economy thrived on fishing and mining.  Manganese was 
extracted from the Inakura-ishi Mine, located upstream of the Furubira 
River.  #5  #6

INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industries in Furubira are fishing, fish processing ( cod 
roe ), and strawberry cultivation.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = paddy 
rice 16 hectares in 2015, strawberries 1, pasture - ( zero ), and 
fodder - ; dairy cows - in 2015.  STATISTICS OF FISHERIES = walleye 
pollock 717 tons in 2017, atka mackerel 702, 
  
( continue ) --> squid ( flying squid 506, other squid 4 ), octopus 
423, salmon 204, Pacific cod 292, right-eye flounder 267, herring 80, 
shrimp/prawns 72, sand lance 38, olive flounder 33, sea urchins 24, 
crabs 17, mackerel 13, shellfish ( abalone 1, other shellfish 2 ), sea 
trout 1, yellowtail 1, and other fish 291. 

In Zenguenji Temple near the town hall, there is a tea tree.  It's the 
northernmost tea tree in the country.  The location is 43 degrees 15 
minutes north latitude.  In the temple, there also are oil paintings of 
``Five Hundred Arhats'' drawn by Takejirou Hayashi ( 1871-1941 ).  He 
drew the 480 paintings from 1919 to 1939.  #7

( UTF code ; Zenguenji = \u7985\u6E90\u5BFA. ) 

( In Japanese ; ``Five Hundred Arhats'' is Gohyaku-rakan. )

( Takejirou Hayashi --> http://
  ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%9E%97%E7%AB%B9%E6%B2%BB%E9%83%8E )

NOTE 

#1  
 
The country sign of Furubira Town depicts the odd-shaped rock ``Seta-
kamui.''  It stands along the coast near the border with Yoichi Town.  

The name ``Seta-kamu'' means ``dog, god'' in Ainu language.  The rock 
looks like a howling dog.  There is an Ainu legend ; ``A dog was 
waiting for its owner.  The owner went fishing, got caught in a storm, 
and didn't return.  The dog howls for a long time, and became a rock.'' 

( For dog, see Setana Town in Hiyama sub-prefecture ; Makkari Village 
  in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture ; Naie Town in Sorachi general 
  sub-prefecture. )

( For shipwreck, see Kamoenai Village in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )     
  
#2

The name ``Furubira'' means``red, cliff'' in Ainu language.  There are 
many places that contain the word ``red,''  both in Japanese and Ainu 
language.  Red is ``aka'' in Japanese and ``fure'' in Ainu language. 

( For place names including ``red,'' see Akaigawa Village in Shiribeshi 
  general sub-prefecture. ) 

#3
 
Furubira-basho was a trading place during the Edo era.  It was located 
at the southern root of Cape Maruayma.  The terrain blocks the 
northwesterly wind.  It was a natural port.  In 1858, Japanese people 
were permitted to overwinter in Furubira-basho.  ( The Edo era 
continued from 1603 to 1868. )

The trading place Furubira-basho was located in an area that 
corresponds to today's districts of Irifune-chou, Maruyama-chou, and 
Shinchi-chou.  

#4

National Route 229 passes the Toyohama Tunnel at the border with Yoichi 
Town.  The tunnel collapsed in 1996.  One bus and two cars were 
destroyed, and 20 people died.  The residents of Furubira used that 
tunnel for shopping, school, and hospital.

( For National Route 229, see Kamoenai Village in Shiribeshi general 
  sub-prefecture. )

#5

Inakura-ishi Mine was discovered in 1885 ( Meiji 18 ).  It became the 
largest manganese mine in the country during the Second World War.  
After the war, production decreased.  In 1970, Inakura-ishi mine was 
merged into Ooe Mine in Niki Town, which was located on the other side 
of the mountain.  In 1984, Ooe Mine closed.  

Manganese was used for zinc-carbon batteries.  Compared to alkaline 
batteries, zinc-carbon batteries have a lower output but a longer 
lifespan.  I have a flashlight that uses zinc-carbon batteries.  It was 
useful during the power outage by the 2018 Hokkaido Eastern Iburi 
Earthquake.

( In Japanese ; ``Zinc-carbon battery'' is ``mangan-kan-denchi.'' )

( For Ooe Mine, see Niki Town in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )  
 
#6 
 
Catches of herring decreased after World War II.  Some fishers in 
Furubira fishing port started North Ocean Fishing.  They caught crabs 
in the northern Pacific Ocean.  ( by ``Seta-kamui'' No. 119  http://
www.town.furubira.lg.jp/common/img/content/setakamui/setakamui-119.pdf
last accessed in August 2026. )

North Ocean Fishing also ended in the 1980s because of the 
establishment of Exclusive Economic Zone ( EEZ ).  EEZ grants the right 
to economic utilization of waters within a range of 200 nautical mile 
( 370 kilometers ).  

After the establishment of EEZ, Japan acquired huge waters to the 
south, and lost fish to the north.  The north waters are rich in 
plankton and abound with fish. 

Large ships sailing for North Ocean Fishing were discontinued.  This 
reminds me of an old Japanese proverb, ``shogyou-mujou.''  It means 
``everything, not permanent.'' 

( In Japanese ; ``North Ocean Fishing'' is Hokuyou-gyogyou. )

( For North Ocean Fishing, see Hakodate City in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. ) 
 
#7 

The northernmost tea tree in the country is planted in the garden of a 
temple.  It's a garden plant, not for making tea.  Takeo Ichikawa 
stated that such a tree was the ``thinking-of-hometown tree.''  ( by T. 
Ichikawa 1993.  ``Four Seasons And Life Of Japan ( Nihon-no-shiki-to-
kurashi )'' published by Kokon-shoin, ISBN 9784772213288 ). 

( In Japanese ; ``The thinking-of-hometown tree'' is ``bou-kyou-ju.'' ) 

( UTF code ; ``bou-kyou-ju'' = \u671B\u90F7\u6A39. )

The northernmost tea tree that I saw was in Vancouver Island.  On a 
south-facing slope at Westholme Tea Company, tea trees were planted. 
I was surprised.  The location was at 48 degrees 51 minutes north 
latitude.  It must be ones of the northernmost tea trees in the world.

( For tea tree, see Nihei lab 2024-2025a. )  

( Single of the chapter ; ``Derecho Viejo ( Old Law )'' by Pugliese.  
  --> http://youtu.be/zciL8C4G8IE )
