|                  |
|       NIKI       |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 35  |
 ------------------
AREA 168 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 127, CULTIVATED 13 ; COUNTY = YOICHI ; 
POP 4,595 ( 1990 ), 3,800 ( 2010 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = APPLE & CHERRY. 
 
LOCATION  

Niki ( ni-ki ) is a town in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture, central 
Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Niki is an inland town.  The area is located 
in the middle reaches of the Yoichi River.  ( The upper reaches are 
Akaigawa Village. )

( UTF code ; Niki = \u4EC1\u6728. )

NAME 

The name ``Niki'' is derived from Takeyoshi Niki.  He was a pioneer. 
He led a migrant group from Tokushima Prefecture in 1879.  He intended 
to launch an indigo industry in Hokkaidou.  The indigo industry 
flourished in Tokushima Prefecture during the Edo era.  It included 
indigo cultivation and indigo dyeing.  #1 
 
( In Japanese ; ``Indigo'' is ``ai.'' )
 
HISTORY
 
In 1879 ( Meiji 12), Takeyoshi Niki from Tokushima Prefecture migrated. 
In 1880 ( Meiji 13), Tei-ichi Awaya from Yamaguchi Prefecture migrated. 
Tei-ichi Awaya also opened a large farm in an area what is now in Niki 
Town and Yoichi Town.  It was Kurokawa-mouri Farm. 
  
( UTF code ; Takeyoshi Niki = \u7AF9\u5409\u4EC1\u6728.  Tei-ichi Awaya 
  = \u8C9E\u4E00\u7C9F\u5C4B. )
 
( For large farm in the Meiji period, see Kyougoku Town in Shiribeshi 
  general sub-prefecture. )
 
In 1881 ( Meiji 14 ), an administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) of 
Niki village was established.  In 1883 ( Meiji 16 ), Ooe village and
Yama-michi villages were established.  The administrative offices of 
these villages were jointly managed by the administrative office of 
Niki village.

The name ``Ooe'' was derived from the Mouri clan that ruled the 
Choushuu Domain ( now Yamaguchi Prefecture ).  The family name of the 
Mouri clan was Ooe.  #2 

The name Yama-michi village was located in the west bank of the Yoichi 
River.  The name ``Yama-michi'' is Japanese, which means ``mountain, 
road.''  As the name suggests, the area was mountainous. #3 

( UTF code ; Ooe = \u5927\u6C5F.  Yamamichi = \u5C71\u9053. )
 
In 1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Ooe Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was 
established by the merger of Niki, Ooe, and Yamamichi.  In 1915 
( Taishou 4 ), Ooe Village ( 1nd-class municipality ) was established.  
In 1964 ( Shouwa 39 ), Niki Town was established.  Ooe was renamed.  #4

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Matsumae 
  Town. ) 

TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC 
 
The town hall of Niki is located in the north of the area.  It's along 
the alluvial plain of the Yoich River.  The site is the same as the 
former village hall of Ooe Village.  The Hakodate Main Line and 
National Route 5 ( Youtei-kukudou ) pass.  There are stations of Niki, 
Shikaribetsu, and Guinzan.  #5 
 
INDUSTRY 

Distinctive industry in Niki is agriculture.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE 
= paddy rice 431 hectares in 2015, orchards 373 ( grapes 133, cherries 
119, apples 37, plums 7, chestnuts 1, other fruits 43+X ), tomatoes 79, 

( continue ) --> buckwheat 48, beans ( adzuki beans 1, soybeans 1, 
other beans 1 ), pasture 3, flowers 1, potates 1, radishes 1, 
strawberries 1, other vegetables 18+X, and other grains 4 ; dairy 
cows - ( zero ) in 2015. 
  
Fruit and rice are the major land uses.  The north ( Niki ) is a fruit-
growing area.  The south ( Ooe ) is a rice-growing area.  The south is 
the former Ooe village.  Today, Farmers cultivate not only rice, but 
also strawberries and cherry tomatoes.  

In the past, the method for drying rice in paddy fields differed 
between the south and the north.  It was a difference in culture. 
Today, rice is dried by machine.  The taste of rice varies depending on
the drying method.

In the north, fruit orchards continues from Yoichi Town.  Yoichi is the 
main fruit producer in Hokkaidou.  Various fruits are cultivated, such 
as grapes, cherries, apples, plums, blueberries, and prunes.  Apple is 
the oldest.  #6  #7

NOTE 

#1

During the Edo era, the Niki family ran an indigo business in the 
Tokushima Domain ( now Tokushima Prefecture ).  In the Meiji period, 
the indigo business suffered setbacks due to rise in herring fertilizer
prices and flooding of the Yoshino River.

( Herring fertilizer was produced in Hokkaidou and transported to 
  Tokushima Domain for indigo cultivation. )

( In Japanese ; ``Herring fertilizer'' is ``nishin-kasu.'' )

( UTF code ; ``nishin-kasu'' = \u9C0A\u7C95. )

In 1879 ( Meiji 12 ), Takeyoshi Niki led 361 people, 101 families, 
and departed from the Port of Tokushima.  They landed in the Port of 
Otaru and started development in an area what is now Niki Town.  The 
migrant group from Tokushima Prefecture was called Tokushima-dantai, 
which means Tokushima group.

According to ``Niki Town History ( Niki-chou-shi ),'' published in 
1968, the second migrant group from Tokushima Prefecture came to 
Hokkaidou Island in 1886.  They intended to settle in an area what is 
now Setana Town.

The second migrant group was scammed for travel expenses in the Port 
of Koube.  They sold their belongings to buy tickets to Hokkaidou.  
They also intended to launch the indigo industry in Hokkaidou.

They contracted cholera on board the ship.  At frist, they thought it 
was seasickness, but it turned out to be cholera.  Thay carried indigo 
dyeing vats.  The dead were packed in the vats.  One dead man was 
packed in a vat.  A dead mother and a child were packed in a vat. 
 
They wanted to bury the dead on land, but there were no ports for the 
cholera ship to disembark.  They could manage to bury the dead at night 
in Niigata Prefecture.  As the death toll rose, the rest were buried at 
sea.  

( In Japanese ; ``Burial at sea'' is ``suisou.'' )
 
The second migrant group from Tokushima Prefecture landed at the Port 
of Otaru and headed south for Setana, but they couldn't reach.  They 
arrived at Niki village along the way and stayed there.  Niki was the 
place where the first migrant group from the same prefecture developed.

Members of the second migrant group died of the infection.  Their 
destination was far from the port.  The dialects spoken varied among 
the migrant groups.  They must have felt relief when arriving at Niki 
village settled by people from their home prefecture.

( In the 1990s, when I was an undergraduate, prefectural associations 
  ( ``kenjin-kai'' ) existed at University of Tsukuba.  The Aomori 
  Prefectural Association performed their traditional Neputa dance at 
  the school festival. )
   
In the Meiji period, cholera was endemic in the country.  In ``haiku'' 
poem, the word ``Cholera ship ( Korela-sen )'' is a seasonal word for 
summer.

The second migrant group from Tokushima Prefecture moved to Hokkaidou 
in June.  It's a humid and warm season in Honshuu Island.  The putrid 
fluid of the dead leaked from cracks of dyeing vats.  The survivors on 
the ship feared that they, too, might contract cholera. 

Today, Tokushima Prefecture is famous for Awaodori dance.  In Niki 
Town, an Awaodori dance association was established in 1976. 

( For development by the Tokushima Domain, see Shin-hidaka Town in 
  Hidaka sub-prefecture. )

#2  
  
Migrants from the former Choushuu Domain ( now Yamaguchi Prefecture ) 
settled on Kurokawa-mouri Farm and Ooe village in the early Meiji 
period.  They included samurai families who were in difficulty for the 
abolition of the domain system.  ( by ``Niki Town History ( Niki-chou-
shi )'' published in 1968 ; by ``A Story That Happened In Yoichi 
No. 56,''  http://
www.town.yoichi.hokkaido.jp/machi/yoichistory/2009/sono56.html
last accessed in August 2026. )

( In Japanese ; ``Abolition of the domain system'' is ``haihan-
  chiken.'' )

( The Choushuu Domain was ruled by the Mouri clan.  The family name 
  of the clan was Ooe.  So, the names of farm and village included 
  ``mouri'' and ``ooe.'' )

( For Kurokawa-mouri Farm, see Kyougoku Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )
 
The fist migrant group from Yamaguchi Prefecture consisted of 95 
people, 19 families.  They started paddy rice cultivation from the 
beginning. 
 
( In Hokkaidou, because of the cold climate, the early migrants 
  conducted Western-style agriuclutre.  In 1873, Kyuuzou Nakayama 
  succeded in paddy rice cultivation in the Ishikari Plain. 

( For Kyuuzou Nakayama, see Kita-hiroshima City in Ishikari sub-
  prefecture. )
  
In the Meiji period, farmers in Ooe village cultivated glutinous rice.  
The varieties were Akague ( red hair ) and Bouzu ( no hair ).  They 
also cultivated a small amount of non-glutinous rice.  This type of 
paddy rice cultivation was common in the Ishikari Plain at the time.

( The Ishikari Plain is located in Ishikari sub-prefecture and Sorachi 
  general sub-prefecture. )
 
( In Japanese ; ``Glutinous rice'' is ``uruchi-mai.''  ``Non-
  glutinous'' rice is ``mochi-mai.'' )

( For Akague ( red hair ) variety, see Kita-hiroshima City in Ishikari
  sub-prefecture. )

( For Bouzu ( no hair ) variety, see Uryuu Town in Sorachi general sub-
  prefecture. ) 
 
#3  

As for the former Yamamichi village, the name ``yama-michi'' means a 
mountain road in Japanese.  It was derived from the Yoichi Mountain 
Road, which was called Yoichi-goe-sandou.  The word ``san-dou'' is also 
pronounced as ``yama-michi.''  The mountain road was constructed in 
1856, towards the end of the Edo era.  

( UTF code ; Yoichi-goe-sandou = 
  \u30E8\u30A4\u30C1\u8D8A\u5C71\u9053. ) 

The Yoichi Mountain Road connected the present-day Iwanai Town and 
Yoichi Town.  Both are located on the Sea of Japan side.  Before the 
open of the road, people moved waters around the Shakotan Peninsula. 

( For shipwreck around the Shakotan Peninsula, see Kamoenai Village in 
  Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )     

The Yoichi Mountain Road was difficult to traverse.  Today, National 
Route 5 and the Hakodate Main Line run along the former mountain road.  
Even in the late 20th century, National Route 5 was difficult to pass 
between Niki Town and Kyouwa Town.  It was a winding road that included 
Inaho Pass.
 
Inaho Tunnel opened under Inaho Pass in 1962.  National Route 5 was re-
constructed and widened.  ( Nowadays, national highways in Hokkaidou 
cross mountain passes via tunnels. )

The name ``Inaho'' means ``rice ear'' in Japanese.  The Kanji 
characters ``rice, ear'' may have suggested the rice cultivation in the 
former Ooe village.  According to the book ``New Niki Town History 
( Shin-Niki-chou-shi ),'' published in 2003,  the name is derived from 
``inau'' in Ainu language.

Inau are religious wooden sticks used by Ainu people.  There are Inau 
sticks in the forest of Hokkaidou University Sapporo Campus.  

( For Inau stick, see Shikabe Town in Oshima general sub-prefecture. )
  
#4 

In 1902, villages of Niki, Ooe, and Yamamichi were merged, and the new 
village was named Ooe.  The merger of Niki in the north and Ooe in the 
south was difficult.  They had different cultures. 

The name Ooe was decided by influential persons.  The reasons are 
as follows.  ( by ``New Niki Town History ( Shin-Niki-chou-shi )'' 
published in 2003. )

( 1 ) Ooe was located in the center of the village area, although it 
      was settled later than Niki village. 
( 2 ) A peasant uprising broke out in Niki village.
( 3 ) The higher-level officials of the Japanese government were 
      occupied by Sacchou-dohi.  

( The word ``Sacchou-dohi'' means the old domains of Satsuma, Choushuu, 
  Tosa, and Hizen.  Those are today's Kagoshima, Yamaguchi, Kouchi, and 
  Saga prefectures. )

( UTF code ; Sacchou-dohi = \u85A9\u9577\u571F\u80A5. ) 
 
The migrants in Ooe village were from the Choushuu Domain.  They had 
connections with high-ranking officials in the country.  Meanwhile, the 
migrants to Niki were from the Tokushima Domain.  In the end of the Edo 
era, the Tokushima Domain participated in the Choushuu Conquest by the 
order of the Tokugawa Shogunate. 
 
When Ooe Village became a town, the name was changed to Niki.  The 
residents in Ooe district ( the former Ooe village ) opposed it, but 
according to the modern, democratic, and majority rule, the name was 
changed to Niki ( by ``New Niki Town History ( Shin-Niki-chou-shi )'' 
published in 2003 ).  ( At times, majority rule undermines harmony. )

Niki district ( the former Niki village ) is adjacent to Yoichi Town 
and close to Otaru City.  The population increased.  Niki apples, a 
specialty of the village, became famous.  There also was Hokkaidou 
Niki High School.  The name was changed from Ooe to Niki.  

( Hokkaidou Niki High School became Hokkaidou Niki High School of 
  Commerce in 1968 and closed in 2012.  Then, Hokkaidou High School of 
  Arts opened at the site in 2014. )
 
#5  
 
As for Guinzan Station, the word ``guin-zan'' is Japanese.  It means 
``silver, mine.''  There were silver mines near the station.  

They were Rubeshibe Mine, Ootomi Mine, Kami-ootomi Mine, and Inaho 
Mine ( by ``Niki Town History ( Niki-chou-shi )'' published in 1968 ; 
by Doi et al. 1968.  Geology and ore deposits of the Ooe district, 
Shiribeshi Province.  ``Report Of The Geological Survey Of Hokkaidou'' 
38 ). 

Those mines were in operation in the Meiji period.  Guinzan Station on 
the Hakodate Main Line opened in 1905 ( Meii 38 ).  Also, in 
neighboring Akaigawa Village, Todoroki Mine that produce silver and 
gold was in operation from 1896 to 1987.  Ores ( o'rz ) were 
transported to Guinzan Station.

The old name of Guinzan was Makunbetsu.  It was derived from ``chirai-
mak-un-pet'' in Ainu language.  It means ``itou'' ( fish ), behind, 
mountain, river.''  It signified the Magunbetsu River that flows in 
Guinzan district.  Today, the river flows through beautiful paddy 
fields.  ( But there is  no ``itou'' fish. )

Itou is a freshwater fish in the salmon family.  Its academic name is 
``Parahucho perryi.''  The word ``perryi'' means Matthew Perry, the 
Commodore of the United States Navy.  He visited Hokkaidou and reported 
the fish to the British biological academy. 

( For ``itou'' ( fish ), see Rankoshi Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

( For Matthew Perry, see Hakodate City in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. ) 

To the north of Guinzan Station, Ooe Mine was located along the upper 
reaches of the Pon-shikaribetsu River.  It operated intermittently from 
1890 to 1984.  At its peak, the mining settlement was home to 200 
households and 4,300 residents.

Ooe Mine was in the former Yamamichi Village.  Manganese and silver 
were produced.  The ores were transported via Shikaribetsu Station on 
the Hakodate Main Line.  

( For manganese mine, see Furubira Town in Shiribeshi-sub prefecture. )

#6 
  
The oldest fruit is apple.  According to ``New Niki Town History 
( Shin-niki-chou-shi ),'' published in 2003 ; 

``In 1880 ( Meiji 13 ), the Hokkaidou Development Commission ( Kaitaku-
  shi ) distributed several dozen apple saplings in the former Niki 
  village.  

  In 1884 ( Meiji 17 ), the apples were harvested.  In 1887 ( Meiji 
  20 ), three farmers bought more than 200 apple saplings in neibouring 
  Yoichi village.

  In 1894 ( Meiji 27 ), apple orchards expanded onto the sloping land.  
  During the Taishou period ( 1912-1922 ), apples became the main crop 
  in the former Niki village.'' 

Apples grown in Japan are descendant of the Western varieties that 
were imported from the USA and Canada during the Meiji period.  They 
grow under cool climates.  

( For fruit cultivation, see Yoichi Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. ) 
 
#7 
  
In the Meiji period, the settlers in Niki village cultivated indigo.  
It was a tradition from their home prefecture, Tokushima.  After the 
1900s, indigo cultivation gradually declined as Indian indigo and 
synthetic indigo from Germany began to be imported.  Settlers 
began growing apples. 
 
Indigo was cultivated for indigo dyeing.  The color is indigo blue.  
Indigo has been used since the 8th century, during the Nara era.  
It has been used to dye clothes ( ``kimono'' ).  It's still used for 
Japanese swordsmanship uniforms and expensive jeans. 

( In Japanese ; ``Japanese swordsmanship'' is ``kendou.''  ``Japanese 
  swordsmanship uniform'' is ``dougui.'' )

Indigo is called ``ai'' ( a'-i ) in Japanese.  Ai-no-sato in Sapporo 
City, Ishikari sub-prefecture, has the same history.  Migrants from 
Tokushima Prefecture intended to run an indigo business there.  Today, 
Ai-no-sato is a new town in Kita Ward, Sapporo City.

( UTF code ; Ai-no-sato = \u3042\u3044\u306E\u91CC. )

During the Meiji period, indigo was exported to Western countries.  It
was called Japan Blue.  My friend's grandfather was an indigo dyer in 
Ibaraki Prefecture.  A photograph showing a German buyer remains with 
the family.

( In Japanese ; ``Indigo dyer'' is ``kou-ya.'' )

( UTF code ; ``kouya'' =  \u7D3A\u5C4B. )
 
These are Japanese proverbs about indigo.
( 1 ) Kouya-no  Shiro-bakama 
     ( Indigo dyer, of, white, cloth )

   This means, ``Indigo dyers wear white clothing.''  It's generally 
   interpreted as, ``Indigo dyers are too busy to dye their own 
   clothes.''  I think this shows the state of ``non-self'' ( ``muga'') 
   http:// en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anatt%C4%81 

( 2 ) Ai-wa  Ai-yori  Idete  Ai-yori-aoshi 
     ( Indigo is, from indigo, come, more bluer than indigo )

   This was translated from an old Chinese proverb, which means ``Blue 
   comes from indigo, but the blue is bluer than indigo.''  Indigo blue 
   deepens with repeated dyeing.  This proverb is interpreted as, 
  ``Surpassing one's master.''  Teachers like this phrase.  

( Single of the chapter ; ``Set Me'' by Spangle Call Lilli Line.  -->
  http://youtu.be/_0wNJU-0FwE )

LOCATION 

Yoichi ( yo-i-chi ) is a town in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture, 
central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Yoichi is located at the root of the 
Shakotan Peninsula.  The area looks like a butterfly.  It faces Yoichi 
Bay on the Sea of Japan.  The Yoichi River flows.  #1

( UTF code ; Yoichi = \u4F59\u5E02. )

NAME  

The name ``Yoichi'' is derived from ``yu-ot-i'' in Ainu language, which 
means ``hot spring, exist, place.''  Or, it's derived from ``i-ot-i'' 
in Ainu language, which means ``snake, exist, place.''  Both signified  
the Yoichi River. 

HISTORY 
 
In the Tenmei period ( 1781-1789 ) of the Edo era, the Matsumae Domain 
established a trading place for herring.  It was called Yoichi-basho. 
Herring fishing flourished from the late Edo era to the Meiji period. 

Yoichi was a 1,000-koku trading place, which boasted caches of 1,000-
koku of herring.  ( One ``koku'' is equivalent to 200 kg of fresh 
herring. )  #2

( In Japanese ; ``1,000-koku trading place'' is ``sengoku-basho.'' )   
 
( For the unit ``koku,'' see Otobe Town in Hiyama sub-prefecture. )

In 1869 ( Meiji 2 ), Yoichi branch of the Hokkaidou Development 
Commission ( ``Kaitaku-shi'' ) was established.  It governed Yoichi 
county.  

In 1871 ( Meiji 4 ), a group of the former samurai migrated from the 
former Aizu Domain ( now western part of Fukushima Prefecture. )  In 
1881 ( Meiji 14 ), Kurokawa-mouri Farm was established by Tei-ichi 
Awaya from Yamaguchi Prefecture.

( In Japanese ; ``The former samurai group'' is ``kyuu-hanshi-
  dantai.'' )

( UTF code ; ``kyuu-hanshi-dantai'' = \u65E7\u85E9\u58EB\u56E3\u4F53. ) 

( For Kurokawa-mouri Farm, see Niki Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture ; the note of Kyougoku Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )
  
In 1900 ( Meiji 33 ), Yoichi Town ( 1st-class municipality ) was 
established by the merger of eight towns and four villages.  The towns 
were located in built-up areas around fishing ports.  #3 
 
( For 1st-class municipality, see the note of Matsumae Town. ) 
 
TOWN HALL, TRAFFIC

The town hall of Yoichi is located in the west bank ( left bank ) of 
the Yoichi River.  Yoichi Station on the Hakodate Main Line is located 
in the east bank of the Yoichi River.  National Route 5 ( Youtei-
kokudou ) run along the railroad. 

National Route 229 ( Seta-kamui Line ) branches off from National Route 
5, passes through fishing ports, and goes around the Shakotan 
Peninsula. 
 
INDUUSTRY 

Distinctive industries in Yoichi are agriculture, fishing, brewing 
( whisky, wine ), and sightseeing.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = 
orchards 695 hectares in 2015 ( apples X ( concealed, but the area is 
the largest in the prefecture ), grapes X, cherries 85, plums 12, 
Japanese plums 4, peaches 3, chestnut 1, other fruits 65+X ), 

( continue ) --> tomatoes 51, paddy rice 26, fodder 23, beans ( adzuki 
beans 3, other beans 3 ), cucumbers 5, pasture 2, strawberries 2, 
cabbages 1, potatoes 1, and other vegetables 31+X ; hens 2,700 head in 
2015 and dairy cows X ( concealed ).  
 
STATISTICS OF FISHERIES = right-eye flounder 502 tons in 2017, Pacific 
cod 246, octopus 287, yellowtail 224, salmon 216, atka mackerel 197, 
shrimp/prawns 170 ( including Nanban shrimp, which resembles Nanban 
( chili pepper ) ), walleye pollock 72, 

( continue ) --> squid ( flying squid 70, other squid 3 ), herring 66, 
sea urchins 46, crabs 32, olive flounder 21, shellfish 16, mackerel 10, 
``konbu'' kelp 1, puffer fish 1, sea trout 1, other fish 161+X, and 
other marine animals 23+X. 

Yoichi is the leading fruit producer in Hokkaidou.  There is a local 
fruit wholesale market.  Apple has been cultivated since the Meiji 
period.  Apples were shipped by train from Yoichi Station.  In the 
past, the station had shipping yards for apples and fish ( herring and 
salmon ).  #4 
 
Recently, grapes for wine, cherries, and prunes have increased.  Local 
wineries opened.  This wine-producing-area is called ``Yoichi 
Valley.''  #5

As for fishery, there are one regional port ( ``chihou-kouwan'' ) and 
four fishing ports ( ``gyokou'' ) ; 
( 1 ) The Port of Yoichi in Minato-machi district, including Yoichi 
    Marine Product Local Wholesale Market ( Yoichi-suisanbutsu-chihou-
    oroshiuri-shijou ).  It was once a base for North Ocean Fishing. 

( 1 ) Yoichi Fishing Port of Honkou, located in Irifune-chou district.
( 2 ) Yoichi Fishing Port of Detarihira, located in Shiroiwa-chou 
    district.
( 3 ) Yoichi Fishing Port of Shimadomari, located in Shiomi-chou 
    district.
( 4 ) Yoichi Fishing Port of Yunai, located in Toyohama-chou district.

( UTF code ; Detarihira = \u51FA\u8DB3\u5E73.  Honkou = \u672C\u6E2F.  
  Irifune-chou = \u5165\u821F\u753A.  Minato-machi = \u6E2F\u753A.
  Shimadomari = \u5CF6\u6CCA.  Shiomi-chou = \u6F6E\u898B\u753A. 
  Shiroiwa-chou = \u767D\u5CA9\u753A.  Toyohama-chou = 
  \u8C4A\u6D5C\u753A.  Yunai = \u6E6F\u5185. )
 
( For North Ocean Fishing, see Hakodate City in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. )

The country sign of Yoichi features Candle Rock and Nikka Whisky 
Distilling.  The TV drama ``The Story Of Massan And Rita'' was 
broadcast on NHK ( Nippon-housou-kyoukai ).  It was about the couple 
who opened the distillery.  #6  #7
 
NOTE

#1

The western part of Yoichi Town is the Shakotan Peninsula.  Sea cliffs 
continue.  Candle Rock stands along the coast.

( Other Candle Rocks are located in these places ; Tomamae Town in 
  Rumoi sub-prefecture ; Setana Town in Hiyama sub-prefecture ; Kushiro 
  Town and Hamanaka Town in Kushiro general sub-prefecture.  In 
  Hamanaka Town, there are two Candle Rock.  One was shortened by 
  erosion. )

The eastern part of Yoich Town is sandy coasts.  Ancient ruins are 
distributed from the eastern part of Yoichi Town to the western part of 
Otaru City.  Those are shell mounds, circular stones, and murals in 
caves.  Those were from the Zoku-joumon era. 

The word ``Zoku-joumon'' means the continuation of the Joumon era.  
Joumon was the era of hunting and gathering.  In Honshuu Island, the 
Joumon era continued from 140,000 BC to the 8th century BC.  
  
After the Joumon era, in Honshuu Island, the Yayoi era began.  Yayoi 
was the era of rice.  The Yayoi era continued from the 8th century BC 
to the 3rd century, followed by the Kofun period from the 3rd to the 
7th century.

In Hokkaidou, the climate is too cold for ancient people to cultivate 
paddy rice.  So, Zoku-joumon era ( continuation of the Joumon era ) 
continued until the 7th century.

Fugoppe Cave in Yoichi Town has murals from the Zoku-joumon era.  The 
murals were discovered in 1950.  Those were discovered by two young 
brothers from Sapporo City ; one was a junior high school student and 
the other was a high school student at Sapporo Minami High School. 

Near Fugoppe Cave, other murals were discovered during the construction 
of railroad in 1927, but they weren't preserved.  They were considered 
as doodles drawn by modern people.

( For mural from the Zoku-joumon era, see Otaru City in Shiribeshi 
  general sub-prefecture. )

( For the Zoku-joumon era, see Fukagawa City in Sorachi general sub-
  prefecture ; Otaru City in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )

#2  

Near the mouth of the Yoichi River, the office ``unjou-ya'' of Yoichi 
trading place and the herring mansion ``nishin-goten'' are preserved. 
The office ``unjouya'' was built in 1853, during the Edo era.  It's the 
only remaining ``unjou-ya'' in Hokkaidou.  

( UTF code ; ``nishin-goten'' = \u9C0A\u5FA1\u6BBF.  ``unjou-ya'' = 
  \u904B\u4E0A\u5BB6. ) 

( The word ``unjou'' meant ``tax'' in the Edo era.  In Ezo Island 
  ( now Hokkaidou Island ), it was a tax to the Matsumae Domain and 
  the Tokugawa Shogunate. ) 

( For the oldest building in Hokkaidou, see Kaminokuni Town in Hiyama
  sub-prefecture. )

The herring mansion ``nishin-goten'' was managed by the Fukuhara 
family.  They also manged the fishing ground in front of the herring 
mansion.  Seasonal fishermen stayed in the mansion.  The season was 
from late March to May.

In the past, herring were caught in large quantities.  They were 
processed into fertilizer.  Schools of herring last appeared in Yoichi 
Bay was in the 1950s.  Today, herring is expensive.  A grilled herring 
is 920 yen at Otaru Aquarium.  It's more expensive than ordinary 
``ramen'' noodle or ordinary buckwheat noodle ( as of 2018 ).
  
( For herring fishing, see Mori Town in Oshima general sub-prefecture ; 
  Esashi Town, Kaminokuni Town, and Otobe Town in Hiyama sub-
  prefecture ; Kamoenai Village, Otaru City, Shakotan Town, Shimamaki 
  Town, Suttsu Town, and Tomari Village in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture ; Ishikari City in Ishikari sub-prefecture ; 

  ( continue ) --> Haboro Town, Mashike Town, Obira Town, Rumoi City, 
  and Tomamae Town in Rumoi sub-prefecture ; Rebun Town and Rishiri 
  Town in Souya general sub-prefecture. ) 

The fisherman's song ``Souran-bushi'' was sung during herring fishing.  
The song originated in fishing villages from Yoichi Town to the 
Shakotan Peninsula.  This is part of the lyrics ; 
 
``Yaaren-souran-souran  Souran-souran-souran  Hai-hai,
  Oki-no-kamome-ni  Shiodoki-toeba  Watasha-tatsutori  Namini-kike.''

  ( I asked a sea-gull about the tide ( time to leave ), it replies 
   ``I'm taking off, ask the waves.'' ) 

The same lyrics appear in Dodoitsu, which is a Japanese song-like 
poetry developed during the Edo era. ( http://
en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dodoitsu )

The Yosakoi Souran Festival is held in Sapporo Oodori Park in June.  
The festival combines Souran-bushi with the Yosakoi Festival of Kouchi 
Prefecture.  The festival was started by students at Hokkaidou 
University. 

( For Esashi-oiwke ( fisherman's song ), see Esashi Town in Oshima 
  general sub-prefecture. )

( For Souran-bushi ( fisherman's song ), see Kamoenai Village and 
  Shakotan Town in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )

#3 

These are the eight towns and three villages that were merged in 1900.
Towns ;  
   Hamanaka-chou    ( \u6D5C\u4E2D\u753A )  
   Kotohira-chou *  ( \u7434\u5E73\u753A )  
   Naka-mahchi   *  ( \u4E2D\u753A ) 

   Ookawa-chou      ( \u5927\u5DDD\u753A )  
   Sawa-machi    *  ( \u6CA2\u753A )  
   Tomisawa-chou *  ( \u5BCC\u6CA2\u753A )  
   Umekawa-chou  *  ( \u6885\u5DDD\u753A )  
   Yamausu-chou  *  ( \u5C71\u81FC\u753A )  

Villages ;  
   Fugoppe-mura     ( \u755A\u90E8\u6751 ) 
   Kurokawa-mura    ( \u9ED2\u5DDD\u6751 )  
   Oki-mura         ( \u6C96\u6751 )
   Yamada-mura      ( \u5C71\u7530\u6751 )  

Towns retain Japanese-origin names.  Towns with asterisks ( * ) were 
located around the Port of Yoichi.  ( Kotohira-chou was located near 
Mt. Maruyama.  Yamausu-chou was located near Sawa-machi. )  

The pronunciation of ``-chou'' or ``-machi'' in town names can be 
searched on the post office ( Yuubin-kyoku ) web-site.  ( In the past, 
prominent local families served as the post office. )

( For the pronunciation of ``chou'' and ``machi,'' see Mori Town in 
  Oshima general sub-prefecture. )  
 
According to ``A Story That Happend In Yoichi No. 173'' ( http://
www.town.yoichi.hokkaido.jp/machi/yoichistory/2019/sono173.html  last 
accessed in August 2026 ), in the Meiji period, the towns ( ``chou'' 
and ``machi'' ) flourished not only from herring fishing but also from 
salmon fishing.  

According to ``Kagai-zomeki'' ( http://gionchoubu.exblog.jp/23348433/ 
last accessed in August 2026 ), there was ``yuukaku'' ( a led-line 
district ) in Sawa-machi, Yoichi Town.  It was build in the Ansei 
period ( 1855-1860 ) of the Edo era.  ( The Edo era continued from 1603 
to 1868. )

#4 
  
In 1875 ( Meiji 8 ), Hokkaidou Development Commission ( ``Kaitaku-
shi'') distributed 500 apple saplings to farmers in present-day Yoichi 
Town.  The saplings were planted on the land cleared by the former 
samurai from the Aizu Domain.  ( by Hokkaidou-kaitaku Club ``Aizu 
Samurai And Yoichi Apples''  http://
www.hokkaidokaitaku.club/2023/12/4.html  last accessed July 2026 ) 

Almost all the apple saplings died, because the farmers ( ex-samurai ) 
weren't interested in fruit production.  They needed to produce staple 
food such as paddy rice and beans.
 
( For fruit sapling, see Nanae Town in Oshima general sub-prefecture. ) 

In 1879 ( Meiji 12 ), the first apples ripened in Yamada village, 
Yoichi county.  In 1880 ( Meiji 13 ), Yoichi apples were exibited at a 
fare in Sapporo.

In 1889 ( Meiji 22 ), Yoichi apples were exhibited at a fare in Oosaka. 
In 1902 ( Meiji 35 ), Yoichi Station on the Hokkaidou Railway ( now 
Hakodate Mein Line ) opened.  Trains shipped Yoichi apples.

Apples are hard enough to be stored in wooden crates and transported by 
train.  Apples can be stored for a long period.  The market of Yoichi 
apples expanded to nationwide.  

After the Russo-Japanese War ( 1904-1905 ), Yoichi apples began to be 
shipped to Russia.  An apple wholesale market and apple warehouses were 
constructed adjacent to Yoichi Station. 

In 1912 ( Meiji 45 ), an orchard of Collage of Agriculture, Touhouku 
Imperial University, was established.  That's now Hokkaidou University 
Yoichi Orchard in Yamada-chou district, Yoichi Town.  Apples evoke 
Westernization after the Meiji period.  ( The university system was 
also imported from the USA in the Meiji period. )

After Wold War II, plums increased due to demand from US garrison in 
Hokkaidou ( by Ms. Matsugahira's master's thesis submitted to Graduate 
School of Letters at Hokkaidou University in the school year of 2015. ) 

( For fruit production, see Niki Town and Otaru City in Shiribeshi 
  general sub-prefecture ; Soubetsu Town in Iburi general sub-
  prefecture ; Sapporo City in Ishikari sub-prefecture ; Urausu Town in 
  Sorachi general sub-prefecture ; Mashike Town in Rumoi sub-
  prefecture. )

* * *
 
During the Meiji period, almost all apples in Japan were changed to 
Western varieties.  Japan's local apples ( ``wa-ringo'' ) are small 
and sour.  

( For Kanji characters for ``wa-ringo,'' see the Pronunciation section 
  of this file. ) 

( For the first Western apple, see Nanae Town in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. )

Even among the same Western varieties, apples cultivated in the USA and 
Canada are smaller than those cultivated in Japan.  They are about the 
size of a fist.  While Japanese ones are about the size of an alarm 
clock.  This is because Japanese farmers remove flowers of apple trees, 
and apples grow larger. 

Today, apple trees in Japan are small, although apples are large.  The 
cultivation method is called ``waika-saibai,'' which means ``dwarf, 
cultivation.''  This makes it easier for farmers to harvest.  Dwarf 
apple trees in winter looks like Bonsai, which are miniature trees for 
ornament.

( For Bonsai village, see the Saitama chapter by T. Nihei 2018. )

To turn apples red, farmers twist apples on the tree.  The cultivation 
method is called ``tama-mawashi,'' which means ``ball, turn.''  Like 
getting a suntan, apples turn red in the sunlight.  

Two famous apple varieties are Kokkou and Fuji.  Kokkou is the oldest 
variety.  It's an American variety called ``Ralls Janet,'' which the 
Meiji government imported and distributed.  

( UTF code ; Fuji = \u3075\u3058.  Kokkou = \u56FD\u5149. )

In Hokkaidou Prefecture, the apple variety Kokkou was called ``Yonjuu-
kyuu-gou,'' which means ``No. 49.''  In the prefecture, Kokkou was 
firstly harvested in Yamada village ( now Yoichi Town ) in 1879 ( Meiji 
12 ).  ( by Hokkaidou-kaitaku Club ``Aizu samurai and Yoichi Apples''  
http://www.hokkaidokaitaku.club/2023/12/4.html  last accessed July 
2026 )

In Aomori Prefecture, the apple variety Kokkou was called ``Yuki-no-
shita,''  which means ``snow, of, under.''  In Yamagata Prefecture, it 
was called ``Ki-jirushi,'' which means ``Ki, mark.''  The word ``Ki'' 
signifies No. 7 in the Japanese Hiragana table ; a, i, u, e, o, ka, ki,
 ku, ke, ko. 

( UTF code ; ``ki-jirushi'' = \u30AD\u5370. ) 

In 1900 ( Meiji 33 ), the name of these apple varieties ( Yonjuu-kyuu-
gou, Yuki-no-shita, and Ki-jirushi ) were unified as ``Kokkou,'' which 
means ``nation, light.''  It commemorated the marriage of the crown 
prince ( later Emperor Taishou ). 
 
Fuji is a new apple variety.  The name was registered in 1962 ( Shouwa 
37 ).  The development of the variety started in Fujisaki Town in 
Aomori Prefecture in 1934 ( Shouwa 14 ).  It took years before the name 
``Fuji'' was registered.

The name ``Fuji'' signifies ``Fujisaki Town'' ( Fujisaki-machi ).  It 
also signifies Mt. Fuji, which is the highest mountain in the country, 
and also Fujiko Yamamoto, who was an actress and the first Miss Japan.  
( by ``History Of Fuji ( Fuji-no-rekishi )'' http://
www.fujisaki-kanko.jp/fuji/fuji_history.html  last accessed in July 
2026 ) 

#5  
 
As for grape cultivation, the number of small-scale wineries increased 
in Yoichi Town and also in the Ishikari Plain.  They are operated by 
individuals or families.  These new wine producing regions in Hokkaidou 
Island are called Yoich Valley and Ishikari Valley.  

Yoich Valley is located in Yoichi Town, Shiribeshi general sub-
prefecture.  Ishikari Valley is located in the Ishikari Plain in 
Ishikari sub-prefecture and Sorachi general sub-prefecture.

Those were named after wine producing regions on the Pacific Rim, 
namely, Napa Valley in the USA, Okanagan Valley in Canada, and Hunter 
Valley in Australia. 

( For wine, see Ikeda Town in Tokachi general sub-prefecture ; Otaru 
  City in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture ; Urausu Town in Sorachi 
  general sub-prefecture. )

( For fruit cultivation in Yoichi Town, see M. Terada 2010.  Current 
  conditions and regional characteristics of the fruit tree cultivation 
  in Yoichi Town, Hokkaidou.  ``Kaihatsu-ronshuu ( Journal Of 
  Development Policy Studies )'' 86, 77-86. 
  HTTP://2242.MOOO.COM/PAPER/2974.PDF )

#6  

The name ``Massan'' is the nickname of Masataka Taketsuru, who produced 
whisky for the first time in the country.  He studied whisky 
distillation in Scotland between 1918 and 1920.  

In 1923, he worked for Masuya brewery ( now Suntory Holdings ) in 
Oosaka Prefecture and started whisky production.  In 1929, the first 
whisky was released.  The brand was Suntory White. 

( The spelling ``whisky'' is typically used in Scotland, including 
  Japan and Canada.  The spelling ``whiskey'' is typically used in 
  Ireland and the USA. )

In 1934, Massan retired Masuya brewery.  He moved to Yoichi Town, 
Hokkaidou Prefecture, and established Dai-nihon-kajuu brewery ( now 
Nikka Whisky Distiling ).  He chose the place to produce real whisky.  
The climate is cool and the soil has peat.  The environment resembles 
Scotland. 

Peat is partially carbonized moss and grass saturated with water.  It 
can be used as a fuel when dried.  Burning peat produces a good aroma.
The raw materials for whisky are malted barley and water.  The malted 
barley is dried by burning peat.  Peat is used to add flavor whisky.  

The name ``Nikka'' is an abbreviation of Dai-nihon-kajuu ( Empire Japan 
Fruit Juice ).  In 1935, Massan began selling apple juice made from 
Yoichi apples.  In 1940, the first Nikka whisky was released.  

Whisky production takes time.  Before the whisky was ready, Massan 
sold apple juice, apple cider, and apple wine, using Yoichi apples.  
Incidentally, the pronunciation ``massan''  in Portuguese means 
``apple.''

( UTF code ; ``maca'' ( ``apple'' in Portugese ) = mama\u00E7\u00E3. ) 

Nikka Whisky Distilling has a whisky museum.  Visitors can drink old 
whisky.  I visited there in 2017.  The price of one shot ( 15 cc ) was 
500 yen for the 5-year-old whisky, and 1,000 yen for the 10-year-old 
whisky.  The 15-year-old and the 25-year-old whiskies were sold out. 

Whiskies aged between 15 to 25 years are ideal.  Beyond 25 years, the 
alcohol contents drops.  Nikka's whisky in Yoichi is aged in white oak 
casks imported from the USA.  Every year, around two percent of the 
whisky evaporates from the cask.  About a quarter of whisky disappears 
in 10 years.

Mr. Kijuurou Kondou, who had been the owner of a liquor trading company 
in Nagoya, told that the natural evaporation was called ``angel's 
share.''  Hearing that, I came to think that everything has an angel's 
share. 

The alcohol contents of Japanese whiskies is slightly lower than 
imported ones.  For example, High Nikka is 39 percent and Black Nikka 
Clear 37 percent, while imported ones, White Horse is 40 percent and 
Teachers 40 percent.  This is due to the former liquor tax system. 

In the past, whiskies with an alcohol content of 40 percent was 
classified as 1st-class liquor ( ``ikyuu-shu'' ) and subject to a high 
tax rate.  Whiskies with an alcohol content of 39 percent or less was 
classified as 2nd-class liquor ( ``nikyuu-shu'' ).  Japanese distillers 
produced 1st-class and 2nd-class whiskies. 

The liquor tax system was modified in 1989 due to pressure from the 
GATT ( General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade ).  The classification of 
1st-class and 2nd-class was abolished. 

Still, when I was an undergraduate student ( in the early 1990s ), 
imported whisky was expensive.  At the student dormitory, my friends 
treated me to the imported whisky and brandy that their parents didn't 
drink.  Those imported liquors were for gifts.  Inexpensive liquors 
were spirits ( ``shouchuu'' ).

The liquor tax system was modified again in 1996 due to pressure from 
WTO ( World Trade Organization ).  The tax rates for whisky and 
spirits were unified.  Imported whisky has become cheaper.  Japanese 
administrative organizations sometimes make funny decisions and they 
are modified by foreign people.

( For spirits ( ``shouchuu'' ), see Nishi Ward in Sapporo City, 
  Ishikari sub-prefecture. )

* * * 
 
The British Minister for Foreign Affairs, Baron Home ( hyu'um ) of 
Hirsel, visited Japan, and said, ``40 years ago, a smart Japanese guy 
stole our country's treasure, using a fountain pen and a notebook.''  
He mentioned Massan, who studied whisky production in Schottland.  
Massan also took his wife, Ms. Rita ( Jessie Roberta Cowan ), to Japan. 

There is Rita House at Nikka Whisky Distilling.  It's a Western house. 
Massan wrote, ``It was Rita who was happiest about the move to Yoichi.  
The climate and the scenery resemble Scotland's, in particular, the 
atomosphere in the mornings and evenings.''  ( by Masataka Taketsuru's 
autobiography ``Whisky And I'' published in 1972, not for sale. ) 

* * *

A figure of a bearded man, which comes from the label of Nikka Whisky,
is the landmark of Susukino district, Sapporo City.  Susukino is the 
largest entertainment district in Hokkaidou.  ( Nikka's bearded man --> 
http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susukino#/media/
File:Spectaculars_of_Susukino-Sapporo.jpg )
 
Masataka Taketsuru ( 1894-1979 ), known as the nickname Massan, was the 
founder of Nikka Whisky.  He drank one bottle of whisky a day.  It was 
Hi Nikka, the 2nd-class wishky.  The amount is 720 cc.  He served soy-
sauce-flavored lightly rice crackers with his drink.  Even he became an 
elderly man, he drank half the bottle a day.

Momofuku Andou ( 1910-2007 ) was the founder of Nisshin Foods.  He 
invented the instant noodle ``Chiken-ramen.''  He ate Chiken-ramen and 
rice for lunch every day.  What do you do every day?

#7  

The Space Memorial Museum ( Uchuu-kinen-kan ) is located next to Nikka 
Whisky Distilling.  The first Japanese astronaut, Mamoru Mouri, is from 
Yoichi Town.  In 1992, he traveled to space aboard the US space shuttle 
Endeavour.  

The family name Mouri brings to mind the Mouri clan of the Choushuu 
Domain ( now Yamaguchi Prefecture ).  Migrants from Yamaguchi 
Prefecture migrated in Yoichi Town and neihbouring Niki Town. 

( For Kurokawa-mouri Farm, see Niki Town in Shiribeshi sub-
  prefecture. ) 

The first Japanese who went into space was the journalist Toyohiro 
Akiyama.  He wasn't an astronaut.  In 1990, he flew the Soyuz of USSR 
( Union of Soviet Socialist Republics ).  In the same year, USSR 
dissolved and the Russian Federation was established.

Massan, who was the founder of Nikka Whisky Distilling, and Mamoru 
Mouri's father was a Go buddy.  ( ``Go'' is a traditional board game.  
Massan is a nickname of Masataka Taketsru. ) 

Mamoru Mouri's parents ran a public bath.  After Mamomu Mouri went to 
space, the name was changed to ``uchuu-no-yu,'' which means ``space, 
of, bath.''  

( The town entrusted the operation of the public bath to the Mouri 
  family.  Profitable operations are outsourced.  Loss-making 
  operations are handled by municipalities.  This is the basic of 
  administration. )
 
In 2013, Professor Yuuichi Hashimoto and I visited the Space Memorial 
Museum during a bus excursion from Faculty of Letters, Hokkaidou 
University. 

At the time, a ``Janken'' tournament was held in the museum.  Janken is 
a hand game that is called ``rock, paper, scissors'' in English.  We 
won it.  I was sorry for the other children. 

The prize for the winners was rice cakes ( ``mochi'' ) developed as 
space food.  They can be eaten soon after soaking them in water.  
Generally, rice cakes are eaten after being grilled.  Inside a 
spaceship, astronauts cannot gill them.  I thought the space-food rice 
cakes would be good for breakfast on the earth.

Yoichi is a famous tourist town.  Summer is crowded.  But, winter is 
quiet.

( Single of the chapter ; ``Jueves ( Thursday )'' by Pugliese.  --> 
   http://youtu.be/naFV6EYrr0k )

|                  |
|     AKAIGAWA     |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 37  |
 ------------------
AREA 280 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 253, CULTIVATED 7 ; COUNTY = YOICHI ; POP 
1,470 ( 1990 ), 1,262 ( 2010 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL PRECIPI 1,369 MM ( AV 
1981-2010 ), SNOW 977 CM ( AV 1988-2010 ) ; COUNTRY SIGN = MOUNTAIN & 
RIVER. 
  
LOCATION  
 
Akaigawa ( a-ka-i-ga-wa ) is a village in Shiribeshi general sub-
prefecture, central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  Akaigawa is an inland 
village located between Otaru City and Kucchan Town.  

( UTF code ; Akaigawa = \u8D64\u4E95\u5DDD. )

The topography consists of basins and mountains.  Forests occupy 90 
percent of the area.  To the west lies Akaigawa caldera.  Its diameter 
is seven kilometers.  The Yoichi River, the Akaigawa River, and the 
Shiraigawa River flow.  #1  #2

NAME

The name ``Akai-gawa'' means ``red, river'' in Japanese.  It's a 
translation of ``hure-pet'' in Ainu language, which means ``red, 
river.''  It signified the Akaigawa River, which flows through Akaigawa 
caldera.  The soil looked reddish due to country rock.  #3
 
( In Japanese, ``country rock'' ( geology ) is ``bogan.'' )

( UTF code ; ``bogan'' = \u6BCD\u5CA9. ) 
  
HISTORY  

In 1894 ( Meiji 27), Tei-ichi Awaya from Yamaguchi Prefecture leased 
660 hectares of land and started development.  ( by ``Akaigawa Village 
Founder, Tei-ichi Awaya'' http://
www.hokkaidokaitaku.club/kaitakunissi/2026-04-08.html  last accessed in 
August 2026. )
  
In 1899 ( Meiji 32 ), the administrative office ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) 
of Akaigawa village was established.  It was separated from Ooe village 
( now Niki Town ) in Yoichi county.  In 1906 ( Meiji 39 ), Akaigawa 
Village ( 2nd-class municipality ) was established.  

( For 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Matsumae Town. )

VILLAGE HALL, TRAFFIC 

The village hall of Akaigawa is located in a caldera.  Farmland spreads 
across the caldera.  National Route 393 ( Akaigawa-kokudou, Maple-
kaidou ) passes outside the caldera.  

The national highway is a cutoff connecting Otaru City and Kucchan 
Town.  Akaigawa Village borders Sapporo City, but there are no roads 
that directly connects Sapporo.  #4 

INDUSTRY  

Distinctive industries in Akaigawa are agriculture and sightseeing.
STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = pasture 152 hectares in 2015, paddy rice 
121, potatoes 34, beans ( adzuki beans 10, soybeans 10, other beans 
1 ), broccoli 15, fodder 10, orchards 8, cabbages 4, 

( continue ) --> Chinese cabbages 1, flowers 1, radishes 1, watermelons 
1, and other vegetables 102+X ( pumpkins, sweet corn, asparagus ) ; 
dairy cows X ( concealed ) head in 2015. 

Dosanko Nousan Center sells local agricultural products.  It has a 
warehouse that uses snow to keep cool temperatures.  The warehouse 
preserves potatoes, broccoli, and cabbages.  #5

As for sightseeing ( tourism ), Kiroro Ski Resort is located on the 
foot of Mt. Asari-dake.  It's one of the major ski resorts in 
Hokkaidou.  Near Kiroro Ski Resort, there are tourist ranches along 
National Route 393.  #6  #7 

NOTE

#1  
 
Akaigawa caldera is located to the west of Akaigawa Village.  It was 
created between 1.7 million to 1.3 million years ago.  Obsidian was 
extracted in Akaigawa Village.  ( Obsidian is volcanic glass. )

( In Japanese ; ``Obsidian'' is ``kokuyou-seki.'' )

In the Joumon era and the Zoku-joumon era, people in Hokkaidou Island 
traded obsidian.  Obsidian was used for tools such as knives, spear 
heads, and ornaments.

( For the Zoku-joumon era, see Yoichi Town in Shiribeshi general 
  sub-prefecture ; Fukagawa City in Sorachi general sub-prefecture. )

The trade of obsidian dates back to Paleolithic Age ( Kyuu-sekki-
jidai ; 25,000 to 10,000 years ago ).  The ancestors of Ainu people 
traded.

About 40,000 years ago, people moved to the Japanese archipelago.  The 
last Glacial Period occurred from 100,000 to 10,000 years ago ( by 
Nihei lab 2023-2026_t ).  As time passed, the Yayoi era began about 
3,000 years ago ( 8th century BC ).  Yayoi was the era of rice. 
  
* * *

( In 2012, I bought a piece of obsidian at an Ainu shop.  The shop was 
  located in front of the Ainu Museum in Biratori Town, Hidaka sub-
  prefecture.  An elderly woman at the shop said, ``This isn't for 
  sale.  My son played with it.  You can keep it.''  

  I paid a few hundred yen ( small coins in my wallet ) for the 
  obsidian.  After returning to the laboratory in Sapporo, I found that 
  a barking dog or a barking bear was carved on the obsidian.  I 
  understood that her son had used to practice carving on it. )

( For obsidian, see Engaru Town in Okhotsk general sub-prefecture. ) 
   
* * *
 
To the west of Akaigawa Village is a mountain range that separates it 
from Sapporo City.  The mountain range includes the Yochi Three 
Mountains, namely, Mt. Asari-dake, Mt. Shirai-dake, and Mt. Yoichidake.

Mt. Asari-dake ( 1,281 meters ) is the border between Akaigawa Village, 
Otaru City, and Sapporo City.  Two ski resorts are located on the 
mountain, namely, Sapporo Kokusai and Kiroro Ski Resort.  The name 
``Asari'' comes from ``masar'' in Ainu language, which means coastal 
grassland.  ( Ainu place names usually mean rivers. )   

Mt. Shirai-dake ( 1,301 meters ) is located in Minami Ward, Sapporo 
City.  The name ``Shirai'' is Japanese, which means ``white.''  The 
Shirai River flowing from the mountain was white due to minerals and 
silt.  

The Shirai River ( White River ) is a tributary of the Toyohira River, 
which flows through Sapporo City.  Toyoha Mine was located along the 
river.  There is another Shiraigawa River ( White River ) in Akaigawa 
Village.  There were gold and copper mines along the river.  ( See Note 
No. 2 below. ) 

( For Toyoha Mine, see Minami Ward in Sapporo City, Ishikari sub-
  prefecture. ) 

Mt. Yoichi-dake ( 1,488 meters ) is the boder between Akaigawa Village 
and Minami Ward in Sapporo City, Ishikari sub-prefecture.  Mt. Yoichi-
dake is the highest mountain in Akaigawa Village and Sapporo City.  A 
hiking trail to the mountain starts at Kiroro Ski Resort in Akaigawa 
Village. 

( For the name ``Yoichi,'' see Yoichi Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
 prefecture. ) 

#2  

The Yoichi River originates on Mt. Asari-dake and empties in the Sea of 
Japan.  The mouth is located in Yoichi Town, Shiribeshi general sub-
prefecture.  

The Akaigawa River goes through the outer rim of Akaigawa caldera and 
meets the Yoichi River near the border with Niki Town.  The name 
``Akai-gawa'' means ``red, river'' in Japanese.  Ainu people called it 
``fure-pet,'' which means ``red, river.''  

The Shiraigawa meets the Yoichi River near Akaigawa Roadside Station.  
The name ``Shirai-gawa''is Japanese and  means ``white, river.''  
Maybe, it was named after the color of the river, which included cray 
and minerals.  There used to be mines along the river. 

They were Meiji Mine and Todoroki Mine along the Shiraigawa River.  
Meiji Mine existed intermittently from 1890 to 1927 and from 1938 to 
1954.  It produced copper and mercury.  ( by Office of the Hokkaido 
Remarkable Geosites 100. ``Kyuu-maiji-kouzan ( Old Maiji Maine )'' 
http://www.geosites-hokkaido.org/geosites/site0049.html  last accessed 
in August 2026. )  

Todoroki Mine existed from 1896 to 1987.  It produced 2,637 kilograms 
of gold and 104 tons of silver.  It was the sixth largest gold mine 
in Hokkaidou after Kounomai, Chitose, Teine, Shizukari, and Sanru.  It 
was the forth largest silver mine in Hokkaidou after Kounomai, Toyoha, 
and Teine.  ( by Office of the Hokkaido Remarkable Geosites 100.  
``Kyuu-todoroki-kouzan ( Old Todoroki Mine )'' http://
www.geosites-hokkaido.org/geosites/site0568.html  last accessed in 
August 2026 ; by ``Todoroki'' http://
www.aikis.or.jp/~kage-kan/01-3.Doo/Akaigawa_Todoroki.html  last 
accessed in August 2026. ) 

Ores from those mines were shipped to Guinzan Station on the Hakodate 
Main Line.  There was a horse-drawn railway to the Guinzan Station.

( For Guinzan Station, see Niki Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

( As for gold mines, Kounomai was located in Monbetsu City in Okhotsk
  general sub-prefecture.  Chitose was located along Lake Shikotsu in 
  Chitose City, Ishikari sub-prefecture.  

  Shizukari was located near Shizukari Pass in Oshamanbe Town, Oshima 
  general sub-prefecture.  Sanru was located in Shimokawa Town, 
  Kamikawa general sub-prefecture. ) 

( UTF code ; Sanru = \u73CA\u7460. ) 
 
( For gold, see Naka-tonbetsu Town in Souya general sub-prefecture. )
 
#3  
 
Many places have names related to red.  In Hokkaidou, many of them 
are derived from the word ``fure'' in Ainu language.  These are 
examples.

( 1 ) Furubira Town in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture, ( 2 ) Furuu 
county, including Tomari and Kamoenai villages, in Shiribeshi general 
sup-prefecture, ( 3 ) Furenai district in Biratori Town in Hidaka sub-
prefecture, ( 4 ) Fuuren district in Shosanbetsu Town in Rumoi sub-
prefecture ; 

( continue ) --> ( 5 ) Fuuren district in Nayoro City in Kamikawa sub-
prefecture, ( 6 ) Fuuren district in Betsukai Town in Nemuro sub-
prefecture, and ( 7 ) Lake Fuuren located at the border between 
Betsukai Town and Nemuro City.

( UTF code ; Furubira = \u53E4\u5E73.  Furuu = \u53E4\u5B87.  Fuuren = 
  \u98A8\u9023. ) 
 
In neighboring Niki Town, there are places named Furubetsu-zawa, 
Furetonai, and Fuurehira.  There also is the Furetoi River.  ( by 
``Place Names `Fure' In Niki Town ( Niki-chou-nai-ni-nokotteiru-fure-
to-iu-chimai )'' http://
class-niki.blogspot.com/2017/11/blog-post_22.html  last accessed July 
2026. ) 
 
In neighboring Otaru City, there are Akaiwa district and Mt. Akaiwa 
( 371 meters ) near the center of the city.  The name ``Aka-iwa'' is 
Japanese, which means ``red, rock.''  Agglomerate, which is the rock 
made from volcanic bombs, weathered and turned red.  

( In Japanese ; ``Agglomerate'' is ``shuukai-gan.'' )

( UTF code ; ``syuukai-gan'' = \u96C6\u584A\u5CA9. )

In Oshamanbe Town, Oshima general sub-prefecture, there also is 
Akaigawa Station on the Hakodate Main Line.

In the USA, the name of the state of Colorado means ``colored, red'' in 
Spanish.  The Red River flows as the border between Texas and Oklahoma 
and passes through Arkansas and Louisiana.  The name ``Okla-homa'' 
also means ``people, red,'' in native American language. 

In the Tama Hills, Toukyou Prefecture, where I live now, I walk along 
Akaishi Park to go to my office.  The word ``aka-ishi'' means ``red-
stone.''  There used to be a red rock that served as a signpost.  In 
the park, there is the red rock.  The height is about 80 centimeters.   

( For the place name ``red cliff,'' see Akabira City in Sorachi general 
  sub-prefecture ) 

#4  
 
The nickname of National Route 393 is the Maple Road ( Maple-kaidou ).  
The name was chosen through a public offering.  The name is the same 
as the Maple Road in Canada.  The original Maple Road connects Niagara 
Falls and Quebec City.  

The Maple Road in Akaigawa Village runs among white birch trees, rather 
than maple trees.  But, the yellow leaves of white birch are beautiful 
in autumn.  The light green leaves in early summer are beautiful too.

In Canada, ``maple'' generally means the sugar maple.  It's printed on 
the national frag.  In Japanese, ``sugar maple'' is called ``satou-
kaede,'' which means ``sugar, maple.''  It isn't ``tou-kaede,'' which 
means ``Tang dynasty, maple.'' 

* * *

Akaigawa Village is a member of the Most Beautiful Villages in the 
World.  It's also a member of the Most Beautiful Villages in Japan.  

In 2005, the Most Beautiful Villages of Japan was established by seven 
municipalities ; ( 1 ) Akaigawa Village and ( 2 ) Biei Town in 
Hokkaidou Prefecture ; ( 3 ) Kamikatsu Town in Tokushima Prefecture ; 

( continue ) --> ( 4 ) Minami-oguni Town in Kumamoto Prefecture ; ( 5 ) 
Ookura Village in Yamagata Prefecture ; ( 6 ) Ooshika Village in Nagano 
Prefecture ; ( 7 ) Shirakawa Village in Guifu Prefecture. 

As of 2017, these eight municipalities in Hokkaidou are members of the 
Most Beautiful Villages in Japan ; ( 1 ) Akaigawa Village, ( 2 ) 
Kuromatsunai Town, and ( 3 ) Kyougoku Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
prefecture ; ( 4 ) Biei Town in Kamikawa general sub-prefecture ; 

( continue ) --> ( 5 ) Ebeotsu district in Takikawa City in Sorachi 
general sub-prefecture ; ( 6 ) Esashi Town in Hiyama sub-prefecture ; 
( 7 ) Kiyosato Town in Okhotsk general sub-prefecture ; ( 8 ) Shibetsu 
Town in Nemuro sub-prefecture.

What about going out to find your own beautiful village?

#5 

A warehouse that utilizes snow was built in 1995.  It was subsidized by 
``1995 Special Project On Promotion To Sustain Agriculture And Fishery 
In Mountainous Villages ( Heisei-nana-nendo-sanson-shinkou-tou-
nourin-gyogyou-tokubetsu-taisaku-jigyou ).''  
 
One third of the warehouse is a snow room.  Construction costs were 
expensive.  Electricity bills are low.  The warehouse keeps the 
freshness of vegetables and potatoes by high humidity and low 
temperatures.  Potatoes stored with snow taste better in spring. 

The number of warehouses utilizing snow increased in the 2000s.  In 
2010, it counted 65 in Hokkaidou ( by K. Matsui 2011.  Significance and 
challenges of utilizing snow and ice cold energy for agricultural 
storage.  ``Nougyou-keizai-kenkyuu-houkoku ( Journal Of Farm Management 
Economics )'' 42, 27-39. )
 
#6   
 
The Maple Road ( Maple-kaidou ) is part of National Route 393, which 
runs along the Yoichi River.  It's a tourist road.  Along the road, 
there are tourist ranches.  

Yamanaka Dairy Farm ( Yamanaka-bokujou ) sells ice cream and sausages.  
It's managed by a graduate of Rakunou Gakuen University ( Dairy Farming 
College University ), which is located in Ebetsu City, Ishikari sub-
prefecture.

Hopi's Hill ( Hopi-no-oka ) raises horses and sheep for visitors.  Mt. 
Yoichi is clearly visible from this tourist ranch.  The name ``Hopi'' 
is derived from Hopi people, who are an indigenous ethnic group in the 
USA.  ( Hopi people have this prophecy, ``When the blue star comes, 
purification starts.'' )  

Other distinctive land-use along the tourist road includes ( 1 ) 
Akaigawa Farm ( dairy ranch ), ( 2 ) Kiroro Ski Resort Employee 
Dormitory, ( 3 ) fish farm, ( 4 ) pig farm, ( 5 ) Forest of Hokkaidou 
Shinkin Bank ( Hokkaidou-shin-yo-u-kinko ).  In the bank's protected 
forest, Todomatsu trees ( Sakhalin fir ) are planted. 

The Maple Road leads north and enters Otaru City.  There is Mt. 
Kenashi-yama observation deck ( about 500 meters altitude ), from which 
visitors can see a panoramic view of the center of Otaru City.  Along 
the winding road in Otaru City, Hokkaidou Wine is located.
 
The name ``kenashi'' comes from ``kenas'' in Ainu language.  It means 
``forest.''  The kanji characters ``ke-nashi'' mean ``no, hair.''  The 
word ``kenashi-yama'' in Japanese evokes the image of a mountain 
without trees. 

( In Japanese ; ``Mountain without tree'' is ``hague-yama.'' )

( UTF code ; ``hague-yama'' = \u79BF\u5C71. ``kenashi'' = 
  \u6BDB\u7121. )

( For Hokkaidou Wine, see the Otaru South section of Otaru City in 
  Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. ) 

( For the name meaning ``increasing, hair,'' see Mashike Town in Rumoi 
  sub-prefecture. ) 
  
#7
 
In 1991, Yamaha Resort Co. and Akaigawa Village opened Kiroro Ski 
Resort.  It was the midst of the ski boom during the Bubble Economy 
period.  ( At the time, I was an undergraduate student.  At famous ski 
resorts, such as Naeba in Niigata Prefecture, it took an hour to get on 
a small ski lift. ) 

In 2003, Akaigawa Village sold its shares in Kiroro Ski Resort to 
Yamaha Resort.  In 2007, Mitsui-fudousan Resort acquired Kiroro Ski 
Resort.  

The name ``Kiroro'' was given by Yamaha Resort.  It's said that there 
is no connection between local names and the name Kiroro, but there are 
places called ``Kiroro'' in Hokkaidou.

They are Kiroro district in Urahoro Town, Tokachi general sub-
prefecture, and the former Kiroro village in what is now Honbetsu 
Town, Tokachi general sub-prefecture. 

( UTF code ; Kiroro ( Akaigawa Village ) = \u30AD\u30ED\u30ED.  Kiroro 
  ( Honbetsu Town ) = \u5ACC\u4FB6.  Kiroro ( Urahoro Town ) = 
  \u8CB4\u8001\u8DEF. ) 

The name ``Kiroro'' comes from ``kir-o-ru'' in Ainu language, which 
means ``foot, on, road.''  It signified ``a broad road.''  Or, it comes 
from ``pi-oro'' in Ainu language, which means ``stone, many.''

( For ski resort, see Kucchan Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )

  http://youtu.be/3BUIfEfcfPo )

|                  |
|      OTARU       |
|                  |
|  MAP 2 - NO. 38  |
 ------------------
AREA 243 KM2 ( 2010 ), FOREST 164, CULTIVATED 2 ; POP 163,211 ( 1990 ), 
131,928 ( 2010 ) ; MEAN ANNUAL TEMP 8.6 C ( AV 1981-2010 ), PRECIPI 
1,232 MM, SNOW 676 CM ; COUNTRY SIGN = CANNAL. 

LOCATION   
 
Otaru ( o-ta-ru ) is a city in Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture, 
central Hokkaidou ( ``dou-ou'' ).  The area faces Ishikari Bay and the 
Sea of Japan.  

( UTF code ; Otaru = \u5C0F\u6A3D. )

The rivers of the Ranshima, the Shioya, the Katsunai, the Asari, the 
Hariusu, the Zenibako, and the Hoshioki flow.  ( These are listed from 
west to east. )  Built up areas distribute along the rivers.

( The Katsunai River flows near Minami-otaru Station.  The Hoshioki 
  River is the border with Sapporo City.  The mouth of the Shinkawa 
  River, which flows through Sapporo City, is included in Otaru City. )

NAME  
 
The name ``Otaru'' comes from ``ota-ru-nay'' in Ainu language, which 
means ``sand beach, road, river.''  Or, it comes from ``ota-or-nay'' in 
Ainu language, which means ``sand beach, place, river.''  

Both signified the mouth of the Otarunai River, which used to be 
located near the mouth of the present-day Shinkawa River.  An Ainu 
village existed there.  An Ainu village is called ``kotan'' in Ainu
language.
   
Both sides from the mouth of the Shinkawa River, long sandy beaches 
continue.  One is Oohama Beach, which is also called Otaru Dream Beach. 
Beach huts ( ``umi-no-ie'' ) line there.  ( Map --> http://
maps.gsi.go.jp/#16/43.157845/141.207715 )

( My son caught sardine on the sandy beach using a fishnet.  The fish 
  died soon.  The Kanji character for sardine ( ``iwashi'' ) literally 
  mean ``weak fish.'' )

( UTF code ; ``iwashi'' = \u9C2F. ) 
 
HISTORY 

During the Kyouhou period of the Edo era ( from 1716 to 1736), 
Otarunai-basho ( a trading place ) was established by the Matsumae 
Domain.  
 
The office ( ``unjou-ya'' ) of the trading place was originally located 
on the sandy beach near the mouth of the present-day Shinkawa River, 
which flows through Sapporo City.  The office of the trading place was 
relocated 12 kilometers to the west.  That was due to strong winds.

The new office was built on the leeward side of Cape Takashima.  The 
name ``Otarunai-basho'' continued to be used.  The location was what is 
now the Port of Otaru.  #1 
 
After the Meiji period, in 1869 ( Meiji 2 ), Zenibako branch of the 
Hokkaidou Development Commission ( ``Kaitaku-shi'' ) was built.  
Zenibako became a base for the develelopment of Sapporo.  The name 
``Otarunai'' was shortened to ``Otaru.'' 

The name ``Zenibako'' means ``money box'' in Japanese.  It signified 
the wealth of herring fishing in the Edo era.  It's an auspicious 
name ( good-luck name ). 

( For auspicious name, see Chitose City in Ishikari sub-prefecture. )

( For herring fishing, see Yoichi Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture. )
 
In 1880 ( Meiji 13 ), the Horonai Line opened.  It was the third 
railway in the country.  ( The first one opened between Yokohama and 
Shinbashi and the second one opened between Koube and Oosaka. )  The 
Port of Otaru prospered as a coal shipping center.  It also served as 
an outer port for Sapporo.  #2  #3

In 1899 ( Meiji 32 ), Otaru Ward ( Otaru-ku ) was established.  In 1902 
( Meiji 35 ), Asari Village, Shioya Village, and Takashima Village 
established.  Those were 2nd-class municipalities.  In 1921 ( Taishou 
10 ), Takashima Vilage and Shioya Village became 1st-class 
municipalities.

( UTF code ; Asari = \u671D\u91CC.  Shioya = \u5869\u8C37.  Takashima = 
  \u9AD8\u5CF6. ) 

( For 1st-class and 2nd-class municipalities, see the note of Matsumae 
  Town. )

( For the former Ward in Hokkaido, see Kushiro Town in Kushiro general 
  sub-prefecture. ) 

( For the name ``Asari,'' see the note of Akaigawa Village in 
  Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )

( For the name ``Shioya,'' see the Otaru West section of Otaru City, 
  Shiribeshi general sub-prefecture. )
 
In 1922 ( Taishou 11 ), Otaru City was established.  Otaru was the 
first city ( ``shi'' ) in Hokkaidou, along with Sapporo, Hakodate, 
Asahikawa, Kushiro, and Muroran.  In the same year, Takashima Town was 
established.  

In 1940 ( Shouwa 15 ), Otaru City merged Takashima Town and Asari 
Village.  In 1958 ( Shouwa 33 ), Otaru City merged Shiioya Village.  #4 

CITY HALL, TRAFFIC, POPULATION 

The city hall of Otaru is located along Okobachi River, which flows 
through central Otaru and empties in the Otaru Canal.  National Route 5 
and the Hakodate Main Line run along Ishikari Bay and the Sea of Japan.
This road and railroad connect built-up areas located along the mouths 
of rivers. 
 
The Port of Otaru has been designated as one of the Important Ports 
( Juuyou-kouwan ).  It developed as a shipping port for coal, an outer 
port for Sapporo, and an export port for agricultural products.  #5 
 
Since the 1960s, the population of Otaru has declined.  In the 1960s, a 
large port opened along the Pacific coast in Tomakomai City, Iburi 
general sub-prefecture.  It included a heavy industrial zone.  ( The 
Port of Tomakomai has been designated as the Important Port and the 
International Strategic Port. )  #6  #7 

The country's energy source shifted from coal to oil.  The freight yard 
of Otaru-chikkou Station, which had been used for coal, turned into a 
shopping center in the 1990s.  

( Otaru-chikkou Station is adjacent to the east side of the Port of 
  Otaru.  Coal was shipped using the station between 1936 and 1970.  
  ( by ``Port Of Otaru ( Otaru-no-kouwan )'' http://
  3city.net/archives/database/otaru/4911/  last accessed in August 
  2026. )  #8

  The former Temia Station was adjacent to the west side of the Port of 
  Otaru.  Coal was shipped using the station between 1880 and 1980.  
  ( by ``Temiya Elavated Pier ( Temiya-kouka-sanbashi )'' http://
  web.archive.org/web/20110711041443/
  http://www.itogumi.co.jp/magazine/index.html  last accessed in August 
  2026. )  

INDUSTRY  
 
Distinctive industries in Otaru are sightseeing, transportation, trade, 
manufacturing, and fishing.  STATISTICS OF AGRICULTURE = tomatoes 10 
hectares in 2015, orchards 8 ( grapes 2, cherries 1, plums 1, apples X 
( concealed ), other fruits 2 ), paddy rice 8, potatoes 8, radishes 5, 
pasture 3, strawberries 3, adzuki beans 2, lettuces 2, carrots 1, 

( continued ) --> cucumbers 1, green onions 1, and other vegetables 
20 ; dairy cows X ( concealed ) head in 2015.  STATISTICS OF FISHERIES 
= right-eye flounder 3,068 tons in 2017, walleye pollock 1,685, Pacific 
cod 637, crabs ( red snow crabs 613, snow crabs 11, other crabs 3 ), 
atka mackerel 624, squid ( flying squid 599, other squid 1 ), 
 
( continued ) --> octopus 423, herring 241, salmon 169, sea urchins 91, 
seaweed ( ``konbu'' kelp 57, other seaweed 5 ), sand lance 42, 
shellfish ( abalone 2, other shellfish 31 ), ``hatahata'' 19, sharks 3, 
sea trout 2, mackerel 1, other fish 411, and other marine animals 146. 
 
Otaru and Sapporo were the first industrial cities in Hokkaidou.  
Industries in Otaru were run by private companies.  Industries in 
Sapporo were run by the government.  

In Otaru, miscellaneous manufacturing industries thrived, such as fish 
processing, rice milling, wood, metal, rubber, and glass products. 
Transportation and storage industries thrieved too.  #9  #10
 
Today, Otaru is a tourist city.  Being close to Sapporo City, most 
visitors are day trippers. ( They stay in Sapporo. )  #11  #12  

Still, Otaru is an industrial city.  About 100 factories are in 
operation in Zenibako Industrial Park.  #13  #14 

NOTE

#1

During the Edo era, the Matsumae Domain established three trading 
places ( ``basho'' ) in the area that is present-day Otaru City.  They 
were Otarunai, Takashima, and Oshoro.  

Takashima-basho was located at Cape Takashima.  Today, Otaru Aquarium 
is located at Cape Takashima. 

Oshoro-basho was located in the west of Otaru City.  There is a small 
peninsula that includes Oshoro Bay.  Ranshima Station on the Hakodate 
Main Line is located at the root of the peninsula.  ( Map --> http://
maps.gsi.go.jp/#15/43.207866/140.858653 )
 
( For Otarunai-basho, see the HISTORY section above. ) 

Oshoro district in Otaru City has a long history of human habitation.  
Oshoro Stone Circles ( Oshoro-kanjou-resseki ), which are ruins 
date back 3,500 years ago, are distributed from Oshoro district to 
Yoichi Town.  Those were built in the Zoku-joumon era. 
 
( For the Zoku-joumon era, see Yoichi Town in Shiribeshi general sub-
  prefecture ; Fukagawa City in Sorachi general sub-prefecture. )

Temiya Cave is located next to the Railway Museum ( now Otaru Museum ).
Ancient engravings are preserved in the cave.  The motifs are animals 
and humans.  Those were engraved in the 6th century.  The period is the 
Zoku-joumon era ( post Joumon era ). 

I visited Temiya Cave with my children.  They expected the cave was 
like a dungeon of Minecraft, but it was shallow.  The engravings 
resemble the ones in Fugoppe Cave in Yoichi Town.  
 
#2  
 
In 1880 ( Meiji 13 ), four administrative offices ( ``kochou-yakuba'' ) 
were established in Otaru county ( Otaru-gun ).  They covered towns 
( ``chou'' or ``machi'' ) in built-up areas and villages ( ``mura'' ) 
in the suburbs.  They were as follows ;

( 1 ) One administrative office for seven towns ;  Arihoro-chou, 
Irifune-chou, Minato-machi, Nagai-chou, Ryoutoku-chou, Sakai-mach, and 
Yamanoue-chou.

( UTF code ; Arihoro-chou = \u6709\u5E4C\u753A.  Irifune-chou = 
  \u5165\u8239\u753A.  Minato-machi = \u6E2F\u753A.  Nagai-chou = 
  \u6C38\u4E95\u753A.  Ryoutoku-chou = \u91CF\u5FB3\u753A.  Sakai-mach 
  = \u583A\u753A.  Yamanoue-chou = \u5C71\u4E0A\u753A. )

( For ``chou'' and ``machi,'' see Mori Town in Oshima general sub-
  prefecture. )

( 2 ) One administrative office for eight towns ; Doba-chou,
 Katsunai-chou, Kawara-chou, Masakae-chou, Shintomi-chou,
 Shiomidai-chou, Takasago-chou, and Wakatake-chou.

( UTF code ; Doba-chou = \u571F\u5834\u753A.  Katsunai-chou = 
  \u52DD\u7D0D\u753A.  Kawara-chou = \u5DDD\u539F\u753A.  Masakae-chou 
  = \u771F\u6804\u753A.  Shintomi-chou = \u65B0\u5BCC\u753A.
